And the moment the docket exists, condition two is engaged, and condition two says a Schedule 12 object is not to be removed except on a docket so countersigned — and it is not a Schedule 12 object, and the moment we make it one, in order to get it out of the building, we have built ourselves a rule that says he cannot have it back."** **It is the first time in this case that the best control in this building stopped a good person doing the right thing, and it is the first time the instrument that caught everything in Volume 01 is found to have no subject at all.** Mara Iles says it to a man on the stairs: *I want it noted somewhere that the reason a door mat cannot leave a public museum in this city is that there is no form for a person to write a person's name on when they are giving you a thing they own.* The last four words of the notebook entry for the twenty-second of December are: **A description is the only thing left.**

## Chapter 0055 — The Carrying

**Monday the 21st to Thursday the 24th of December 2024, Christmas Eve, nine in the morning to about eight at night.** It opens on the finding: **it failed on Wednesday the twenty-third, in a conservation office, and nobody in the building had been able to say why for twenty-one hours.**

**The first two days were the Museum looking for a form**, and Mara Iles describes the going-through of it in a corridor with the door shut and it is the argument of the entire case: **there is no instrument in this building for a member of the public taking a thing of their own out of a public room, and there never has been, and the reason there has never been one is that in a hundred and forty years nobody has ever wanted to do it, because a person does not walk into a museum with a door mat.** Four things that nearly work — a gift form (a transfer of ownership he is not making, and there is no disposal route for a door mat), a bequest form (a longer version of the same answer), a loan form (the good one, and it wants a borrowing institution and a collection manager's box and a schedule reference, and there is no borrowing institution, there is a man in a cardigan, and the thing would have lived in this building for ever), and **the goods-out docket.** And the foot of the goods-out docket, which is the chapter:

> *This docket records the movement of material from the loading bay of this Museum. It is not evidence of title. Material not owned by this Museum may be moved out only on the written authority of the Director, which must be attached.* **A material count is made against this docket. The docket remains open on the desk of the post holding it until the item is on its pallet and the pallet is sealed.**

**And then, on Thursday morning at about eleven, with a door mat on a corridor floor and ninety minutes of argument spent: *There's a book. There's a book at the front desk with a ribbon in it and four columns and it has been the same book since I came here, and about a hundred and forty people a day put their name in it, and it is the only document in this building that has ever recorded a person going into a public room, and it is not a form, and that is why nobody has thought of it, because it is not a form and I have never had to think about it.* And the sentence that is the whole of the fourth inches: *Eleven rows. It has got a column for the time in and it has got a column for the time out, and the time-out column is the one nobody fills in, and the reason nobody fills it in is that a public museum does not care when a person leaves, which is true, and the reason it matters today is that this man came in at ten past ten and he is going out at about four and we have a mat on a pallet-shaped hole in a form and the only thing anybody in this city has ever used to move a person and their belongings from A to B is a signature and a time.** Mara Iles stands in a corridor for about four seconds and says nothing, and Elias Venn works out in those four seconds that something has just happened to a person he has known for nine weeks and he will not find out what it was until March.

**And the Director's sentence, from a corridor on 24 November, said again on the Monday in a different tone, and the reason it is on the page is the second half of it.** *I cannot sign for it. I cannot indemnify it. And I cannot undertake anything about it. And on the twenty-fourth of November I said that to a registrar in a corridor and it was the answer to a question about a crate… But I have had two days with it and I have worked out why I said it, and it is not because I am frightened of a door mat. **It is because I have nothing to be responsible for.** I cannot be responsible for a thing that has no number, in a building whose every control is a number, because a control is a promise that the thing is where somebody thinks it is… and if I sign a piece of paper saying that I have it, then somebody in four years is going to come and ask me where it is, and I am going to have to say I signed a piece of paper about it in December 2024 and it did not have a number on it, and that is a sentence about me and not about a mat, and I have spent nineteen years not having a sentence about me in this building.* So: no.

**And then they ask the wrong person first, and that is on the page because it is why the right person says yes.** Elias Venn finds Perry Loach in a service corridor at five past five on Christmas Eve and asks him, and the man says no, and the reason is the same shape as November and better: **"You asked me. That's the whole of it and you didn't even notice you'd done it. I've got a grid with my hours on it and no name on it, and it closes on Friday, and if a thing of mine is on a piece of paper in a corridor in this building on Friday because a man without a job asked me, then on Friday somebody is going to look at that piece of paper and find my name on it attached to a door mat… You don't want a thing of mine to end up on a piece of paper because you asked. **You want the office to ask.**"** And then, after *I can't ask the office, I'm barred from the building*: **"Then you're stood up. You want somebody to do something, you get a letterhead to do it with."** Elias Venn goes up two flights and says, *The Director will see you for four minutes about a door mat*, and the Director says yes in about two words and then says the one thing that is the reason it worked: **"Tell him the office asked."**

**What happens at about twenty past five, in full, as a record:**

> **24 DEC 2024 — REMOVAL FROM BUILDING, NON-COLLECTIONS MATERIAL. Entry in the visitors' book, ordinary column, and the counter's own line beneath it. No docket raised: no pallet, no material of this Museum, no authority required, the removal being by the person who presented the item and to the address from which he presented it.** Time in 10:12. Time out 17:24. **Carried by: P. LOACH, services contract. No grade exists. GRADE FIELD LEFT EMPTY AT THE COUNTER'S INSISTENCE.** Received by **FRANK DUNNING, 88 Sillick Lane, in his own name, in his own hand**, signature witnessed by M. PRIEST, who wrote *not a Museum object* in the reason column and initialled it. And the last line of that page: **17:24 — OUT. DUNNING, F. — own property, not ours. A. WARD, Dir., asked.**

**Four words in a column, on the last line of a page, on Christmas Eve, and it is the only document in the business in which a Director of this Museum has recorded that she was asked to do a thing rather than that she did it.** Elias Venn is not in the building at twenty past five and was not in the room and the correct behaviour of a man with a WITNESS card is to leave when the witness business is finished, and he did, and **he found out on the twenty-seventh, from a systems technician, in a corridor, in about four seconds, and neither of them made anything of it, and it is the only time in eleven weeks that a thing has come to him and been better than he expected.**

**And Margaret Priest gives the man his own card back across the counter at about ten past five**, out of the bundle of about four hundred, without asking anybody, and says why: *Because you came up this counter on Monday and asked me for it, and because I have been putting things in boxes on this counter for nineteen years and about four of them in nineteen years have ever come back out again, and I would like there to be one.* He puts it in the same plastic wallet as the bank statements. **It is the only piece of paper in the world that has Frank Dunning's name and his address on it and has not come back through a machine with a dash in a box on it, and it exists because this Museum prints four hundred and twelve of these a year and keeps them, which is not policy and is not good practice and is the luck of the whole thing.** And the chapter closes on the sentence that the volume turns on: **a description is the only thing you get when there is no number, and a name is the only thing you get when there is no number, and the reason there is no form for this is that the thing is not a Museum object, and the reason there is no Museum object is that nobody in this country will accept a gift of a door mat from a man who cannot prove that the mat is his.**

## Chapter 0056 — A Name Field

**Monday the twenty-first of December 2024, 10:10 in the post room to 21:15 at a table in a rented room. And this chapter is dated EARLIER than Chapters 54 and 55, and that is deliberate.** The Museum's own records were already losing the row a day before a mat came through the front door, and nobody in the building connected the two, and the finding is that the connection is that nobody in the building was looking.

**The post room.** A manila envelope marked DEAD-LETTERS with a number on it in biro, and the number is three hundred and fifty-three, and the number is from the eighth of December, and **it was written on the last morning of his employment by a man who did not know it was the last morning of anything, and no one has written on the envelope since, because the envelope is a job and the job stopped on the ninth and nobody replaced it, and the envelope has not been emptied.** It is now on a second envelope and the second envelope is not marked. The box behind the boiler has the other lot and has been in that corridor since the spring because it came off a load of shelving in November, and Margaret Priest says it will be there in March. **And Ivo Pell, who did two hundred and six dead letters in October and has never told anybody, offers to do them: *Not now. It is a systems day, the building is open, and I have not got anything else to do between now and the fourth of January, and I would rather do four hundred and some envelopes than sit in a systems office with a mug.***

**The systems office, about eleven, in company, on a notice under item six.** The block is called **THRESHOLD GROUP — ADDRESSES — HALL OF FORMER FAITHS (notification)** and it is the only block in about nine hundred lines that is not a record: **source: district address and occupancy file, daily feed, 04:1x; filter: notification zone; rows: 412; (block not versioned; superseded values not retained).** And: **"That block has no history. It is not a record, it is a wire. It gets overwritten every morning at about a quarter past four and whatever was in it yesterday is not in this building anywhere, on any disk, in any backup, or in anybody's head, and it is the only block in the index of nine hundred lines that is like that, and the other eight hundred and ninety are things this building owns."**

**The three systems, named for the first time and this is the batch's architecture.** A delivery sheet. A council feed. A museum. And they are all reading the same thing and the only one anybody ever knocks on a door about is the first. And the feed is **the same file the electoral roll is built off and the council tax base is built off**, and the Museum's *threshold group* is a *filter* on it, and it is a wire, and it is the reason nobody has lost anybody: **we're the only one of the three that gets a printed copy, and the only one of the three that has ever done anything as a result of a difference, and we've never compared the print to the feed, because comparing a print to a feed is a job and comparing a print to a print is a job and there's only one print in this building and it's in a box.**

**The comparison, in about two hours, with a print run and a screen and a pencil, and the print run is the only reason the finding is a finding.** Three runs: the **19 November** notification, 412 cards, no blanks, posted; the **2 December** closure-and-reopening notice, 412 cards, no blanks, hand-delivered to letter boxes on the 2nd and 3rd; and the **annual acknowledgement of the twelfth of December**, 412 cards, **nine blanks**, posted, **all nine returned undeliverable on the 15th and 16th.** The feed block as at 21 December: 412 rows, nine blanks. **Nine rows, all nine in one run of the sheet, and the run was one street. No row added. No row removed. No address altered.** And the sentence that is the whole of the chapter: **the Museum printed nine blanks on the twelfth of December, put them in nine envelopes and posted them, and has not read a print run in its own print room, because a print run is a print run, and nobody has ever in the history of this building compared a run to the thing it was printed off.** Transcribed by hand, no screen photographed, the Registrar's initials on it, and **condition of 1 December 2024 not engaged** — which is Ivo Pell's condition from the first of December, and he says so before anybody asks.

**And then the dead letters, counted in forty minutes by a systems technician, which is the only counting anybody in this business has done in eight weeks that anybody else has ever heard about.** Four hundred and eleven in the envelope and about ninety in the box, of which **ninety-one are the Museum's own, and nine of the ninety-one are the annual acknowledgement of the twelfth of December and all nine came back marked undeliverable in the second week**, and thirty-one of the four hundred and eleven came back in the third week of December and nine of the thirty-one are the nine. He writes the number on a new envelope in his own hand with the date and **his own name**, and it is the first time anybody has written a name on that envelope since the eighth of December. *That is the first time anybody has written a name on that envelope since the eighth of December, and it is because you are in the room, and it is because you were in the room on the second of December too… and the whole of that arrangement is a load-bearing wall, and I have never once been thanked for any part of it and I am not going to be.* And then the honest version: *Ninety-one. I counted it twice. Three of the ones I put in the museum's pile are not ours, they're a charity's, and the charity's is in the Sillick Lane run, and I've left them in the general pile because I am not a registrar and it isn't my job to decide whose post a thing is.*

**The five books, and the finding about the five books is the reason nothing happens on the Monday.** The **visitors' book** — every member of the public, a name, a time in, a time out — **holds no address and has never held one, because a public museum's book is not an address system and adding one would be unlawful.** The **device day book** — a person, a device, a time out, a time back, once a line with a name and no time out — records the issue of a thing, not a person. The **key board** — a card number and a time and, since the twenty-ninth of November, one line with a number and a time and no name — and **it is the only one of the five that has never pretended to be a personnel record, and Mrs Farrier, who has done it for fourteen years, has said so in a corridor and been right.** The **personnel file** — about nine hundred names — **cannot answer it, and the file that could have, the card index of posting sheets, two hundred and eleven lines, every one of them *c/o Museum letting, Sillick Lane, Low Ward*, is a card index in a drawer on an archive corridor, and it is the only document in this building that ever connected a name to a street, and it does not include one person in this case who is not an employee.** And the **systems index** — **it cannot answer *who is at this address* in any form, and it has never been asked to, and it is not capable of it, and no system in this building is capable of it, and that is not a fault in the design, that is what the thing is.**

**And the sentence, in a conservation office with the door shut, to a systems technician and to a man who was in the room on notice and not from any of it: "This building has two hundred and forty-seven objects with a location in it and about nine hundred names in a personnel binder and four hundred and twelve addresses on a feed, and it has never once, in a hundred and forty years, had a record of a person and a place together, and that is not because we are careless. It is because a museum is a place where things are, and we built it that way on purpose, and it is correct, and the entire business of the last two months has been me trying to get a person and a place into the same sentence using instruments that were not built for it."** And then, and it is the batch's turn being set up: **"I can put a name on a card. That is the one thing this institution can do that nothing else in this city can do… because a card is a name in a public room and a name in a public room is a hook and a hook is the only thing that has ever caught anything in this business, and the reason it catches is that it is small and it is printed and it goes in an envelope, and about four hundred and twelve households on that list have had one, and nine of them have had one come back."**

**And the schedule of collections, one page, dated the ninth of December, INCOMPLETE and expected to remain so, and the pencil at the foot of it in the registrar's hand on the twenty-first, which is four words and the reason the last ten days of the year turn over: the first entry in the schedule of collections is an object in a cupboard, and the second entry would have to be a person at a house, and there is no box for that, and the Registrar will not invent one.** And the notebook entry, six lines, and the last of them: *there is no job in this city in which anybody's duty is to compare Thursday with Friday. And I am a man who takes things off lists.*

## Chapter 0057 — The School

**Tuesday the twenty-second, Wednesday the twenty-third, Thursday the twenty-fourth and Tuesday the twenty-ninth of December 2024, and a corridor, a gate, an office, a fire door, and the standing bar of 18 November.** St Anselm's, Hall Lane, two-form-entry, about three hundred and eighty children and about two hundred adults, built about 1871 for the children of the mill, and its roll has been kept on a corridor wall in a hardback folder since about 1948, **and on the twenty-ninth of December nobody in that building knew that it was the best name-to-place record within four miles.**

**The first thing anybody in that building did about a shrine in a classroom was the correct thing, on the correct day, with the correct document, and it took about nine minutes.** A caretaker found it on Tuesday the twenty-second at about four in the afternoon **because it was in the way**: a table against the wall by an external door, a cloth, a candle in a jar with a wick nobody could remember being lit, a card standing against a jar of coins, a photograph of a woman who was not alive, and against the leg of the table, standing on end, a coir door mat. **It's blocking the push bar. You can't get the door open with your hip and there's a bin against the other side of it.** He moved the table eleven inches, and he did not open the mat, and he rolled it out onto the corridor floor with his foot, **because it was in the way and because he is a caretaker**, and he put it against the wall opposite a radiator. **And that is how a household object reaches three hundred and eighty children. Not by a god. By a push bar, and a word on a form, and a caretaker with a foot, and an object put against the wall opposite a radiator because a corridor is warm.** **And it happened on the same afternoon that a man of about sixty-five carried one up the hill to a museum in a plastic bag, four miles and four miles from the other, and nobody knew.**

**Marion Kelsh, 54, school business manager, nineteen years in post, dealt with it in about nine minutes on the Tuesday, and there is no mysticism in her at all.** Four things, all on a piece of paper. She read her own **fire risk assessment**, eleven pages, updated every August, which has a line on it that says *no obstruction at any exit or entrance, including temporary items*, **and the word *including* is the whole of the chapter.** She went down the corridor with a clipboard. She photographed the mat on the floor with a scale rule in the frame, on her own telephone, because she has done that for a school insurance file since 2011, and she is not Museum staff and is not subject to a condition dated the first of December and does not know that one exists anywhere in this city. And she filled in a form that is not the Museum's, not a Registry form, and not a form about gods, objects, classifications or rope lines:

> **ST ANSELM'S (HALL LANE). FIRE RISK ASSESSMENT — ANNUAL REVIEW, RECORD OF ACTION.** *Item found: obstruction at exit, external door, ground floor, corridor side. Object: personal item brought into a classroom by a pupil. Removed from the entrance and stored.* **Date found 22.12.24. Corrected 23.12.24, 08:40. Action by: M. Kelsh, school business manager.** **Storage: school office, locked filing cabinet, shelf B, item logged on the premises inventory at line 214.** *(Form untouched. The form is a school form and has no amendment box in it, and nobody had to find one.)*

*"Stored," she said, when a supply teacher asked her at nine o'clock why it was in her filing cabinet, **"because the assessment says it can't be in a corridor, and because it belongs to a child, and because a thing that belongs to a child is not a thing this school has any business throwing out. So it's on the inventory. Line 214. It's got a line number. It's got a number, which is the school number, not an accession number, and it is a number and it is on a piece of paper and the piece of paper is in a folder in a drawer in a building that has never heard of a museum."***

**The Museum found out on Thursday the twenty-fourth at about half past ten, when Marion Kelsh telephoned the front desk and Margaret Priest took the message and put it on a board, which is what the board is for, and the Museum shut at six that evening for four days and the message sat on the board over the weekend, which is the correct behaviour of a board and is the reason nothing happened until the twenty-ninth.** She was not on the notification list. She was a person who had a mat in her corridor. *I'm not going to pretend I know what your museum does… On Friday a caretaker moved a table out of the way of a fire door and rolled a mat into our corridor, and on Saturday about forty children went round it, and on Sunday the head said we were having a discussion about it in assembly and I said no, because the day we put that in an assembly is the day thirty-eight children in one room have got a shrine in it, and there is one person in this school responsible for that and it is me and it is not a decoration.*

**The standing bar of 18 November, read in full, in a corridor at St Anselm's at about ten past eleven on the Tuesday, by Mara Iles, to a woman of fifty-four and a man of thirty-two and nobody else — the first time in six weeks that anybody had read it to somebody who had not already read it.**

> **That the Hall of Former Faiths remains open, and the school term continues, and no party not holding a collections pass is to be admitted to that room while the object is under Annexe E; and that no school group is to be admitted to that room at all; and that the hall certificate stands.** **Noted: the term booking of St Anselm's (Hall Lane) for 11.00 on 7 November 2024, ref. SK/BK/0912, was struck through on 18 November and initialled, and no person has since asked by whom.**

*"It is four lines and it is the best piece of drafting any of us has done this year, and it is a resolution about **that room**, and it has been read eleven times in six weeks by six people in this building and every one of them read it as a rule about schools. It is not a rule about schools. It is a rule about a room.*" **"Then it doesn't cover us."** **"It does not cover you, and it has never covered you, and it has never been read to a school."** And then the sentence that is the whole of the four inches, said to two people she did not know: **"I have been in this business nine years and I have signed about four hundred conditions and I have never once in my life read one out to somebody outside the building, and I have just read one out in a corridor in Hall Lane, and the reason I have done it is that you have got a mat in a corridor and I have got a form that I cannot use, and the two of us are the only people in this business who have ever had to write down the same sentence."**

**And condition two fails, again, and it failed first in this building six days earlier over a different mat, and nobody in the corridor knew, and a condition does not get better by being asked twice.** A classroom mat is not a Schedule 12 carried object. The moment anybody proposes that it be one, conditions 1 and 2 apply, and condition 2 says it cannot be removed from the building except on a docket countersigned by a person who is not an officer or employee of this Museum, **and the only person who is not an officer or employee and who is connected to that object is a girl of nine in Year 5 whose name is written on the underside of the corner of it in ballpoint, and the Museum cannot establish that the name belongs to a person at the address written beside it, and the school will not release a pupil's property to a person the school cannot identify.** The condition was written on the fifteenth of October by a Director of fifty-three to stop a Museum employee being the only signature on a document about a thing in a public room. **It is the best condition anybody in this building has ever written. It is in force. It has not been displaced.** And nobody argued, because the condition does not have a subject. It has a box. And the Director, into the silence, about herself, for the fourth time in two months: **"Then I have written a condition that can hold a door mat in a building for ever and I could not have told you that in October and I have been a Director for nineteen years and I did not know, and I am not going to pretend I designed it and I am not going to pretend I did not."**

**And Elias Venn asks one question in the corridor and waits for the answer, and it is about the inventory line, and it takes eleven seconds, and it is the only thing he did in that corridor all day.** He had come to a school on a Tuesday morning alone, uninvited, unannounced, with no reason anybody could audit, and the man at the gate asked him which child he had come for and he said *none* and the man said *there's nobody to see* and he said *I know, I'll wait by the office* and the man let him stand by the office for an hour, **and that is the whole of his standing in this case and it is better than any door he has ever had.** He asks whether line 214 is a number, and she says yes, and then she tells him the rest, unasked, and it is the best argument anybody in this business has made against him in ten weeks:

> "**Your museum has a card with a name on it, and my school has a book with a name on it, and the two of us are the only two places in this county that could put a name on that mat. And the school isn't going to, because it's a pupil's mat and the pupil is nine and I'm not putting a nine-year-old's name on a thing in a museum in January.**"

**And then the other half, which is the chapter's floor: "My book has got a name and an address and a classroom in it for every child in this school, and I could write you that name in about four seconds, and I could put it on your card, and I am not going to, and it is not because of data protection and it is not because of a rule. It is because that child has got a mat by the door because it is the warm place by the radiator, and she stands on it every morning, and about thirty-eight children in that room do, and if I put her name on a card in a museum then she becomes a thing that is in a museum, and she is nine, and I would like her to be a person who stands on a mat."**

**And he stands in a corridor and works out the next four days in about nine seconds while a caretaker's bin goes past, and he is right about all of it.** Two institutions within four miles could put a name on that mat. A school, which will not, because she is nine. And a museum, which has a hundred and forty years of putting names on cards and has, in that whole time, **exactly one recorded instance of a card in a public room going out with nothing in the field where the name goes, and that instance is the object at plinth eleven, and the label room will not do it twice.** And the entry for the twenty-ninth, written in a school in the dark at about ten to seven the next morning: **condition 2 failed on the twenty-third, in this building, over the mat at 88, and it has failed again this week over a mat in a school, and the reason both times is that the only person who is not an officer or employee of this Museum and who has anything at all to do with a mat of this kind is a child of nine.** Three hundred and eighty children. Four hundred and twelve addresses. About fourteen doors. One pound. And a form in an office with a clause in it about querying a line, which he has not asked anybody about, because he has not been in a room since the twenty-second of December and the last time he was in one he was a witness.

## Chapter 0058 — The Card

**Wednesday the thirtieth of December 2024, 10:00 to about eight at night, and the batch's turn, and it is a card, and the card is not the mat's.** The Hall of Former Faiths closed at three o'clock on Thursday the thirty-first and reopens at ten on **Wednesday the thirteenth of January 2025**, under condition 3, ten working days, and the notice says *The Museum apologises to the Hall's threshold group of about four hundred addresses*, and about ninety people will come to that door on Friday the eighth of January with a pound each and find it shut and a card in a plastic sleeve. **A door mat from a classroom in Hall Lane closed the most-visited room in a public museum for ten working days in the middle of the holiday, and there is no monster in it and there never is going to be.** And it was a Wednesday morning, the sixth working day of the year, and everything that has ever happened to Elias Venn in this business has happened on a Tuesday, and he noticed that on the Wednesday in a corridor at about six in the evening and did not write it down.

**The Director signs the second document she has signed since the first of December, and says the cost out loud before she signs, and has it minuted in that order.** *Before I sign this, I am going to say the cost out loud, in a room, and the minute will record that I said it before I signed and not after, and if the minute does not record that then I have not said it.* And then: **condition three is her own, and it is the same sentence as clause three of Standing Instruction 14, and she wrote the clause and she wrote the condition, and both of them were excellent in October and she has been waiting all term for something to test them and did not expect it to be a mat and is not going to pretend she has not been waiting.** And then the cost, in her own words: the notice goes up that afternoon and the room shuts tomorrow the thirty-first, and the tenth working day of the year is the thirteenth of January, because the first of January is a public holiday and a condition counts working days and not calendar days and she has had somebody count them for her twice; **the Hall of Former Faiths is the reason this building has a roof; about four hundred and twelve addresses were told on the second of December that the room was open; there are going to be about ninety people at that door on the Friday with a pound each; and she has said the cost first, in a room, in December, on purpose, because she is not going to be a woman who says it in January.** And: **a door mat has more classification in front of it than a bell has, and that is because the bell was here first and because the mat is in a school with three hundred and eighty children in it, and I would take a mat over a bell every day of my working life and I have.** She also records the day of the week on the minute, because a condition that counts working days has to hang off a date somebody can check, **and because she is a Director who has been caught out by a weekday exactly once in nineteen years and does not intend to be caught twice.**

**And the Registry answers, in the form it gets all its answers, from a woman of about forty-five that nobody in the building had ever spoken to, and it is the shortest and the most correct document of the year.** Ref. QA/CR/1426, received 30 December: *This schedule has no classification for activity observed outside a museum context. Your matter cannot be listed against a classification. It is not a Schedule 12 proposal and it is not a Schedule 9 proposal. It is referred to your own schedule of collections and to the inquiry that is sitting. No inspection slot is available before 6 February 2025. There is no emergency provision. I am not sorry. If you are about to display it, do not display it on the strength of a letter from this office.* — **S. TULLY, assessor, quiet accord registry.** *Name on the rota of 23 December 2024. First appearance on any rota in this file. Not previously spoken to by any member of staff of this Museum.* **Five months of rota bulletins in a systems technician's folder and a name on it, and the first time it was said in a room it was in a sentence that refused the Museum and told it not to display the object.** And nobody could classify the mat, and the classification route being shut, the Director's own route was the only one left, and her own route is four conditions she wrote herself, and condition three is the only instrument in the building that will move a thing out of a public room. **So a door mat closed a gallery.**

**Then, at about half past one in the afternoon, the label room.** Fenn, surname only, has made about four hundred cards and has never once been asked whether the name on one of them is right. *Eleven blanks' worth of card, then. That's the job… about a hundred and forty years ago somebody decided that a space on the card the size of a name is a hole in a museum, and you don't have holes in a museum.* **"Who decided that?"** *Everybody. In order, over a hundred and forty years, and none of them wrote it down.* And then: *I've been asked to cut a card for a door mat and I've got no maker and no date and no place and no collection line, and the form wants a heading, and a heading is a name. So either I put a name in it or I leave a hole. And I'm not putting a name in it. And I can't leave a hole, because everybody before me made sure you can't leave a hole. And the way they made sure of it is the thing I want somebody to look at before I do it.* **"Where."** *Eleven feet that way. It's the one that's been under a panel for a month. Get somebody to lift the tape.*

**Nobody lifted the tape. The card came off instead, and the panel stayed where it is, and it has been where it is since the twenty-seventh of November.** It is a sheet of A4, printed, designed by a woman of fifty-four at a kitchen table in Low Ward, taped to the front of a case at a slight angle over a plinth card, and the last line on it says *the pay office kept its own book. That book is on the table*, **and it is still taped on, and the tape has gone yellow, and nobody has taken it down because it is the requester's panel and the requester has not come for it, and a requester's panel is not a Museum's to remove.** The card came off with a card tool in a room with the Director, the Registrar, a Board Secretary and a trustee of sixty-four. **Nobody in that room read the top half, because every person in it had read the top half in November.** The top half is the November description — *unpitched at rest, no inscription, no figure, no devotional content, larger than life-size, used for sounding, and worn hollow inside the lip where it was held, cast 1902, staple repair, fracture remains visible, Quiet Accord classification Annexe D permanent, to be displayed, under no circumstances to be removed from public view by a museum acting alone.* The bottom half is the finding, and it is four lines that have been on the foot of a card in a public gallery for about a hundred and twenty years, below the description, where nobody's eye goes:

> *Recovered on the closure of a textile works, Low Ward.* ***Acq. 4471 / B.*** *Municipal Works, unremarkable industrial object, retained for completeness of the Low Ward series.*

**Three false claims and a name in the position where a name goes, on a public card, for a hundred and twenty years.** *There is no textile works in Low Ward that closed in 1902, there is no textile works in Low Ward that was casting in 1902, the Low Ward series was opened in 1961, and four-four-seven-one over B is not a Museum reference in any form.* And the fourth line, read out by a trustee: **"That is a name. It is in the position where a name goes. It is the name of a body that does not exist, and it has been on a public card in this building for about a hundred and twenty years, and it is the only name on that card, and there is a panel over the top of it that a woman of fifty-four printed herself and put up for the workers on the twenty-seventh of November, and the panel is still on the case, and nobody is going to take it down."** And then the sentence the whole mechanism of the case is made of: **"Nobody knows. It was not written by me, and I have said in public, twice, that it is not a Museum number and that the rest of it is three false claims, and I have been waiting eight weeks for somebody to ask me about the fourth line specifically, and nobody has, because it is the fourth line and it is under a panel, and that is the entire mechanism of this case. It is not that the lie is old. It is that the lie is at the bottom."**

**And then the question the whole of the afternoon is for, asked of a label-room finisher in a corridor by a woman who has cut four hundred cards and has never been asked.** *So the field's either got a name in it or it hasn't. And it can't be a name, because there's no name. And it can't be blank, because a blank's a hole. And the reason it can't be blank is not me. The reason it can't be blank is that the last time this building had a space in a public room, somebody filled it in later, and I wasn't here and I don't know when, and I know because the foot of that card has got a body on it that doesn't exist, and that is what happens.* *What if it stays blank.* **"Then it stays blank, and it is the first one in a hundred and forty years, and every person who comes in here for the next hundred and forty years will look at it and see a space where the name should be, and they will not know that anybody decided anything. And you know what a space is, don't you. A space is the most honest thing a card has ever had on it and it is the single most dangerous thing you can put on a public card in a museum, and both of those are true, and I am going to do it, and I am not going to be thanked for it, and I would like it minuted that I said it was dangerous first."**

**The card, cut at about four o'clock. Heading: DOOR MAT.** Four lines of description, ending *Function: a fixed-size surface for a person to stand on at a doorway while a person decides whether to open a door. Presented by a member of the public. Not a gift. Not a bequest. Not a loan. Not accessioned. Location: on view, Hall of Former Faiths, on the floor, inside the rope.* And: ***Maker's line: struck through. [ no name available ]*** — and under the line, in the finisher's own hand, four words. **No accession number. No collection line. No series. Not in the schedule of collections: there is no box for this and the Registrar will not invent one.** **The first public card in about a hundred and forty years that carries a name in the position where a name goes, and the name is the sentence *no name available*, which is not a name, and which is a hook, and which will be in a room for six weeks over the holiday with about four hundred and twelve households who have been told the room is shut.** And the card does not name Frank Dunning, and does not name the child, and does not name the mill, and does not name a body called Municipal Works.

**And the escalation, itemised on the page, in order, and it is the number of people who can be hurt and not the size of anything:** about fourteen doors on the east side of one street, four of them with an empty field · a round of about two hundred and ten addresses · nine addresses off a road in Broad Street in three days · about four hundred and twelve households on a list that is a wire, nine of which had a name in November and nothing on the twenty-first of December · a school of about three hundred and eighty children and about two hundred adults, with a mat in a corridor for a weekend because a push bar could not be pushed with a hip · a registrar's room, a systems office, a delivery office, a records office on Marsh Lane, a bonded-goods clerk six weeks ago, a front desk with a box behind a boiler · **and a bell, on a plinth, in a sealed case, in a room that is shut, in a building four miles from all of it, which has given one note on the twenty-seventh of November and one on the thirteenth of December and none since, and which has lost a gallery and a fortnight of a rope line's public room because of a thing made of coir, and is not asking for anything, and will not be asked about.** No monster. **There is a mat, a bell, a stone with a hollow in it, a jar of coins, a list that nobody versioned, a code with four words at the foot of a log, and about two hundred and thirty people who press about eleven thousand keys a day and cannot be told from one another.**

**And the corridor at half past five, in full, in four minutes, between a man of twenty-five and a man of thirty-two on the last working day of the year.** *They cut the card.* **What did they put in the heading.** *Door mat.* **And the name.** *There's no name. She wrote no name available on it with a line through it, and she said she'd rather do that than put a name on it, and she said it was the most dangerous thing you can put on a card and she said that before she did it… And they took the plinth thirty-four card off the case and read the bottom of it out in front of the Director, and there's a panel still on the case, and the tape's gone yellow, and nobody's taken it off because it's the requester's.* **And the Gallery.** *Shut till the fourteenth of January. Ten working days… about ninety of them came and got shut out, and there's a telephone message from a man who wanted to know why. Somebody in there said **a door mat has more classification in front of it than a bell has**. And I want you to know that I have been in this building four years and I have never once heard anybody say anything like that, and I have thought about it every hour since, and I have not worked out whether it was a joke.* And then, at about ten past five, the question, said to a man of twenty-five in a corridor because he had had the chance in a room with a Director in it at ten past one and had not used it: **"Ivo. What is a name field for."** And the answer that was not the answer and then the answer: **"For finding him. That's what it does. It doesn't know that. It's a field, and you put a key in it, and the system's job is to go and *get* — you put a key in, and the thing that comes back is a person, and the system does not know a person from a postcode, it's a lookup, and it will go and get you a postcode just as fast. That's it. That's what a name field is for. It's not for the name."** And Elias Venn's own sentence, which is the chapter's finding and the volume's question: **the only difference between a man who is a person and a man who is a postcode is a name in a field, and there is no field on this earth that knows which one it is looking at.** And Ivo Pell's reply, which is the last exchange: **"There's a field on this earth that knows. It's about four inches square and it's got about four hundred lines in it and it's got two hundred and forty-seven locations and there's a hundred and forty years of a rope line in the building around it, and it does know. And it has a maker's line with a body on it that doesn't exist, and it has had since about 1902, and nobody read the bottom of it for a hundred and twenty years."** Neither of them says anything else. **Elias Venn goes out of the front door, because the side door needs a card and the front door is the door the public uses, and it cost him nothing, and it is the only door in this city that has ever opened anything for him without anybody deciding to.** And the last six lines of the entry for the thirtieth: *a description is the only thing left… A name is a hook and a hook is the only thing that has ever caught anything in this business… I asked Ivo what a name field is for, and he said for finding him, and then he said it's not for the name, and then he said a museum knows, and has had a false name on the bottom of a card since 1902. That is the question. I am not going to be able to put it in a box this time. There is no box… **I have been the only man in nine rooms for six weeks. I have not been in a room for eleven days. And I am going to be a hundred and forty years old on Friday.***

## Chapter 0059 — The Threshold

**Monday the fourth of January 2025 at ten past three in the afternoon, and Friday the eighth at about six in the evening, and a rule about the light.**

**The mechanism, in his own words, and it is not a mystery and it is not a god:** a household whose name field is empty cannot be delivered to; a household that cannot be delivered to does not get a council's letter, a doctor's letter, a school's letter, an electoral return, a bank statement, a card from a museum, a determination notice, or a registered letter from anybody; **and a household that cannot be delivered to is not a household that anybody goes and sees, because a person goes and sees a household by writing to it, and the writing comes back, and after the writing comes back twice the whole system stops trying, correctly, because that is what a system is for.** And then, over four days from the sixteenth of December, four unrelated competent people each did the four inches, and a woman of eighty-one sat in her own kitchen on New Year's Day and worked out the one thing none of them could do: **somebody has to say it. Not write it. Not key it. Stand on a step and say a name out loud in a street, to a person at a door, in front of witnesses, so that a person is a person on a Tuesday and not a field on a wire.**

**The three rules, arrived at in a quarter of an hour on a doorstep, on a sheet of A4 in a plastic wallet in a hall in Sillick Lane, not minuted anywhere.** **Named** — *we call it standing on the mat. It is not the museum's and it is not a museum's and it has nothing to do with the museum and nobody has authorised it and nobody is going to.* **Timed** — *from three o'clock until you cannot read a number off a door. After that it is off for the day. Nobody goes at night. Nobody goes in the dark. That is a light rule and not a clock rule, and you can keep it in your head, and in January it is about an hour and a half and in June it is nine.* **Abandonable** — *anybody taking part may stop at any point, and stopping is not a failure, and nobody is to be asked afterwards why they stopped or why they did not go, and nobody is to be asked to say they have done it.* And a fourth thing which is not a rule and is the only instrument the practice has: **whoever says a name writes it down and takes it home and puts it on a sheet on a table in the hall.** A name, an address, a date, a time, and the hand it was written in, on a table, under the post, where a person waiting for something can see it.

**There is no form for it, and the reason is not that nobody has got round to it. Five institutions were asked and all five said no and all five were right, and the reasons are the argument.** The **Museum**: *it is not a practice, it is a relationship, and this institution has a category for the first and no category for the second, and the category it has is Schedule 12, carried object, no activity observed in the period of inspection, and putting a person's street into that would be a lie on a public card and we cut one of those in October and it is still lying on a plinth.* The **conciliation service**: *it is not a claim, it is not a dispute, it is not a matter of payments, and you would be the first person in nine years to bring me a third thing, and the third thing would last about a fortnight and then it would be a thing people say they did in a room in January.* The **council's addressing service**: *the occupancy field is maintained from returns and walk surveys and is not a function of this authority.* The **post office**, at Nether Kiln Lane, in a room with a printer in it: *the round is a delivery round and there is no such thing as a non-delivery visit, and if you want your field put back you want a walk survey and a walk survey is a person going to your door, and the form for that is on the board, and the relationship box on it is where you say who you are to the address, and you are the person to the address, so you can fill that in yourself, and there is no charge and it is about four working days.* **And she said that last part twice because she had never said it out loud before and she wanted to be sure he had it.** The **library back room**: twenty names in pencil, and the list is the people who turn up, and it is not kept. **So there is no form for it, and there is a form for putting a name back in a field, and the two are four working days apart, and nobody on that street has ever been told about either.**

**The first person to try it was not Elias Venn and it was not a woman of fifty-four. It was Vera Pilling of number 6, who is eighty-one, and it was on the first Monday of the year, and it cost her her chest.** 80, 84, 88, 90 — four doors with no names in them and four names in a woman's handwriting going back about fifty years. At 84 somebody answered, seventy-one, in that flat since 1988, and she went in for a cup of tea and wrote it on the sheet in her own hall with the date and the time and the fact that she had gone in. At 88 Frank Dunning said *You'll have heard*, and she said: **"Frank Dunning. Number eighty-eight. Monday. I did it on the Monday and I'm telling you on the Friday because a name in a street is worth nothing on the day it's said,"** and he said *You've not said it out loud in about nine years, have you*, and she said **"No. And I'll tell you the thing about that, because you're the first one on this street that's said anything to me since the spring. I have not been called anything in this street for nine years, not by post and not by mouth, because the post stopped and because the mouths in this street are all the same age as me and we've all got the same thing wrong with our ears."** **That is what the practice does. Not a god. A mouth.**

**And then she got to 90, where there is nobody, and said the name anyway to a shut door. And then she got to 80, and that is the one on the page.** The field at 80 was never updated for the man who came into that flat in April, because a tenancy does not notify an addressing service and nobody has ever thought about why not, and the field held the name of the man who had it before him, who has been dead since about 2019 — **so for eight months the field at 80 was correct about a dead man, and the post came, and the postman knocked, and the man who lives there now has had his post for eight months because a man of about fifty knocked on a door and asked a question, and nobody has ever written down that any of that is what happened.** And she said the dead man's name at the living man's door and the man said **"Who?"** And her own correction, on the pavement, unprompted, and it is the most competent sentence in the chapter: **"You can't just say a name. You've to knock first, and then you say the name, and if a body's there you say the name to them, and if there's no body you say it to nobody and you write it down and you go home."** Then she got as far as the wall at the bottom of the lane on the Friday the eighth and had to sit down on it for about four minutes, and went to the clinic on the Monday, and the clinic is on Fisher Lane and the woman who ran it had been coming to the Friday queue at the library for six years and did not know she was going to be in the story. **Not a collapse. A woman of eighty-one stood on a doorstep in the cold wind for about an hour and a quarter and her heart did what a heart of eighty-one does. She's to have it monitored and she's to stop and she's not to stand about in the cold. And she'll do what she's told for about eleven weeks and then she'll be back, because she is eighty-one and she has never in her life been told to stop.** **The cost is the whole of the cost, it is a body, it belongs to the first person who tried it, nobody had asked her, and the practice has abandonability in it and she used it, and the practice carried on without her on the Friday, which is what a practice with a third rule in it is supposed to do.**

**And Elias Venn on a pavement at about ten past six on Friday the eighth, properly dark, the practice off for an hour and a half, one of about nine people, the ninth, nobody had asked him to do anything.** He had been asked on the Thursday morning by telephone, in ninety-one seconds, by Mara Iles, and had said yes, and had then spent two days finding out that he had no idea what yes meant. And then, the moment, and it is the hard lock honoured and not reversed: **"Is anybody writing this down anywhere."** **No.** *And Elias. **If you go, you will need a word.** You have not got one. I have not given you one and I am not going to, and I have thought about that for two days and I have not changed my mind.* **"I know the reason."** **"Then say it back."** **"Because the practice has to be stoppable by somebody who is not the man doing it, and if I have got a word then the word is mine, and a thing that can be stopped by the man who is doing it has not been stopped, it has been interrupted, and you wrote that on a pad on the twenty-seventh of November and copied it off a man of twenty-five and took the credit for it."** **"There. And it is the reason, and you have never once heard me say it about a man who is not in a room, and there it is."** **"That's fine. I've not got one anyway."** **"You have not got one anyway,"** she said, and stopped, and then said the sentence she telephoned to say: **"Elias. Nobody has ever given you a word because there has never been one to give. You did not have one in October and you did not learn one in November and you have not learned one now and nobody is going to, and I am the person who would have to, and I have decided that I will not, and it is not a kindness and it is not a punishment. It is because a word that comes from a registrar in a building four miles away is not a word anybody in Sillick Lane can use at four o'clock in the afternoon when it is getting dark."** And he gives her the answer, and it is the best line he has: **"Well, they have got one. It's not mine and it isn't yours. Somebody on that street wrote it on a piece of A4 in a plastic wallet, and the third rule says anybody taking part can stop, and that means the word is *anybody's*, and it isn't a word, it's a *permission*, and I have not got a permission, I have got a coat and a pound and a notebook with nine pages in it that cost thirty-five pence and I cannot even give the mat back to a man because the room was shut."**

**And he does not go to a door, and the reason is not the bar and is not the pound, and it is the whole of where he is in the world: the practice is a record with a name against it, made by a person, at a time, in front of a witness, and he cannot be the person who makes it, because a record with his name on it — Venn, E. — is a record about a man who is in a disciplinary file four miles away, and a woman of fifty-four has said out loud in a room of eighty people that a name in a Museum file is a thing that happens to a person, and she was right, and he has been on the wrong end of it for six weeks and knows exactly what it costs.** So he does the next thing, on a pavement, in the dark, with no notebook out, because he has not taken a notebook onto a street at night since October and is not going to start.

**And at about half past six, at number 14 Sillick Lane, in a front room with a fire on, he gives Cliff Sowerby a registered letter.** He has had it since the thirtieth of October. It has been in nine coats and four rooms and a bus and a hospital car park and a laundrette, and he has never opened it and never asked, and nobody has ever asked him for it, and **nobody had ever told Cliff Sowerby that it existed** — Cliff was told about a printed card on the twenty-eighth of November by a woman of fifty-four and that was the only notice he had had of anything. *I'm not going to give you this in a corridor. I'm going to knock, and I'm going to stand there, and I'm going to hand it to you, and you're going to open it, and I'm going to watch you read it because I have to know that you read it and you have to know that I knew that you hadn't.* It is four lines from a firm of solicitors at fourteen Lincoln's Lane and it tells him that a schedule of interim payments has been opened in the matter of payments for the final trading week of October 1974, that a line has been opened in his name, that the line is public, and that he can come to the conciliation office on any weekday between ten and four and ask to see it. *"That's it?" — "That's it. There's a counterfoil."* He gives it back and says: **"I have been on a list since the Thursday before I came to that gallery and nobody has said so, and I have got a stamp on my hand in biro from the thirtieth of October, and I have been carrying it round for two months."** *You could have brought it round in November.* **"No. You couldn't have. It wasn't yours and it wasn't a Tuesday."** And he puts it on the sideboard next to the teapot, which is where a document goes in that house, **and he does not put it in a drawer, and Elias Venn notices that he does not put it in a drawer and understands about four years later why.**

**And the last image of the first movement of this book, and it does not work.** Frank Dunning stands on his own step at about twenty to seven, in the dark, on his own, because it is his front door and there was nobody at it, and says his own name out loud into a street at about seventy decibels. **Nothing happens. Nobody hears it. A man of about sixty-five says his own name on his own doorstep in the dark in January and the street hears it and does not remember it and the delivery office does not update and the district feed at four o'clock the next morning has a new row and no new occupancy field, and the field at 88 Sillick Lane is still empty and will be on Tuesday.** **And it is the right thing to have done, and there is no evidence that it is the right thing to have done, and a man of sixty-five did it about four hundred times a year for the next eleven months and told nobody except two people, one of whom wrote it down in a notebook, in a rented room in Low Ward, on the ninth of January, at about half past eleven at night, with no light.**

**And the honest floor, said to a man with a notebook standing on a pavement, nobody having asked him a question: "You'll have noticed we've all got our doors done and the ones we've not got are the ones that aren't on the sheet. **The ones on the sheet, we can get to. The ones off it, we've not got a door.**"** And: *Nine on a road called Broad Street, and I don't know the street, and I don't know anybody on it, and there's a man on a round who's told me about it and he's not the only one.* And the last true thing he says to Elias Venn that winter: **"You can only knock on a door you know is a door. And you know it's a door because it's on a list. And the ones that aren't on the list have had it taken off, and taken off by a machine, and nobody's keeping a copy of the list that was there before."** And then, and it is the last line of the chapter: **"Somebody kept a copy. It is in brown paper bands in a box behind a counter in a building you've been barred out of since the first of December, and it's got four hundred and twelve names on it, and it is the only list in this city with those names on it, and it is in a Museum, and you are the only person I have ever met who has ever read it."**

## Chapter 0060 — The Same Date, in Two Systems

**Monday the eleventh of January 2025, about six in the morning to half past eleven at night, and it took him five days and four attempts, and the reason it took four is that the first three were him being pleased with himself and he noticed.**

**The finding, in one sentence, and it is a fact and not a shape: the same date is written in two systems in two buildings four miles apart, and one of those systems is the source of the other, and nobody in either building has ever been told that the second one exists. Saturday the fifth of December 2024 is in both of them.**

**Attempt one, Monday evening, at a kitchen table, wrong, and the way it is wrong is on the page because it is the mistake every person in this business would make:** *the field went empty on the sixth, therefore the field went empty because of the sixth* — when the delivery office's own note in the woman's hand says the sixth at twenty to four in the morning was a nightly file refresh and not a keying, and the code was set the previous afternoon at twenty-two minutes past four, **and the previous afternoon was the fifth.** He had read that note eleven times and written a sentence about the sixth because the sixth had been in his head since the Sunday before, and the fifth was the day that was in the document.

**Attempt two, Tuesday, wrong in the other direction and worse, because it was clever:** *the field went empty because the Museum's list lost it* — when the Museum's list is a wire, overwritten every morning, with no history, and **a thing that is overwritten cannot be shown to have been different yesterday**, and the Museum is the last place in the chain and not the first, and on the Tuesday evening he had it backwards because the Museum is the only institution in this case he has ever been inside.

**Attempt three, Wednesday, the right shape, and it fell over for a reason that took him an evening to accept:** to test a link you need both ends, and he had one end on the back of a receipt in a woman's handwriting and the other on a screen in a building he had not been inside since the eighth of December, and there is no lawful way for a man with no employer and no card to get a line out of a screen four miles away. **And that is the sentence the whole of Volume 02 has been arriving at for six weeks: the only instrument that has ever caught one in this business is a record with a name against it, and a record with a name against it is in a building, and he cannot get into the building, and the building's instrument is being overwritten every morning by a file from another building that nobody has ever told him about.** Then he telephones somebody at ten past ten at night, which he has done once in eleven weeks.

**The Friday morning of the eighth of January, twenty past nine, the district addressing service, and he has no card and no standing and asks the only question that matters: "Is there a record of the files you send out."** *Every one. Since about 2011. It's the supplied-file archive and it's there because suppliers ring and ask what we sent them on a given day, and it happens about twice a month.* *And a record of the changes inside them?* **"Change schedule. Same thing."** And then the sentence that stops him: **"Nobody has ever asked me for either of those. That is not a complaint. I am telling you it because you asked me the question as though it was ordinary and it is not ordinary, and I have been doing this for six years and the archive has never been out of the drawer."** A records officer of about fifty called **Mrs Toft**, who has never been asked for anything. Two days' notice, and on the Friday a printed schedule with a header on it.

> **DISTRICT ADDRESSING SERVICE — INBOUND CHANGE SCHEDULE.** Supplied to all subscribers, daily 04:1x. Retained under the supply agreement, **clause 7**. *Not a public document.*
> **05/12 | 88 SILLICK LN | OCCUPANCY | *(name)* | *(blank)* | DS-NCO-11190 | 06/12 04:11**
> **10/12 | 84 | … 11/12 | 80 | … (9 lines on the Museum's filtered zone in the same period. 141 in the district. A further 2,904 since 1 January.)**
> **SOURCE REF PREFIX DS-NCO: derived from delivery-system non-delivery and occupancy codes. A change carrying this prefix is applied without local verification. Clause 7 permits a subscriber to query any single line within twenty working days. No subscriber has ever queried a line.**

**Two thousand nine hundred and four changes in nine days. Not nine. About three hundred a day, in every zone, in every road, in the whole district, and none of them is a decision anybody made, and every one of them is a date and a field and a source reference and an hour in the morning. And the reason nobody has ever asked is the last line of the legend: no subscriber has ever queried a line.**

**Monday the eleventh, ten in the morning, the systems office, fourth floor, on a witness notice under item six, in company, after eight, on a systems day, which is the one day of the week on which anybody in that building will let a stranger stand in a room with a kettle in it and not ask him a single question about why.** Two documents on a table, four inches apart, and they are the only two documents in the entire business that matter.

> **[1] DELIVERY OFFICE CHANGE LOG — FLS-2, extract, 16.12.24, 09:52.** *05/12 16:22 · 88 SILLICK LN · NAME · (populated) → (cleared) · NCO · OP-2201.* **Held on the back of a receipt, in a woman's handwriting, given out loud in a room with a printer in it on 16 December to a man in a coat. Not a copy. Not a release. The only copy.**
> **[2] DISTRICT ADDRESSING SERVICE — INBOUND CHANGE SCHEDULE.** *05/12 · 88 SILLICK LN · OCCUPANCY · (name) → (blank) · DS-NCO-11190 · applied 06/12 04:11.*
> **And a sheet of Museum notepaper under both of them, with four lines and a distance on it, and the distance is on the sheet because somebody asked for it.**

> **JOIN. E. VENN, called as a witness, item 6. 11 Jan 2025, 10:20. In the presence of I. PELL, systems technician, and M. ILES, Senior Conservator and Accession Registrar.** **1.** The same effective date appears in two systems: 5 December 2024. **2.** The systems are not independent. The district's occupancy field is changed by an inbound change carrying a source prefix derived from a delivery-system code, applied without local verification. The delivery office is on Nether Kiln Lane. The district addressing office is on Marsh Lane. **The distance between them is four miles.** The Museum of Quiet Saints is three quarters of a mile from the delivery office and is a subscriber to the district's daily file. **3. The delivery system is upstream. The district is downstream of it. This Museum is downstream of the district. This Museum has not been told, by any person, in any document, at any time, that it is downstream of a delivery sheet.** **4.** No screen was photographed. No exposure was made. Both documents are paper and both are transcribed by hand and are initialed by the Registrar. Condition of 1 December 2024 not engaged.

**And the clause, and the count, and the man of twenty-five.** *There's a clause seven. Clause seven says a subscriber may query any single line within twenty working days. And it has been in that agreement since about 2011 and it has never been used and I am a subscriber and I have known about clause seven since about the second week I was here.* He looks at the sheet and does the arithmetic and it takes him about nine seconds and he does it twice. **"And it is still open. There are two days of it left. The last of the nine was applied on the twelfth of December, the first working day counted from the fifteenth, and the twentieth working day is Wednesday the thirteenth of January, which is the morning the Hall reopens, and there is nobody in this building who has ever known that, because there is nobody in this building who was counting, and I was counting at about half past eleven this morning and I am the only person in the world who has ever counted."** And then, in four minutes, the whole of the moral position of the batch, and it is said by the systems technician and it is not said by the man with the notebook: **"Two days. And that is the entire instrument. Two days, on a form, in an agreement of about ninety pages, in a drawer, in a building four miles away, and it is the only legal thing anybody in this case has ever had, and it was in the drawer the whole time, and I have known about it since the second week I was here. And I have never used it, because there was never anything to use it on. A file comes in at a quarter past four every morning and I put it in a folder. That is a subscription. A subscription is not a record. **I have been receiving a record for four years and filing it as a subscription.**"** And: **"We use it. On Wednesday, tomorrow, in a room with a registrar in it, and I will write to a records officer of about fifty called Mrs Toft and ask her one question about one line, and the line is 88 Sillick Lane, and the question is what the field said on the fourth of December, and I have known for four years that I was allowed to ask and I have never once had a reason. And she is going to ring me, because the clause says a subscriber may query a line and it does not say how, and she is going to ask me why a museum wants to know what a field said on a Wednesday in December, and I am going to have to write a sentence, and I have never written a sentence in that agreement in four years, and I have got two days."**

**And then the answer, and it is worse than anybody in this business had supposed, and it is on a piece of A4 that anybody in this city can obtain for nothing, and nobody has.** Not a Registry form, not a Museum form, not a form in this case at all: the head of the district addressing service's own **occupancy definition**, on page one of the walk-survey instruction given to about two hundred and thirty postmen.

> **OCCUPANCY. The name of the person or organisation responsible for the connection of services at this address.** **Used for the recovery of supplies and for the routing of correspondence. A change of occupancy does not of itself alter the existence of the address, the number of persons at it, or any person's legal residence.**

**Responsible for the connection of services. Not a person. Not a resident. Not a household. A payee.** A name field in this city is a field for who to bill, and who to write to, and it fills in from a walk survey in which somebody knocks on a door and is told, and it clears itself overnight when a code is set, and it is a billing contact and a correspondence route, and it is the oldest and most ordinary thing in the built world, and it is the same size as a person, and it does not know that. **And a district addressing service has about nine hundred subscribers, and the electoral roll is built off it, and the council tax base is built off it, and a public museum's local-notification list is built off it, and the fourth line of a card at plinth thirty-four has been in a public room for about a hundred and twenty years under the words *retained for completeness of the Low Ward series*, and the field in front of the name on that card has always been an occupancy field, and the name that was in it was the name of an organisation that does not exist.** **And a council's occupancy field went empty at twenty-two minutes past four in the afternoon on Saturday the fifth of December 2024, and a card went into a letter box, and it came back.**

**And he asks it in a room and waits for the answer and gets the fourth answer.** *Ivo said a name field is for finding him. Mara, you said on the fourth of December that a name is a thing that happens to a person. A systems technician at Nether Kiln Lane said the code is about a house that isn't a home. And this says a name field is for somebody to pay a bill, and it has said that since about 2011, and it says that a change of occupancy does not of itself alter the existence of the address or the number of persons at it or any person's legal residence. So I want to ask the thing I have been carrying since the Sunday the thirteenth of December: **what is a name field for.**"* Mara Iles takes her reading glasses off, which she has not done in the room before. **"For the bill. That is the answer and it is the right answer and it is the whole answer, and it has been available to anybody who wanted it since about 2011 on page one of a two-page instruction given to two hundred and thirty postmen, and I have never seen it, and neither has he, and neither has the Director, and it is the most important sentence anybody has said in this building since the seventh of November, and it is not a sentence anybody said. **It is a definition. It is in a box at the top of a form.**"** And then the man of twenty-five, and it is the correct correction: **"And it is the answer to a different question, and it is on the form, and the question you are asking is not the question the form answers. The form answers *what the field*. You are asking *what it is for*."** **"And nobody knows. That is the actual answer. It was built for a bill, and then about forty other things got hung off it, and there are about nine hundred subscribers, and the furthest one from the beginning is a Museum that prints a card about a bell and puts the occupant of a flat on it, and that one has been doing it for a hundred and forty years, and if you asked the man who wrote the original instruction in about 2011 what the field was for he would say a bill, and he would be right, and it would not help."**

**The seven consequences, held open, none of them resolved, in full, because a first ten days exists to hold things open and to name them.** One: clause 7 closes on Wednesday the thirteenth, nobody in the building knows it is there, a man of twenty-five has known for four years and has never had a reason, and he has to write a sentence about a person by Wednesday, and nobody in this city can write that sentence except a man who is barred from the building it is about. Two: a wire with nine blanks and no history, and a last good copy of four hundred and twelve names in brown paper bands in a box behind a counter, and about ninety people at a shut door on Friday. Three: two thousand nine hundred and four changes in nine days in every zone, about a third of a street in a fortnight, nine addresses off a road in Broad Street in three days in the last week of November, eleven households on that road who have answered their doors this month because their fields are still full, and a row on a rounds sheet every Thursday, and the rounds sheet is the only list that has any of these households on it. Four: a school of about three hundred and eighty children and about two hundred adults with a mat on premises inventory line 214, a woman of fifty-four who will not put a child's name on a card, and a public museum that has cut its first card in a hundred and forty years with four words in the field where a name goes. Five: Form A/3, registered and posted on the twelfth of December, eleven stamps, a period of twelve months in the box for a period, unanswered, and it will be answered on or about **the sixth of February, which is the same day as the first inspection slot on a rota in Northglass, and neither of those two facts was arranged by anybody.** Six: a bell on a plinth in a sealed case in a room that is shut, one note at ten minutes to eleven on the morning of the twenty-seventh of November in a room of eighty, one at four minutes past eleven on the morning of the thirteenth of December in a room of about forty, none since, the crack in its shoulder still open under the staple, not going to heal, **and it has lost a gallery and a fortnight because of a mat, and nobody in this building has any idea whether that is a thing it did or a thing that happened.** And seven, and it is his, and it is the oldest: a man of thirty-two with a sheet of Museum notepaper in front of him with two documents on it and four miles written at the top, and nine pages in a notebook that cost thirty-five pence, and a cardboard folder on a shelf between a boiler and a chair with a company's letter in it that he has not put in a Museum file and is not going to, on purpose, under clause six, and **a municipal remittance that arrives on the first of the month at cost centre M.Q.S. 4 for a post that no longer exists**, and a mother in a laundrette who has never once asked him for anything, and a letter on the shelf above the boiler that is eighteen years old and has not moved and that he is not going to open in January because he said he would do it after he had told somebody everything and there is a thing on a table in this city he has not told. **And a bell in a corridor four miles away, at about eleven minutes past eleven on the morning of the eleventh of November 2024, half a tone flat, about a second and a quarter, of something three floors away, on a line that died on the fifteenth of October so that nothing came down the wire and nobody came. He has never said that to anybody. It is in a notebook, in his own hand, in a set of figures he cannot prove, and the word *or* in front of his own figures is still the honest part of the page, and there is a woman in Sillick Lane with a hundred and forty names in a drawer and a woman of seventy-eight who has read a sheet of carbon in her own hand and a man of twenty-five who is standing in a systems office with a mug in his hand and two days in which to write a sentence about a person, and none of them knows, and he is not going to tell them, and there is nobody in this city he is allowed to tell.**
