**And the two documents stayed on the table, and the two documents are the cliffhanger, and the cliffhanger is a fact and not a shape: 5 December 2024, 16:22, a delivery sheet; 5 December 2024, 04:11 the next morning, a district file, four miles away; the same date, two systems, two buildings, and a museum three quarters of a mile from the first of them, downstream of both, with four hundred and twelve rows on a wire that is overwritten every morning and has no memory, and nine blanks in it, and one printed run in brown paper bands in a box behind a counter, and a card in a plastic sleeve on the front of a mat that says *no name available* in the field where a name goes.**

**And the last entry, written out longhand at about half past eleven that night, on the ninth page of a book with nine pages in it, in the middle of it and not at the end, and not torn out, because tearing it out is a thing he would do on his own in a room with a door shut: a name field is for the bill, and that is the answer and it is in a box at the top of a form and it has been there since 2011, and the question is not the one the form answers, and there are about nine hundred subscribers and about two hundred and thirty postmen and a Museum with a hundred and forty years of a rope line in it, and the furthest thing from the beginning of the chain is a public card about a bell, and nobody in the chain has ever been asked the question, and the reason nobody has ever been asked it is that every one of them is a person doing a correct thing inside a department, and **a thing you can be asked about is the only thing in this business that has ever caught anything. So somebody has to be asked, in a room, with a document. That is all.** And: *I cannot do it, because I am barred from the building and I am not a member of the public of anything and I have no standing in any room in this city, and the last time I was in a room with a form in it I was a witness, and the time before that I was a man with a notebook in a doorway, and before that I was a man in a corridor at twenty-six minutes past one in the morning with nothing. And there is a clause seven that closes on Wednesday and nobody in this building knows it is there and I am not the subscriber and a man of twenty-five has to write a sentence about a person and he has never written one. **So somebody has to ask.***

---

# VOLUME 02, CHAPTER SUMMARIES — BATCH 0002 (CHAPTERS 61–70), "THE SENTENCE, AND THE ROOM"

## Chapter 0061 — Clause Seven (Wed 13 Jan 2025, 10:00 – 18:00)

**The Hall reopens at ten and about ninety people are in it before eleven and the mat is on the floor on a plastic sheet inside the rope with a card that says *no name available*, and about seventy of the ninety walk within four feet of it and not one stops.** The workers' panel is forty-seven days old and the tape has gone the colour of weak tea and the bell is silent. Elias Venn is in the room on a pound and reads the card four times and works out that the description on it is word for word the one he heard read out to Frank Dunning on 22 December, **and does not check it — the fourteenth time he has not asked.** At a quarter past ten a systems technician has a clause 7 form out on the fourth floor. **At half past eleven Mrs Toft telephones from Marsh Lane — not about a query, because none has been sent, but because she has a form in front of her with a box on it that says *no query received* and she has to fill it in at four o'clock, and the box has never been filled in, and out of order, because it is the only part of this she cares about, she asks why a museum wants to know what a field said on a Wednesday in December. She asks three questions in the right order and the third is whether the answer contains a person's name.** Ivo writes four paragraphs in about an hour and a half and sends them at ten past two — the twentieth working day, the day the clause closes. **The answer comes at ten past four: `DUNNING, F.`, a name the case has had in a plastic wallet since 24 December, and it took four minutes.** **The corridor scene is about the minute and not about the morning: he says *I wrote it, this afternoon, four paragraphs, about a person, to a stranger*, and that he should have told Elias Venn he could not ask it, and the Director says *that is a sentence about a procedure, it happens to have a person in it*, and he says that is the whole of what he has learned in seven weeks and he has learned it from a woman who refused him in a library.** The Director's position: the Museum did not ask the question; a man asked it and was not allowed to and found the man who was. Elias is not the subscriber and cannot be, and that is the first lawful door in the case he cannot walk through.

## Chapter 0062 — The Comparison (Mon 18 – Tue 19 Jan 2025)

**The clause 7 answer is a null and is minuted as a null, and the finding is that the clause permits *any single line* and the case needs a file.** Mara Iles finds item 6 of the inquiry's terms of reference — *any other matter the chair directs* — which has been doing nothing since 18 October. The Director's letter is signed on the 18th with the cost said aloud first: a records officer is going to write down that the Museum cannot read its own history, and it will be in a file in her building. **The reply comes on the 19th with the record of issue on the fourth page: nine entries in six years, eight her own retention audits, the ninth a museum, in her own hand — the first document in this business a person kept on her own initiative to record that she had been asked.** The binder: every morning from 1 November to 19 January, eighty mornings. **Eight December mornings are transcribed in ink with a registrar present, and the change is in three mornings and not one: 412/412 to the file of Sat 5 Dec, 412/411 from Sun 6 Dec, 412/409 from Fri 11 Dec, 412/408 from Sat 12 Dec — no row added, removed or altered, no field but OCCUPANCY touched. And the second half of the finding is worse: four of the nine have a morning in that binder and five of them have a date and no morning anywhere in this case at all, because the district's extract for the period carries nine lines in this Museum's zone and this case holds four of them.** About a third of one street, stated once, as addresses and never as persons, with the Registrar's own sentence that nobody in the room is entitled to convert it. **Ivo Pell nearly says why and is stopped by a registrar who uses the word *prosecution* to mean *don't*.** Hargreaves is not on the telephone this chapter; the depot's position is carried by Roy Tilley in Chapter 66. Elias, on a witness notice, initials a sheet that says in capitals that no document was taken by the witness, and does not ask for the binder, and notices **a fourth column on the front sheet that he does not read**, and writes down that he knows he is going to want to read it.

## Chapter 0063 — No Name Available (Wed 20 Jan 2025)

**A public meeting in the Hall of Former Faiths at one o'clock, about ninety people in the room and about nine of the four hundred and twelve addresses not able to be written to, and nobody in the room able to say which nine, and the reason is the subject of the meeting.** The Director decided on 13 January, said the cost out loud first, and minuted the order. **She says the 1902 sentence out loud in a public room: the bell was cast in 1902, the fourth line of the card says *Municipal Works*, there is no such body and no file, and I found out on the thirtieth of December and I am the Director.** Nobody makes a noise. She refuses to offer a writer. **Ivo Pell asks for four minutes and gets them, from the woman at the front counter who has been there nineteen years and who gave them to him without asking him a single question about why, and stands on the wrong side of the rope and tells ninety people what a name field is: a billing contact, thirty-one characters, an update button, and a machine at a quarter past four in the morning.** A woman asks why he is not telling the council. **At about two the Director says what the Museum is going to do about the mat: nothing, and the four words stay, and it cost a gallery and about ninety people with a pound each on the Friday the eighth.** At twenty to two somebody asks what the rope is for, and she says she does not know, in front of ninety people, and asks the room. Nobody knew. **She does not say *it is not one rope*; that is Chapter 64's discovery and it is left to Chapter 64.**

## Chapter 0064 — The Hundred and Forty Years of a Rope (Thu 21 Jan 2025)

**Four competent people are asked what the rope line in the Hall of Former Faiths is for and all four answer about ropes.** Fenn: *you can see it; it's the only object in this building that doesn't want anything from anybody.* Mrs Farrier: *there are nine in a bin in the store and about a hundred and forty came out of it in 1994 and there has never been an inventory, because they are not objects, they are furniture.* Nunn, a transfer clerk who has tied about forty thousand: *a rope is a printing press with a walking speed.* **Hattie Bramm comes in at ten past ten on a pound, in the ordinary column, in company with nobody, and is the only person in this case that nobody can direct.** She finds the join in about nine seconds: **it is not one rope, it is two, joined in the middle of the floor by a sheet bend — a sailor's knot on a landlocked floor — in about four seconds in good light, about 1935, and the west half is thinner and older.** And then she stops the conversation: *don't you dare make those two the same number — the rope and my hundred and forty names in a drawer, because the moment they are in the same sentence the rope becomes evidence and my hundred and forty become furniture, and one of them is a list of women who stood at a gate in the wet in 1974.* **She declines, in a public room, to have the workers' panel taken down, and the reason is that the correction will go under it and the person who reads it will be somebody in about 2040 who lifts a thing off a case.** Ivo Pell gets on his knees to count the lays and then refuses to open a box of minutes from 1935, because *a thing stops being a rope the second time somebody writes a word about it in a room with a date in the minute.* And Hattie Bramm says, to about twenty people, without looking at Elias Venn once, what he is: **the reason, and furniture, and the only person nobody has ever had to account for.**

## Chapter 0065 — Two Pounds (Fri 22 Jan 2025)

**The mat on the floor of the Hall has been there twenty-three days and a registrar kneels on it with a steel rule and finds that the card beside it describes every mat in the city, in her own words, in three places, including two staples.** The mat turns over: the wear is on both faces and in the middle and does not know which way a door goes, because it lay in a school corridor. **A card is a class and the Registrar says so before anybody asks her.** Frank Dunning telephones the front desk, which he has never done, about **a second mat: two pounds, off a shelf on Fisher Lane, down by a wall at about number forty since Saturday the 18th, with the same three rules and no new sheet, and about nine people standing on it.** Ivo Pell names the thing nobody had named: **a practice with a mat in it is not portable; a practice with two pounds in it is, and about nine of them have been sold in this city since the fourth of January and nobody's job is to know.** Marion Kelsh refuses the practice for a school before she is asked twice — *a school has about three hundred and eighty doors and not one of them is anybody's* — and says she wants the mat in the gallery back and is not going to ask. **The Director almost says the Museum could lend them a mat, and a registrar of twenty-eight says no before she has finished, and the reason is a duty, a schedule, a Friday count, and a woman of sixty-five whose mat goes to a cupboard.** The Director then says, in a corridor, deliberately, in her own mouth, **that it is not a Museum practice.**** **And at ten past four Ivo Pell offers Elias Venn a word, and he says no, and Ivo does not push, and the refusal is on the page and the cost of it is that he has said no to the only kind thing anybody has offered him in eight weeks.** Mara Iles writes four lines to S. Tully, the first letter this building has ever sent to Northglass that was not a reply to anything.

## Chapter 0066 — The Person on the Round (Mon 25 Jan 2025)

**FLS-2 comes out of a rack on a Monday, and the only object in this case ever held by the only person legally entitled to carry it is in a postman's inside pocket three quarters of a mile from a delivery office.** Elias Venn goes down to the corner outside 88 with no reason and says so at the start. **Sixteen fields have cleared on the round since the beginning of December and the sheet is not Roy Tilley's and goes into a rack of about nine hundred on 9 February 2025 and is binned two years later, and there is not one person in this city whose job it is to know it was different.** He is being moved in February. He will not find out and will not want to: *if I find out, then on the ninth of February I'm a man who knew, and then the office knows I've been asking.* **He offers to write the sixteen out — addresses, dates, column — in his own hand, with no name on it, and Elias Venn says no, because a record with no name on it is a weapon with no name on it, and what he offers instead is *the number, out loud, on a pavement, in daylight, in two days, and then a record with a name on it that nobody can be got at over*.** **Denise Hargreaves gives the depot's position for the third time and it is correct: the sheet is right, and it asks not whether a house is still there but whether we have somebody to give it to, and those are not the same question and have not been since about 1994; and about nine hundred sheets are in a rack and two years ago somebody chose nine of them and audited them and she does not know which nine and will not pretend to.** He walks back up the lane with nothing in his hand and puts himself in a postbox, and a rule goes at the top of the first page of the nine-page notebook: **a fact a person gives me on a street is theirs until it is in a file. Do not write it down.**

## Chapter 0067 — A Name in a Field (Tue 26 Jan 2025)

**Ivo Pell has believed since 21 December that the same daily feed the electoral roll is built off is printed at the top of the file. Mrs Toft reads him the header over the telephone and there is a last sentence he has never once read: *This file is not a register of persons. It is not a source for the register of electors, the council tax base, the electoral roll, or any statutory return. It is a distribution list with a name on it.*** And it makes the case smaller and worse: **the roll is made by about two hundred and twenty registration officers going round with satchels, knocking, taking a name in the person's own hand, and a form has no box that asks whether the house is reachable by post.** Ivo Pell proposes the public register — lawful, free, and the whole street in an afternoon. **Mara Iles refuses it in front of him, tells him to go and do it if he wants to, and says the reason: a name on a card in a public room is a hook.** He does not go. **Marion Kelsh brings both her books, refuses both before she is asked twice, separates them — the premises inventory is a list of things with about a hundred and forty names in it and does not say where anybody lives, and the school roll is a card index in an office in the same building with about three hundred and eighty lines in it and a name and a house in every one — gives the only number of people in the case, about three hundred and eighty children and about two hundred adults, and refuses the arithmetic: you cannot put a number of children next to a number of addresses, and a difference between two numbers is a number nobody can rule against. She attributes the ruling to a woman from a conciliation service who told her in a room in Ferry Bank in November, and says out loud that she was told it and did not hear it.** And at half past one a registration officer of thirty-one on a doorstep in Broad Street says that about two thirds of the people on a roll are the people a letter does not reach, and that if a letter does not reach them they do not come to the door, and that the reason nobody has ever noticed that as a problem is that the people it happens to are the people who were never on.

## Chapter 0068 — The Lawful Door (Wed 27 Jan 2025)

**Item 6 lays out the doors of this city in a table in a committee room and finds that the only instrument any of them reaches a household with is a sheet of A4 in a plastic wallet on a table in Sillick Lane, which has no form under it and dies with whoever keeps it.** A form is the only door that has ever opened anything; the Museum has about four hundred forms and not one of them reaches a door. **The item goes to a board, because items go to boards, and Frank Dunning comes in on the Wednesday, as he has every week since December, and reads his own sheet of A4 discussed on a card in his own museum.** Margaret Priest advises him to say no and does not ring the woman herself, and that is the only one of four things she has not asked in nineteen years that she is proud of. **The Director telephones Hattie Bramm, states the actual question before asking it, and then hands the telephone to a registrar.** Hattie Bramm refuses in about eleven seconds and gives the reason before she is asked: *a list of people is not evidence until it is in a file, and the second it is in a file it can be used, and the day a Museum has got that sheet it is a museum that knows who lives on that street, and you cannot un-know it.* **The decision, at ten past four, four lines, cost said aloud first: the Museum will not receive, retain, request or file any record of the practice, and will keep only the fact of its own observation — one officer present on the fourth, eighth and eleventh of January, and a second officer who records it from a photograph and a report, and the fact of a person speaking a name aloud at a door — and has no duty in respect of it, and is a party and not a bystander, and will answer for that in due course.** The two names are the Director's and the Registrar's, and the Registrar has never been to Sillick Lane in her life and insisted on being named, and gave the reason: **a record with one name on it is a record that dies with that person, and a record with two names on it survives one of us.** **Doreen Sarn's seventh refusal comes back by post in two days in four words — *No. Ask somebody else* — and goes on the board and not in a file, because the six pieces of paper in five buildings about six people who would not have a thing of theirs put in a file are a list of people, and a file is a set of hooks this building owns.** And Elias Venn, in a corridor, says *right*, and tells her it is the only document in the case with a person's name on it that was put there on purpose and did not cost a living person anything.

## Chapter 0069 — The Ones with No List (Thu 28 Jan 2025)

**Broad Street, half past nine in the morning, a pavement, and a man with nothing in his hands at all for the first time since 6 October.** A registration officer of thirty-one takes him round four of the nine. Two are no answer and she writes the date on the back of her own form and puts it in her satchel. **The third is a woman of about sixty who has lived at that address nineteen years, has paid her council tax every month of it, has never been on anything and has never had a letter about it, and does not know the word register; and a form is filled in on a hall table in about nine minutes and she is told it is not a punishment and that she has been voting for forty years at a longer door into the same room.** And then the moral floor, once, by somebody competent, and not argued about: **there is no form in this country for a person at a house that no system has got a name for. I have looked. I am the person who would know.** The reason is that a form wants a name, a name is what a person gives a stranger at a door, and the only person who has ever spoken to anybody about those nine houses is the woman with the satchel, who writes a code on a form that a printer prints and a printer does not need to know which houses. **And in a corridor in Hall Lane, Marion Kelsh states the floor in her own words: the ones with no name in any system cannot be counted, listed, reached or found later, and the number of people in this case who cannot be helped is not a number and is not going to become one, and every attempt this case has made to turn it into a number has produced a worse number.** And at a quarter past three on a table in a hall in Sillick Lane, Frank Dunning says the other half and says it better: **it is three weeks and three days old, it is a piece of A4 in a plastic wallet, and it dies with him, and nobody is going to come and take it off him, and that is the correct arrangement because if somebody did, it would be a thing somebody was keeping, and then it would be a department, and about nine people on a pavement would be people in a department, and it would be finished.** And he has not been asked a question about it since it started, and that is the best three weeks he has had in nine years, and he is sixty-five.

## Chapter 0070 — The Same Date, in Two Systems, Again (Wed 3 – Fri 5 Feb 2025)

**The fourth column on the front sheet of the district's file is headed OCCUPANT (PRIOR), and it is populated in nine rows out of four hundred and twelve, and the nine are all names, and the Museum threw all nine away every morning for a fortnight without knowing it had ever received them.** The district keeps the old value as a routing courtesy: it is there so a letter going to a house that has just changed hands does not go to the wrong person. Eight of the nine prior values are names the Museum already holds. **The ninth is `NAYLOR, W.` at 80 Sillick Lane — a man who died in about 2019, whose name had been correct in that field for about forty years and then, for about eight months, correct about a flat in which a living man was living, because a tenancy does not notify an addressing service and nobody has ever thought about why not — and the row is dated the eleventh of December, applied on the twelfth at eleven minutes past four, which is the morning this Museum printed nine blanks and put them in nine envelopes.** A woman of eighty-one said that name at that door on 4 January and was told *Who?* **The three documents and the three dates, and the finding: the fifth of December 2024 is in two systems and the sixth is in three, and the sixth is the only one of the three that appears in a document this Museum has ever held, twice, in its own subscription, on paper. The fifth is a body pressing a key on a Saturday afternoon and it is the only one with a person in it, and it exists in this city on exactly one piece of physical paper, which is a counter receipt in a plastic wallet in a rented room, and it is not a copy and it is not in a file and it dies with him.** In a rented room above a laundrette he takes the wallet out and looks at the four things in it. **And then a registrar of twenty-eight writes a dead man's name on a Museum document, in her own hand, in a room, in a column headed OCCUPANT (PRIOR), with a witness, transcribed by hand, no photograph, no exposure — and it is the first person's name this institution has put on a document on purpose in about a hundred and twenty years, and there is no field on any form in this building for a person's name in a description of a place, and there has not been one since about 1860, and she wrote it because the person who asked her to is not allowed to.** Fenn says put it on, because a hole in a description of a place lets whoever comes next fill it in. The Director reads it into the record and specifies the order: **a woman of eighty-one said it first and could not write it down and has not been asked since, and the reason she has not been asked is that nobody in this case has thought to ask her.** And the last page of the ninth notebook, on the evening of the fifth of February, is five lines, and the last of them is the answer to the question he has been carrying since 13 December, given in his own hand: **a name field is for the second person, not the first. It is not for the person it names — it is for whoever has to find them again, and the person it names is the one thing in the chain with nothing to do.**

---

# VOLUME 02, CHAPTER SUMMARIES — BATCH 0003 (CHAPTERS 71–80), "THE MIDPOINT"

## Chapter 0071 — The Slot (Sat 6 Feb 2025, 10:20 – 13:10, Northglass)

**A rota on a wall in a plastic sleeve, printed in November, four assessors, and a box that says 11:00. There is no emergency provision and it has not had one since about 2011.** Elias Venn is in a building in Northglass **because a piece of paper has his name on it** — Form A/3, lodged 12 December 2024, eleven stamps — and that is the first room he has walked into in this case that he was named to be in. Mara Iles and Ivo Pell are with him, and Ivo has to identify the subscriber before anything about a file is said in the room, which is correct. **S. Tully, about forty-five, has been an assessor for nineteen years and has never been spoken to by anybody in this case, and she reads her own sentence of the thirtieth of December back at them with the punctuation, and says she has never had that done to her before.** Two answers come back on paper, both late, both on the same morning, and neither is the answer anybody wanted. **The first, to Mara's letter of 22 January: *this schedule has no box for it*** — and then four lines nobody asked for: the schedule describes things and a proposal must name the thing; **there is no object in the Museum's file, because a description is not a classification and a card is a description**; and there is exactly one category in the schedule for a place and it is `PLACE OF INTERMENT`, it requires a date of permanence, **and she would not use it on a door mat.** **The second, to A/3: a period runs from a determination, there is no determination and there cannot be one while there is no box, so the request is not refused and not granted and not capable of being answered and has never begun** — and the reply went out of time at nine that morning and is issued late with the lateness on it. And the only thing in either document that is not a refusal: **she lists the question, and the next determination day is Saturday the sixth of March 2025, and the assessor is her, and the Registry has never listed a question off a form that had never been a determination.** A registrar of twenty-eight reads her own letter into box nine of the A/3 — *supplementary statement by another person in the matter*, never used in nineteen years. And then a screen produces his name: **VENN, E., ninth entry in the municipal fixed-positions file of 16 September 2024, cost centre M.Q.S. 4, status active, grade blank, remittance monthly and unbroken** — a payee field, correct, and the only thing in this city that has kept him a legal neighbour since 8 December. **S. Tully says the sentence, once, and says she is not going to say it again: a person's legal neighbour is not a thing the Registry grants; it is a thing other systems happen to agree on by writing a name in a field, and none of them has ever asked the person.**

## Chapter 0072 — Two Answers (Wed 10 Feb 2025, 11:00 – 13:10, the Museum)

**Two documents from Northglass are read in a committee room and the room is a day old.** Mara Iles got what she asked for and says so: *on the twenty-second of January I asked whether the answer would be the thing she had already told me, and it is the first, and it came on the Saturday morning on paper with a date on it.* The Director says the cost before deciding, twice: **giving up the only external office that has ever said no to them on the record, and accepting a determination date they have no standing to reach** — *the sixth of March is a date on a question that cannot be asked, and we are going anyway, and I would like that minuted in those words, because in about nine weeks somebody is going to describe this case as a campaign.* **A minute in two decisions. E. VENN, called as a witness under item 6. Present. No copy. Two originals retained by the Registry.** Then Ivo Pell asks **what a date of permanence is**, and the finding of the morning: **a door mat has none because you can buy another one for two pounds; a house has one because somebody has written a piece of paper about when the term ends** — and then the sentence that turns the batch: **the Museum's threshold group is not a list, it is a filter, selected by a postal round number and a district, so a household that moves round inside the district stays on it and a household that leaves falls off it, and neither is a decision about a person.** **A tenancy is the only document in this country that says a person is a neighbour of a place, and there are about four hundred and twelve of them in this ward and the Museum has never seen one.** And they cannot ask: **Roy Tilley's round changed on Tuesday the ninth, FLS-2 went back into a rack, and nobody in the building knows what day of the week the ninth was, because a man on a pavement in January said he would rather be a man who got moved than a man who knew, and he was right.** The Director discovers in a corridor that **she has been Director for nineteen years and does not know who owns the freehold of a street she has written four hundred letters to**, and orders a written search. And in a corridor at ten to one somebody writes four words on the back of a printout — **THIS SCHEDULE HAS NO BOX FOR IT** — and cannot find anything to do with it, and the Director says *write it down and leave it, and if nobody can say in four days why it is the most useful sentence anybody has said in nine weeks, then it isn't one, and it goes in the bin.*

## Chapter 0073 — The Landlord's Returns (Thu 11 Feb 2025, 09:10 – 16:10)

**A name field is for the bill, and the only way that has ever produced anything is to ring the water.** A records officer of about fifty — asked the right question for the first time in six years, *read me the person who is responsible for the connection of services* — says **ring the water** in about two seconds. A woman in Metered Supply Accounts reads an account holder off a screen for a converted block on the east side of Sillick Lane: **MORTON HAY & BAKER (LETTING & MANAGEMENT) LTD, 11 Pemberton Row, Low Ward; freeholder of record NEWTON BAY (ESTATES) LIMITED.** Neither is on any list this case has been given. **Both are on a bill.** The Director's letter states the cost first, twice, and asks **a question and not a document** and says *if you decline, a letter saying so is the most useful thing this Museum will receive this month.* **Sonia Boulton, about thirty-nine, MRICS, letting agent, comes herself at half past three and will not send anything**, because a letting agent who posts a schedule of tenancies to a stranger is a letting agent who has posted a rent roll, and **she has been refusing them since the autumn — six times, to a council, a councillor, a broker, a journalist, a competitor, and a man in a coat through a records clerk in four seconds.** She carries **a hardback book: every visit she has made since about 2019, in her hand, with a date — the only register in this case, nine hundred yards from a corner of Sillick Lane, and she will not give it to anybody and says the same sentence about it as about the schedule.** She refuses the rent roll, and then she answers the question **by reading the programme out loud in a committee room, in about nineteen minutes, with no names in it**: **an internal review paper of 24 August 2023, nine blocks, about four hundred and twelve tenancies, a quarterly cycle, a block enters programme on a cycle date, no tenancy in that block is renewed at the next term end, notices served on the cycle date at the earliest date permitted, every notice on paper, by hand, at the property, by a person whose name is on the notice, none challenged, none late.** Block five's term ends fell between 20 November and 17 December 2024; **block six went in on 1 January 2025 and its term ends fall between 17 February and 24 March 2025 — this month.** And then the second half, which is the whole of what there is: **a tenancy is the only document in this country that says a person is a neighbour of a place, and the house emptied on purpose and the house where nobody was in on Tuesday afternoon will be indistinguishable in about four months, and that is the harm.** She signs a **Statement of Tenure Programme** — four lines of facts and a repeated refusal, no tenant, no rent, no arrears, no date of birth — **the first document in this case with a name on it and nothing else on it, and the first that could be read out in a hearing without a single person in it.** And she will not name the street's four hundred and twelve to the Museum, because the Museum's four hundred and twelve is a filter on geography and hers is a list of people.

## Chapter 0074 — The Reading That Was Wrong (Fri 12 Feb 2025, 10:10 – 13:00, the store behind the front counter)

**Four documents on a conservation board laid across two trestles in the store behind the front counter, with the counter shutter up and the queue going past the doorway, and the comparison is done in ink, by hand, and it does not fit.** **The stage is the point and it was moved in the review of 2026-09-26: this chapter used to be a fourth-floor committee room with the blinds half down, which is where Chapters 72, 73 and 78 also happen, and the batch varied its subject and not its room. It is now the one place in the building where the only complete print of the Museum's own four hundred and twelve actually is — a print run of 2 December 2024 in brown paper bands in a box behind a counter, with a drawer card from 1954 that says THRESHOLD GROUP, found in that box — and a woman of about fifty with the key board opens the drawer and puts the key back in the right place and is not asked a question about any of it.** Ivo Pell: **eighty-four, ninety and eighty are the landlord's programme and about a fortnight of a man with a handheld; the machine is three weeks long and it is a body.** And then: **"Eighty-eight is a Saturday problem."** The tenancy at 88 Sillick Lane is current, has been since about 2019, runs to the twenty-second of August, and has never been served. **A walk survey means a man at a door; a man at a door at four o'clock on a Saturday afternoon is not a decision; a man of about sixty-five was out, and the field cleared at twenty to four the next morning, and that is all of it.** The Registrar says **nobody is going to ask Frank Dunning whether he was at home on the afternoon of Saturday the fifth of December 2024**, and the reason is arithmetic, not kindness: *the only thing we would want from him is the word no, and a no about a Saturday afternoon is not about a field, and after that he is a man in a hearing who said one thing once.* And **the man who has been standing on that date since 16 December says so out loud in a room, in about nine seconds, and sits down: it is the only date in the case with a person in it, and I have been looking for a person since the sixth of December because I did not want the case to be a machine.** Ivo Pell is kind about it and he has to go and stand by a window, and the Director will not let it be minuted as a confession: *it is a correction of method and it is the most useful thing anybody in this room has said since the twenty-second of January.* The plastic wallet comes out on a table for the first time in nine weeks and goes back in: **not evidence of anything, not a copy, not in a file, and it dies with me — it is a receipt for a machine doing what a machine does, written on the back of it by a woman who wanted a stranger to have it.** And the second half, which is worse: **four of the nine blanks are the programme, one of the nine is a Saturday afternoon, and five of the nine carry no date in any document in any institution in this city, and a programme cannot explain a row that cannot be placed on a day** — and Ivo Pell says the five are the ones to be frightened of and the four are not, and that it is the first time in nine weeks he has wanted to be frightened of a smaller number than the other one.

## Chapter 0075 — THE MIDPOINT (Wed 17 Feb 2025, 10:30 – 15:00)

**About a hundred and ten people in the Hall of Former Faiths, doors propped, a sheet of A4 on a stand, the mat on its plastic sheet forty-nine days in, the panel eighty-two days old, and the bell silent for thirty-five days.** Before the meeting, in a committee room, **one line of a tenancy file is read out of a file belonging to a letting agent: SCAIFE, T., 90 Sillick Lane. Term ended 12 December 2024. Notice served 1 October 2024 by hand at the property. Keys returned. Deposit returned in full. No arrears. No correspondence outstanding. Forwarding address given** — and it is the only person's name read in a room in this case since 4 February, and it is on a sheet in a file in Low Ward and it is not on a card in this building and it is not on the practice sheet, and that is not an accident. Anja Ward gives four minutes and says **this Museum is the last institution in the chain and not the first, and it has behaved for nine weeks as though it were in the middle of it, and the furthest downstream of everything is a woman in a shop in Fisher Lane who has sold nine mats since the fourth of January and does not know it.** Sonia Boulton, from the wrong side of the rope, four minutes: **a landlord has done nothing wrong and if you go home and say one has, you will be wrong in a public room and wrong in a hearing and wrong in about eleven months when it matters. The last four inches of this are a man with a handheld, and there is nothing behind it — no conspiracy, no letter, no man in a room, only a cycle date, a notice, a lock-up and a skip.** **And what is being taken is not a house. It is the ability to be recognised as a legal neighbour: a name in a field, an address on a return, a knock that gets an answer.** Ivo Pell takes ninety seconds for the two things that do not fit, including that one of the nine is a Saturday afternoon. A man in his forties stands up and says: *I've been in the flat nineteen years, my notice went through the door on the first of January, my tenancy's finished on the eighteenth of March, and about four people in this room have used the word harm at me in ten minutes. So what am I meant to say to that.* And Sonia Boulton answers in nine seconds: **you are meant to say that you were there and now you are not, and the difference is a piece of paper, and the piece of paper is the only thing that makes you a neighbour of anywhere, and that file is kept six years and then destroyed on a Friday, and in six years the only version of the last nineteen years of your life at that address will be a forwarding address you gave me in December and a rent counter in a book.** Then the Director says the thing she has decided **not** to say: **there is a household in this ward whose tenancy ended in that programme and which is owed nothing and which appears on no list of people owed anything, and she could name it, and she is not going to, because a name of a person in a public room is a hook** — and closes with the escalation and with the volume's question: **is it a thing or a place, and nobody in this room knows, and the Registry has one box for a place and it is for a burial, and I have failed for nine weeks and I am going to say so plainly, because a Director who is still trying in March will cost somebody another fortnight.**

## Chapter 0076 — The Door That Is Not On A List (Thu 18 Feb 2025, 09:50 – 12:00, Broad Street)

**A pavement, a satchel, eleven forms, and a man with nothing in his hands for the second time in nine weeks.** Mairin Devlin refuses to say the moral floor again before he has asked for it. Five doors: a no answer, a no answer, a man of about seventy in a dressing gown who has never been on a roll and never wanted to be and says *go away* in nine seconds, a woman of about sixty who wants to know once whether being on the roll costs anything and is told **nothing — a list of people who have told us where they live, and the only people it costs anything to are people who have moved and don't tell us, and they get a letter** — and then **number four, which she has never stood in front of**, because the justification box on a special-visit form has wanted two lines for nine years and she has only ever been able to write *concern for vulnerable resident*, which is a lie everybody in that office knows is a lie. **The household at number four is not vulnerable: about thirty-four, works, pays, moved in on Sunday the twenty-ninth of November — the day after the third of the three Broad Street clearances — and is not on the roll, and the reason is a change of occupancy recorded four miles away on a Sunday and applied without anybody knocking.** The box gets a true sentence at last: **address not in roll following recorded change of occupancy, possible omission, date of change not in local copy** — three things about documents, no word about a person, and it will be approved because it is not a lie. **Then the going. He knocks at about eleven twenty and opens with a rule: I am not from the council, I am not from anywhere, here are three things and you can shut the door at any point.** One: **he says the moral floor in her twenty-one words, word for word, at a door, and says he is saying it because two professionals told him and he believed them.** Two: **the woman with the satchel is the only person in this county whose job it is to stand in front of a house and write your name in your own hand, and he is not going to write her address on anything.** Three: **I am not going to write your name down. Not today, not in a book, not on a form, not anywhere. I have got a rule at the top of the first page of my own notebook that says a fact a person gives me in a street is theirs until it is in a file, and I wrote it on the twenty-fifth of January after a postman told me the truth about something, and I have not broken it once and I am not going to start on your doorstep — and it costs me something, because in about a month I will not be able to say that I came here. That is the correct arrangement and it is because of you and not because of me.** She tells Devlin her name and the qualifying date and the form is filled in on a doorstep in about a quarter of an hour, in her own hand, and Devlin says the date out loud so she can be held to it: **Wednesday the twenty-fourth of March, in the afternoon, and if I am not here by four o'clock you can ring the office and ask for me by name, which is the only thing anybody in that building can actually do.** And the last thing said on that pavement is the finding: **they have got a mat down by the wall at about number forty in Sillick Lane, two pounds, off a shelf on Fisher Lane, three rules, about nine people, a sheet of A4 in a plastic wallet, and it does absolutely nothing. It is a man of about sixty-five putting his name out loud at a door where somebody lives. That is the only instrument in this city that does nothing and that is why it works and that is why nobody has stopped it.**

## Chapter 0077 — What the Practice Cannot Carry (Sat 20 Feb 2025, 15:10 – 17:30, 88 Sillick Lane)

**Six weeks and five days old, about nine people, a mat with its worn side facing into the house, a wallet on a table, and a Director in the doorway who came down the hill on a bus and paid a pound.** She states the cost first, in about ninety seconds, and then asks whether the Museum can be useful. **Frank Dunning says no in about four seconds and gives the reason before anybody asks him for it** — no to the sheet, no to a copy, no to a list, no to a register, no to a rota, no to anybody coming round with a clipboard — *and I have been waiting since December for somebody to ask me so they could tell me I'd said no.* Then she tells him, and he already knew: **his block goes into programme on the first day of April, his tenancy ends on the twenty-second of August and will not be renewed, he has known since November because a woman at number twelve has been telling people since September and nobody wrote it down, and nobody has ever said it out loud to him and everybody he has told has said *oh* and gone to their dinners.** He puts it on the sheet, because a date about a house is the only kind of thing that has ever gone on it. Then **Mara Iles says the other half and he is in the room and says four sentences in fifty minutes: the practice cannot be a register, cannot be scaled, cannot be a rota, and the reason is rule three — *anybody taking part may stop at any point, and nobody's asked afterwards why* — because a rota is a duty, and a duty a person can stop at any point without a form is a suggestion with a list on it, and a permission cannot be scaled, and nobody in this business has ever looked at that rule.** And the other other half: **the Museum can hold the mat and cannot hold the knock. A mat is two pounds and goes on a Friday count. A knock is not an object, has no accession number, and there is no box for one in this country, and the only reason there has not been one in four hundred years is that nobody has ever needed one, and about nine people in Low Ward do.** Ivo Pell asks **where has to be for this to be the same thing** — and nobody knows, and the two answers are incompatible, and the Registrar refuses to pick in that hall in February. Frank Dunning asks Elias Venn whether he has a word, and is told no, and says **a street does not own it either, because a street is a council with a different name and I have seen what a council does with a room** — and then concedes the line: **it is in a different hand, it has been on that sheet since 8 January, and he has never asked who wrote it and is not going to**; what is his is the table, the kettle and the running of it at three o'clock, and if he stops there is no practice and nobody is going to keep it for him. And last, a registrar asks permission to write four lines on his sheet, in her own hand, to be read out on the step **on the twenty-second of March**, promising to say nothing — the first thing anybody in this business has written that is a promise to say nothing — and he says *the twenty-second, not the twenty-first, on the twenty-second my tenancy has still got four weeks and two days to run and I am still the man with the table*, and the date is changed to the twenty-second.

## Chapter 0078 — The Party (Wed 24 Feb 2025, 10:00 – 17:00)

**A letter from Halliward Griswold & Reeve of 22 Pike's Buildings, Irving Quay, for Newton Bay (Estates) Limited, dated 19 February and delivered by hand on the Friday, and a one-page letter from the conciliation service — and the second one asks this Museum for the absence of something: *a nil return is a valid return, the officer would be grateful for one in those words, and a party that returns a document it does not wish to produce is worse for that party than a party that returns nothing.* The nil return is due the eleventh of March. The Board paper is due the eleventh of March.** The partner, about fifty-two, comes four days early because the next cycle date falls on 1 April and about forty households will get a piece of paper through a door on that morning. **He says he has been sitting in a room for a fortnight working out whether the Registrar is right — that a museum cannot put a name on a public card without making a hook of it — and that his client owns about four hundred and twelve addresses and he wanted to know whether she meant her building or his.** He establishes that **the programme was described in public on 17 February by the managing agent at a meeting the Director called, in a room she controls, in front of about a hundred and ten people, and that it came out of this building, and that he would rather she knew that today than on the eighteenth.** He tells him what his client's position is: none. He asks what the Museum thinks the matter is about — *a knock* — and says he will ask her to say it again in the same words in March in front of nine people, and that if she says it differently he will notice and will not mention that he has noticed. **And then he turns to the man of thirty-two at the end of the table, who has said nothing in ninety minutes, and asks him whether he writes it down, and is told: nine pages, thirty-five pence, no copy, not filed, deliberately, under clause six. He answers with three things, the third of them the sentence of the batch: *if your evidence cannot be produced then it cannot be tested. An untestable witness in a listed hearing is not a protection. It is a hole in the case with a person standing in it.*** And he is sure, and the partner says that is the whole of what he came for. **And then the schedule of collections: eleven entries, the entries for 1974 incomplete, the second of them not an object, a dead man's name and a house on it, in a board paper that may be requested by a member of the public for ever.** A trustee of about seventy, nine years on that board, **objects first and asks for the word *hook* to be put in the minute so that it will not be tidy**, and his objection is not the name — the name is correct and he read the support — **his objection is that this Museum told the public twice — in a public room in November and in a minute on 27 January — that it would not put a person's name in a public document and here is a man's name going into a Board paper.** He withdraws it and asks that it be minuted that he objected first, and says he has been going to look at a building for nine years and did not know it was possible that one of its schedules could contain a person. And Fenn cuts a card that is a statement and not a description, with the requester's name and a date and a reference to a minute on it, and puts it in a plastic sleeve on a front desk in a public lobby.

## Chapter 0079 — The Card Under the Panel (Thu 25 Feb 2025, 09:00 – 13:00)

**The panel goes back onto the case at plinth thirty-four and it is ninety days old, and the correction goes under it and nobody will ever read it, and it is correct.** Three lines struck through and dated the twenty-fifth, a paragraph underneath, her initials and Fenn's. **Fenn: *you can card a bell. You cannot card a rope. There is no line on the form for it and I have looked twice. It is not a thing, it is a fitting, and the day somebody prints a card that says what a rope is, that rope is an object and it goes in a case.*** And **the box on the ground floor is open, legitimately, for about twenty minutes, and the systems technician is not in the room and was not asked** — he said in January on his knees that nobody would let him open a box of minutes from 1935 on the strength of a systems technician with a Saturday systems day, and being right about the rope has cost him the room, and nobody mentioned it. Mrs Farrier has the key off the board and puts it back in the right place and says **she has said out loud for the first time in fourteen years that about half the minutes in there are *approved the cleaning of the stairs* and *agreed the milk contract*, and nobody has ever asked her, and she is not complaining, she is just saying it has been said.** The November 1935 entry says it all: the cord line is to be re-hung, the western portion having perished at two stanchions, new line of the same appearance and no heavier, **the join to be made as the join the old line was made — stated purpose: CIRCULATION** — the exhibition closed on the twenty-sixth, **attendance over six weeks 11,400**, the line to be moved four feet east to widen the route from the north door to the south door **which was found to be fouled at the third stanchion on the last three Saturdays and again on the closing day** — and **THE LINE IS NOT TO BE MOVED FOR THE PROTECTION OF THE OBJECTS. IT IS NOT ON THE SCHEDULE. IT IS FURNITURE.** Approved: J. H., Clerk of Works. **And the sentence in the minute and the sentence in a transfer clerk's mouth ninety years later are the same sentence: a rope is a printing press with a walking speed.** And a folded list in the back of the September 1935 minute says the room held **nine** carried patterns, with no maker, no date and no accession number for any of them, and it is the first time the number nine has been written down next to the things, and nobody in about a hundred and forty years has asked what it is a count of. **And the Registrar says the sentence that keeps the whole of it out of a god: a clerk of works answered one question and it is the right answer to that one; it is not the answer to what a cord line is doing in a room with nine small things in it that people stop at; and no professional is going to answer that in a store on a Thursday morning, and I would like whoever reads this in four years to know that a professional looked at it and did not claim it.**

## Chapter 0080 — A Covenant Is Not A Miracle (Sun 28 Feb 2025, 15:10 – 16:30, and the night after)

**The practice is seven weeks and six days old, about nine people, the light going off the front window, and a man of thirty-two stands at the west end of about nine feet of floor for fifty minutes and says four sentences, three of which are yes.** The date the case has been missing comes down a telephone from a letting agent who is not coming and would not have come if she had been asked: **a block does not go into programme until the first day of a quarter; block six is in; block seven goes in on the first of April; block eight on the first of July; block nine on the first of October; and after the first of October this programme is finished and there is no more of it in this ward. It is a cycle and it is not a promise and there is nothing in it anybody has given up, and the first of April is about five weeks away, and if somebody wanted a sentence saying those tenancies will be protected until a certain date, I would tell them that is one their landlord has not agreed to, and I would be right, and they would go and find a landlord who had.** Then a man of about sixty-five puts what has actually been achieved in three weeks and a day into a sentence, without being asked, over a kettle: **a date, a boundary, and a document somebody has to keep.** The boundary is rule three. The document is a sheet of A4 in a plastic wallet and it is him. And then: **it isn't a rescue. Nobody in this hall has been rescued. Nobody's rent has stopped, or their deposit improved, or their name back in a field, or their post. Nine blanks on a wire are still nine blanks. A woman of eighty-one is not going back on that street till the middle of April. A postman's round changed on the ninth of February and he does not know it and never will. And if anybody in this hall thinks they've been rescued then I'd like to know their name, because I'd like to talk to them about it, and I'd do it nicely, and I'd do it in here.** And the last thing anybody says in that hall: **it is not a covenant. A covenant is two people and a date, and there is nobody in this ward who can hold anybody to anything — and the thing you have all been trying to write cannot be written, not because it is hard, but because the minute somebody is keeping it, it is not what it is now. It would be the same three rules and there would be a fourth one that nobody wrote, and the fourth one would be *somebody is keeping it*.** And he asks Elias Venn who is going to keep it, and he does not answer, and is told **don't** — *you'd have done the thing you do, gone quiet and made it a moment —* and is told not to say he is not allowed into the building either, **because that is the easiest sentence anybody has said to him in twelve weeks and it is worth nothing and he has been letting it stand in for an answer since October.** And the last image: four miles away a bell that was cast in 1902 under the name of a body that does not exist, with a corrected card under a panel ninety-three days old, **gives no note**, and has given none since four minutes past eleven on **Sunday the thirteenth of December 2024**. **And a man of thirty-two is not the only person in this case who has nothing** — there is a woman of about thirty-four at the end of a road called Broad Street who paid her rent and her tax and her bills and has never had a letter that was not returned, and they are the only two people in this city in exactly the same position and neither of them knows the other exists. The notebook's last line is a question, and it is not the old one: **who keeps it.**

---

## Chapter 0081 — The Determination Day (Sat 6 Mar 2025, 10:20 – 16:10, Northglass)

**A rota on a wall in a plastic sleeve, printed in November, four names and eleven dates, and a determination day at eleven on the sixth of March with S. Tully in the box.** Four people go: the Director, the Registrar, a systems technician of twenty-five, and a man of thirty-two on a witness card, in company, not before eight. The Director says it in a van before they go in: **there is nothing for us to win in that building today, and the second half is that this Museum is the last institution in the chain, and a door mat is not an institution, and a door mat is not in the chain at all, and a door mat is the only thing anybody in this business has ever been able to hand to a person, and that is the whole of the trouble with it.** **Aurelia Roth is not on the rota and S. Tully moved nobody and has not moved a name in nineteen years, and there is one assessor per district per day, and if there had been two of them in the room the case would have got two opinions and a month of nobody being able to say which one the office held.** Then the day's two answers. **There is no object in the Museum's file, because a description is not a classification and a card is a description.** And: **I cannot list a question about a room. Not will not — cannot. A listing needs a question that can go in a form; a matter attaches to a thing; the thing is described in a box on the intake; a determination has to be about something. And there is no form in which the thing a question relates to is a room, and there has never been one, and I have looked twice, and I am the person who would know.** **And what a listing would require: a form, a named person to hold it, a date at the end of it, and somebody willing to be the first person it is used on — and there is no household in this business, and there has not been one since the sixth of December, and nobody has thought to ask whether there is anybody to be had. Nobody in that room is a first person.** At about twenty to twelve, not on the list, a statutory officer asks the requester **whether he has ever had any connection with this office.** He says no, truthfully, and is not lying and is wrong by one box: **box 11, IDENTITY OF REQUESTER, the only box on that intake ever used before 6 February, carries one handwritten entry in nineteen years, in his own hand, dated 6 February 2025 — *VENN, E., confirmed signature of requester in the presence of the assessor* — because she had to be able to say in a determination that the requester was the requester.** And her reason: **a connection is a thing that happens to you. This happened because I asked.** **It is the first record in this city that concerns him that he did not write about himself.** Then the Registrar says the payroll out loud, having asked in advance whether it was S. Tully's sentence and been told it was not: **the only instrument keeping Elias Venn a legal neighbour of Low Ward is a payee field at cost centre M.Q.S. 4 for a post that does not exist, and it is correct, and every step of it is correct and none of the systems has ever asked him.** And nobody in that room describes what is on the table in Low Ward, and the reason is procedural, not sentiment: **a determination is a public document on a public register kept for a very long time, and a description given in this room would be a description in a register in eleven years written by a registrar of a public museum, and that is a hook, and I said the word in November and I am not going to un-say it in Northglass.** The determination issued is five items, is *not determined and cannot be determined*, and is **countersigned: none**, because there is nobody in the building. In the lobby at ten past four: **the answer is a form, and there is no box in this building for one and there is no box in that building for one, and the first person is not in this van.**

## Chapter 0082 — A Nil Return, And A Board (Thu 11 Mar 2025, 09:50 – 16:30)

**A nil return in the words the officer asked for, four numbered lines, two names, handed in by hand at a counter in Morningside at ten past ten with a receipt for it, and it is the first document this case has produced that is a document about nothing.** The decision takes four minutes in the registrar's office with the door shut. The cost is stated before the return is signed: **a party which holds nothing and says so is a party that can be asked to produce nothing for ever after.** The Director tries to take her own sentence out of line four and the Registrar will not let her — *if your sentence is in line four then the return is a policy; if it is not, it is a return* — and the Director puts it back in and says on the record that she did not need four seconds. Two enclosures: the minute of 27 January 2025 and the acknowledgment of 6 February 2025. **And in the corridor, clause six bites for the first time in this batch:** asked whether the schedule of collections should go to the conciliation service as well — **no. She did not ask for it. If the schedule is on her desk on Friday morning then in four years there is a piece of paper in a service in Morningside that this Museum put there and did not have to — and a party that gives an office a third thing it did not ask for stops returning nil and starts supplying.** Then the Board at two o'clock, second floor, ninety minutes, and **the Director's standing conditions of the fifteenth of October framed on the wall behind the chair, four clauses, of which condition two has failed twice and which is ninety feet from a table at which eleven people sat for ninety minutes and did not come up once.** The schedule of collections has about eleven entries; the second is a comparison sheet with a dead man's name on it and **no accession number**; a trustee of about seventy asks one question with three words in it — **is the schedule a record** — and the answer is no: **this Museum keeps records about itself and does not keep records about them.** The Chair refuses to minute it in those words and is right. The Registrar says the cost out loud before the opinion, and the cost is new: **there is no panel for a schedule entry. This building has exactly one cover it has ever been able to offer anybody and it is a piece of A4 and a strip of tired tape, and there is not going to be a second one.** Four to three, the Chair not voting, and the trustee votes with the majority and says he would have liked to be in the minority. At ten past four the Director makes a proposal and it is not adopted: **the minute of the twenty-seventh of January is the most useful thing this building has produced this year and nobody outside it has read it, four people have read it, three of whom work here, and I do not know who it is for, and in nineteen years I have never not known who a document was for.** Noted as a proposal made and not adopted, at her own request, because a Board cannot direct a document to be read. **And it went to Morningside on the Thursday morning of the eleventh, inside the nil return, by hand, because rule 9 says put two documents in an envelope and one of the two was the minute. Nobody arranged that, and by the middle of March there is a copy of it in a building in Morningside that is not in this building's count of itself, and Marchant reads it out in full at the hearing on the eighteenth.** ⚠ *This said Thursday the thirteenth, which is a Saturday on the pinned line, and the notebook entry below it said Friday; the return went on the eleventh at ten past ten, as the chapter's third line and the notebook both have it. Corrected in the Batch 0004 review pass.*

## Chapter 0083 — A Knock (Thu 18 Mar 2025, 10:30 – 11:40, Morningside)

**Nine people and a table and a window onto a yard, and nobody in the room is frightened, which is the first thing to say about the eighteenth of March.** The officer, **Marchant, about fifty-six, nineteen years**, reads the Museum's own minute of 27 January out loud in full at a hearing it created, and says it is the only basis of her jurisdiction. A letting agent of about thirty-nine who was not a party and had not been asked to come says so first: **my client's position is that it has no position**, and reads the programme — nine blocks, a quarterly cycle, first day of each quarter, notices on paper by hand at the property on the earliest date permitted by a person whose name is on the notice, block seven gone in on the first of April, block nine on the first of October and after that no more of it in this ward. Then the question the solicitor asked in February: what does the Museum think the matter is about. **We think the matter is about a knock.** *Say again.* **We think the matter is about a knock.** **He writes it down and does not look up and does not mention that he noticed, and the not-saying takes about two seconds and everybody at that table including the officer knows exactly what it cost him.** Then she says the thing nobody has said. **The matter is not the practice. There is no party in this room who objects to anybody knocking on anybody's door. The matter is about about four hundred and twelve tenancies and a register that does not exist.** Her note, in a file and not on a public register because she chose a note on purpose, in four items — and item four says **the only instrument produced in this matter which has ever reached a household is a knock at a door and about nine names on a table, and it is not a record and it is not admissible and it is the only thing anybody has** — and item two says **there is no forum in this city in which a person may say that a landlord has taken the ability to be recognised as the neighbour of an address, and the reason is not that such a forum has been refused, declined or found wanting, and the reason is that it has never been designed, and the word she has spent nineteen years finding is *forum*.** She gives no date, because a date is the only thing anybody has wanted from her since 6 December and it is not hers to give. At about twenty past eleven, off the list: **is there a household in this matter.** No. **There is no household in this matter and there has not been one since the sixth of December, and everybody in this room knows the reason, and the reason is on a piece of paper in a building on Marsh Lane that says *this file is not a register of persons*.** And last, nobody can answer: the person who keeps the sheet is not in the room and was not asked whether he wanted to be in it, and the officer said that was the correct decision and that it is the reason a matter about four hundred and twelve tenancies is being heard in a room where the only party with no landlord in it is a piece of A4 that is not in the room — **and then said she had never said this in a hearing in nineteen years: nine people at that table did one thing today that was wrong, and not one of them did it on purpose, and every step of it was correct, and there is no form for asking a man whether he wants to be in a room and I have checked and I am the person who would know.**

## Chapter 0084 — Four Lines On A Step (Mon 22 Mar 2025, 15:00 – 16:40, 88 Sillick Lane)

**The practice is eleven weeks old and there are two more people in that hall than there has ever been.** Vera Pilling is not there and will not be until the middle of April, and a man of sixty-five says why in about nine seconds: **she is the only one of us who has paid for this with her body and the rest of us have paid for it with a Tuesday.** A registrar who has never been to Sillick Lane in her life reads four lines on the step at about twenty past three, with the door open, and they promise to say nothing, and a woman with a pushchair asks who it is for — **for a court, in about four years, and the answer will be a person standing on a mat saying a name aloud at a door, and it will be in a transcript and there will be nothing else in the transcript, and I will not be able to improve on it, and I have tried four times.** Then the man with the table decides the handout in about four seconds: **I am not going to hand it to anybody who has not put their hand out, because if I hand it to everybody then in about four days it is a leaflet, and a leaflet makes the building the author, and this Museum's minute says it is not going to be the author of anything about this street.** About four hands go out. He does not count them and says he has counted things at a gate for forty years and is not going to start at sixty-five. **And then, unasked, at ten to four, he asks the room: *do you want to be in this*** — and the answer is allowed to be no and he will not ask why. A man of thirty-two is asked first and says he has never been in it, has never stood on that mat and could not, and that it is a not-yes and he would rather it be accurate than generous. The woman of about fifty says it first and best: **I have been in this since the eighth of January and nobody has ever asked, and I have assumed about four times that somebody somewhere had decided I was in it, and I have never once been able to say who, and I have told myself that not wanting to know was modesty.** And Frank Dunning: **there wasn't one. That is what I have just found out and I found it out at ten to four this afternoon in my own hall with about eleven people in it, and I have been doing this for eleven weeks and nobody asked me and I am the man with the table.** And then the sentence the second half of April is built on: **somebody is going to have to ask us properly. Not you. Not a museum. Not a clipboard. Somebody with a job. And they had better be able to hear a no — because if we had found that out in six weeks instead of eleven, in a room with a landlord's programme in it, one of us would have said yes to something we did not understand and we would have been the reason it went wrong.** The light goes off the front window, the door shuts at ten past.

## Chapter 0085 — The First Of April (Thu 1 Apr 2025, 10:00 – 17:00, a street two hundred yards from the Museum)

**Block seven enters programme, about forty households, notices served by hand at the property on the earliest date permitted, in advance, lawfully, by a person whose name is on the notice. And a man of thirty-two was a mile away in a laundrette, and the chapter is the street and not him.** A letting agent of about thirty-nine knocks from the top of the street down, says *notice* before she says anything else so that nobody is surprised at a door, says the date at the foot of it is the date and she will stand on the step and confirm it, says she is not the landlord, and **writes in the hardback at every door — dates, times, house numbers, about forty of them and about four thousand since 2019 — and refuses it again, kindly, to anybody: not to a museum, not to a council, not to a journalist, not to a woman with a satchel. It is dates and about four thousand visits and my handwriting, and the second it is in somebody else's building it is a list of about four hundred households and a person did it on purpose.** And the street: about forty households, eleven letter boxes, the numbers do not run in order, **and this Museum has printed every one of them a card every year since about 1954 and nobody in this building has ever been down it, and there is no minute, no request, no item-six direction and no doorway on it in any file here.** At about ten past two a registration officer of thirty-one arrives with a satchel for one household, and a woman of about fifty-eight at a door tells her about the woman with the book, and in about nine minutes a doorstep establishes the whole case: **she is not going to be able to tell her whether they can take her name off the roll over a tenancy, and the honest answer is that nobody can, and that she can be on the roll and lose the tenancy in the same week, and those are two offices four miles apart that have never spoken to each other and are both correct.** Then the two systems meet: **a woman with a satchel asks a woman with a book, in those words, whether it is a record, and says *you asked it in the right words, which is more than a museum did*, and gets no** — and then says to her that she has a pad of nine houses on her own pad and there is no list of the ones she cannot get and she has never been able to say that out loud to anybody, and the answer is *then we are the same and I have never met you*, and a letterhead is not introduced because **if I am introduced then in about four years there is a document with two professionals' names on it about one street.** And a woman at a door hands the officer a copy of the notice and she **cannot take it, and her reason is not the reason anybody in this case has been giving for eleven weeks — if I take it I have to write down where I got it, and then it is a document in a council file about your front door, and nobody in that office asked for it, including me** — and she writes a fourth true sentence in a box that has wanted two lines for nine years, about documents, and says she does not want a fifth. And in Ferry Bank on the same afternoon, a woman of seventy-eight is asked for the eighth time since November and does not reply, **because the letter said no reply was required, and a thing with nothing in it is not a refusal and is not going to be recorded as one** — eight requests, seven refusals, the difference is one silence, the board on the ground floor still says seven, it is correct, and in four years somebody will read it and think she said no seven times and said nothing the eighth, **and there is no form for the difference.**

## Chapter 0086 — The Three Answers (Wed 7 – Fri 9 Apr 2025, 15:00 – 17:00, Sillick Lane)

**The residents are asked, properly, by a person with a duty, and they do not all say the same thing.** Nobody asked on behalf of this Museum: a man of sixty-five asked a registration officer of thirty-one on the Monday, and she said yes and told her office, and her office said nothing. **The question is four words — *what do you want this to be* — and he makes her use that one, because I am the man with the table and if somebody asks about me they are asking a man of sixty-five whether his feelings are all right, and they are, and it takes forty minutes.** She asks eight people over three days and writes nothing, and she does not ask a man of fifty-one with a bad chest and writes the omission on the back of her own form with a date. **Wednesday, the money.** A woman of forty-four, first-floor flat on the east side, nineteen years, no gap, wants the money, and says she is owed it by nobody before anybody says it. The Registrar tells her the truth in forty seconds: the money is for people who can show a named employer owed them a wage fifty years ago, she cannot be on the schedule, she is owed something and it is not a wage, there is no fund for what she is owed, **and if a scheme were built for people like her it would be paid by people like the fifty, and that is a thing that has happened in this country about nine times since 1945** — and then: **it is no, and you are owed it, and both of those are the same afternoon.** She does not sit down. **Thursday, the fight.** A postman of about fifty, unasked, in a coat, eleven seconds' thought, and then the only thing anybody has said in this case that is about the future: **four hundred doors a day, same round, same order, the order on a sheet that comes out of a rack on a Monday and is binned in two years; a door that does not answer on a Tuesday answers on a Thursday; a door that has not answered for six weeks has not answered for six weeks — and the instrument is a sequence and not a list, and that scales.** And then he gives the flaw himself, unasked: **a postman is a council employee, what he knows about a household is not evidence, and if I say in a room that a door on a round does not get answered then I am a council officer reporting on a household, and that is the exact thing that has already happened to about nine people in this ward, and it happened with a form, and the form was correct.** He will not write it down. The Registrar, from the back: **it is the first thing anybody has offered this street that could be said out loud in a hearing in about four years without a lawyer.** **Friday, the silence, in a corridor.** A Director comes up the hill and says she is not entitled to ask and comes anyway. **A woman of fifty-four says no in about nine seconds**, gives the same reason as the second time, knows it is the same reason, and then says the moral floor in eleven words — **for a house that no system has got a name for** — and says it is a registration officer's and not hers and that the Director is not going to write it down, **and that is why I said it.** Then she claims the whole of it from a corridor in 1994: the hundred and forty are in a drawer and there is no list of the nine and there never will be, **and you cannot take a list of a thing nobody kept** — and nobody can check the claim and it does not matter, because it is true whichever of them said it first, and the two women who said it have never met. **No to a museum, again. Not you, and not a woman with a satchel, and not a postman, and if you build a form I will not fill it in.** Three answers in one week, and not one of them can be put next to any of the others, and nobody asked them to.

## Chapter 0087 — Vera Pilling Goes Back (Thu 15 Apr 2025, 10:50 – 12:10, Sillick Lane)

**A clinic sheet with four lines and one of them a length of time, twenty-five minutes, and a woman from the clinic who walks her to the pavement because that is the rule and then stands at the end of the road the whole time.** Eighty-one, of number six, forty-one years. **In the clinic since Wednesday the thirteenth of January, which is thirteen weeks and one day; told *this spring* on Tuesday the twenty-sixth of January by a woman of thirty-one with a satchel, and that was fifty-one days after the field cleared, which is seven weeks and two days.** She had stood in the cold on that street on the eighth of December for about an hour and a quarter at about four in the afternoon and somebody noticed, **and nobody in this case has ever asked her about it and nobody is going to now, and the reason is not respect, it is that we do not have a form.** **Three people agree out loud at about twenty to eleven, before it happens, that none of them is going to ask her anything about the case, because one of them was going to want to.** She goes up the east side and stops twice at doors and knocks at neither, each time for about four seconds.
