At eighty: **she knocked, and she waited about nine seconds, and the door opened four inches, and she did not say anything at all for about four seconds, and then she said a man's name — twice, to a person.** It is the name on a comparison sheet of 4 February 2025 in a column headed OCCUPANT (PRIOR), and **she said it first, on 4 January, at that door, and the person who opened it said *Who?*, and nobody has asked her since.** A woman in the doorway says there is a woman living there now and she will be in at half four, and says *I'm not saying you were right about him, I'm saying there's a woman here now.* Then the Registrar asks her to write it down. **No. Twice.** Her reason, in about eleven seconds, and it is the fourth refusal in this case that is about what a document does to a person afterwards: **if your museum puts Walter Naylor on a card about number eighty, then in about four years somebody will come to that door with a clipboard, and the woman in that flat will have to say no about a man she has never heard of, and a no about a dead man is a harder thing to say than a no about a bin. That is the whole of my reason and it is not a moral and I am not being brave — I would do it gladly if there was nobody in that flat.** The Registrar says the cost out loud on a pavement and then says the thing: the card will carry the name and, under it, that it is unsupported beyond a district's retained prior value and that a person who knew the man declined to be recorded — **and the reason is not on it, because the reason is hers and because a reason on a card is a handle** — and it will be wrong on purpose, **and it is the best I can do with a no.** **Elias Venn did not write the name down. He has known it since 4 February and has carried a man's name in his head for eleven weeks and said nothing, and he writes that down instead.**

## Chapter 0088 — Where Has To Be (Fri 16 Apr 2025, 14:00 – 16:30, Morningside)

**A room booked on a form on Wednesday the fourteenth with a purpose on it — *to establish whether a practice requires a location* — and no minute, and four people.** Ivo Pell asks his question of 20 February properly, in three parts, having written it out and not reading from it: **where does a practice have to happen for it to be the same practice; what is the instrument that holds it there; and who is the person who says.** A letting agent answers two parts in about nineteen minutes and refuses the third: **a practice with a shop in it is portable — you can buy the thing it happens on for two pounds, and about nine of them have been sold since the fourth of January, and nobody's job is to know it. A practice with a landlord in it is not, and that is not a moral, it is a fact about what a practice needs. And there is no version of that answer where it comes from me, because the two answers are both true of my client and I do not get to choose which one you use — and if you want it answered then it has to be answered by whoever is going to be wrong about one half of it, and there is exactly one person in this room like that and she knows it.** And the Registrar puts the object-or-place question in one sentence, for the first time in nineteen weeks, in a room with a letting agent in it — and then **refuses it, with a new reason, in about nine seconds: *I am the person who will have to write it down*.** If she answers, then in about four years there is a document in this building with a registrar's initials on it saying what the practice is, in one word, in a public register, and somebody will be able to point at it and say *the museum decided* — **and the museum will not have decided anything; a registrar will have written down the answer to a question she was asked, in a room, on a Friday, and that is exactly the same mechanism as a card, and I have been on both sides of it since November.** Then the answer to parts one and two, which Mara Iles has had since 20 February and did not say out loud until this room, because she did not want to be the woman who tells a man of sixty-five that his hall is not a place: **there is no instrument and there is not supposed to be one. A practice is a permission. Any person may stop at any point and nobody is asked afterwards why. A permission cannot be located — you cannot put a permission in a place and keep it there, because a place holds things and a permission is a thing you are allowed to stop doing, and the person who is allowed to stop is the person who holds it, and they are on a step, not in a building. There is no instrument because a permission is not the kind of thing instruments are for, and there is not going to be one, and there ought not to be one, and I know every instrument there is and not one of them can hold a promise to say nothing.** And the third part: **nobody. There is nobody. And that is the correct answer.** The room produces **four lines of free text on a booking form and no minute at all.** And afterwards: two mats, one of them bought on the Thursday for two pounds by four people who put a pound each into an envelope, **because there is no form for a two-pound mat and nobody was going to put one on a minute** — and at about half past four on a street that is not on a sheet, a mat went down against a wall by somebody who has stood on two of them, and nobody recorded it and nobody has told the man with the table.

## Chapter 0089 — Checked By More Than One Person (Fri 23 Apr 2025, 09:40 – 13:20, the label room)

**A dome camera over a label-room doorway, a card cut to put over the lens with a date on it, no exposure made, and no countersignature required because nothing was made — and Ivo Pell's condition of the first of December working exactly as a man of twenty-five wrote it for a reason none of them could have given in November.** Four people and a records officer of about fifty who has been asked for one thing in six years and has not been thanked, who came in her own time and **refused a bus ticket twice**, and who says the difference between checking whether you kept what you were supposed to keep and checking whether what you kept was right is about eleven years of her working life and she did not know that until she was asked. **Mara Iles does not read the notebook, and the reason is the best decision anybody in this case has made since November and she made it about herself: everything she reads becomes a document in a museum file the moment it is read, and the minute of 27 January says this Museum holds no record of what happens on that street.** He reads all nine pages aloud, in order, for an hour and ten minutes. She brings two things: her own record of issue, nine entries in six years, and a diary and a wall calendar. **Four entries carried a date checkable against a document. Three were correct. One carried a day of the week that was wrong** — `Tue` against the eighteenth of March 2025, which is a Thursday, copied off a firm of solicitors' letter of 19 February that had the date right and the weekday wrong. **And she says it in about eleven seconds and nobody in the room recognises that anything has happened: a date is a fact about the world. A weekday is a fact about whoever typed it. There is no arithmetic in a weekday, and I have never once in six years checked a weekday against a source, and neither has anybody in this room, and I do not know one person in this city who has.** Then, to him and not to the room, in about nine seconds: **if I can be wrong about a day of the week off a wall calendar, then anybody can be wrong about a day of the week in a notebook, on purpose or by accident, and the only way anybody finds out is if a second person reads it — and I would like it noted that nobody thought to ask me for twenty weeks, and it was not because anybody was unkind to me. It is because there is no form for *is there a second person*.** A conciliation service reissues the letter on Wednesday the twenty-eighth with the weekday corrected and a line on its face saying what was wrong, and **the wrong one is not destroyed and the right one is on top of it and both are dated — the first correction at source and versioned in this case, and it is a day of the week, and it is the first of a kind and not the kind that matters.** A partner in Irving Quay telephones at twenty past twelve and does not apologise, and will write into his next letter that a weekday is evidence and that anybody relying on his correspondence should check them, and says **he would not have written that sentence in February.** And a card is cut in four minutes and put on a front desk in a public lobby that names no person and says that a day of the week in a notebook was wrong, and how many entries could be checked, and that the error was found because a second person was asked and not because anybody thought of looking, and **there is no form for a second person, and it is recorded here so that the next person who has a notebook reads this card first.**

## Chapter 0090 — A Boundary With A Date On The End Of It (Sat 24 – Sun 25 Apr 2025)

**A room above a shop, let by the hour, four pound notes and a pound coin in an envelope from about nine people, a key, and nobody's name on the booking — and the shopkeeper took the envelope, gave over the key, and has never been told what the room is for and has not asked.** The day starts at ten with about eleven people and a kettle somebody brought and no Museum and no document and no minute and no form. **At about four o'clock a man of fifty-two, who has a first-floor flat on that street and has had it for about twenty-six years, who has never been on a sheet, who has not been asked a question in three months, and who has watched about nine houses on that street empty in one December, says *I'll write it*, and gives the reason before anybody asks: nobody asked me, that's why I'm going to write it.** He drafts it in about four minutes with a bolt pen out of a woman's bag, on the back of a piece of paper that had been a list of shopping, and nobody reads it while he is writing it. **The first three lines are the rules that have been on a sheet of A4 in a plastic wallet since the fourth of January, and the third has been there since the eighth in a different hand, and nobody in the room mentions that and nobody mentions it afterwards and that is correct.** Then: **it goes on until the first of October. On the first of October we stop, and then we will decide whether to start again. Nobody has to promise us anything and we are not asking anybody to.** Because the first of October is the day the programme is finished in this ward — **a thing with a landlord in it is a document with a date on it, and a thing with a cycle in it is a document with a date on it, and a thing with a date on the end of it that somebody chose is not a document anybody can enforce, and it is the first one anybody in this room has been offered, and it is the only one we are ever going to get.** Then the fourth rule, said by the man with the table before anybody asked him, because he has been waiting since 28 February for somebody to make him: **the fourth rule is *somebody is keeping it*, and I am not going to be the one, and I would have said yes on the fourth of January if anybody had asked and I am not going to be asked now, and a man of sixty-five who keeps a table in a hall is a man with a table in a hall, and the day I stop it stops, and that is the correct arrangement and it is the worst arrangement in the world in that order.** And then, in the last twenty seconds of the four minutes, the drafter reads it back and changes his mind, and the reason is nine words: **nobody asked me.** He has been in the room since ten o'clock, he says, and he has been the only person in it that nobody asked, and both that and not asking are the same mistake and one of them is his. **Nobody is asked to sign anything on the Saturday or the Sunday, and the fifth line on the wall says that nobody signs it and nobody keeps it and it is on the wall.** And then somebody asks about a register, and a man of fifty-two refuses it in about nine seconds, and his reason is new: **a list of people is a list of people and I agree with that. A list of houses is worse, and nobody has said that. There are about nine empty flats on this street and there is nothing anywhere in this city that says what it was — not the district, not the delivery office, not the Museum, not the hundred and forty in a drawer, not a letting agent's book, not the practice sheet.** Sunday morning: the key back, five pounds, the shopkeeper shuts the door and has never asked, and about six people stand on a mat from three o'clock until you cannot read a number off a door, and nobody keeps a list. And four miles away a bell under a panel a hundred and forty-nine days old **gives no note**, and has given none for one hundred and thirty-three days, and **no bell is on a step and nobody has ever asked the two of them about each other and nobody is going to.** The last image is six people owed nothing and four refusals in three weeks: **I was the only person in this case who had nothing on the sixth of December. I am not now.** And the last line is the question, and it is louder than it was on the sixth of February: **who keeps it.**

---

## Chapter 0091 — The Fourth Rule (Mon 3 May 2025)

A room above a shop in Low Ward, let again by the same shopkeeper on the same terms, **four pound notes and a pound coin in an envelope and no name on the booking, and the shopkeeper has now let this room three times and has asked nothing three times and is not going to ask on the fourth, and that arrangement is correct.** The boundary has been on the wall nine days and nobody has signed it. **A registration officer of thirty-one with a satchel, who asked to come and told her office and whose office said nothing, which is what her office does**, announces she is going to put her hand on the sheet and take it off again, and asks for the conversation first. She then fails the fourth rule — *somebody is keeping it* — **for a reason that is not a refusal and is not a favour**: her office holds a record, a record of who is on a street is a list, twenty-five years of who is on a street with a person's name against every line retrievable by anybody in the council who has a reason and by anybody else who can find a reason, **about four hundred people, and that is not a guess, and she has not counted and is not going to count, and the form is not hers after the twenty-fifth of January.** She had the form the whole nine days and the form did not care what she intended it for.

The man of fifty-two gives the case's shape in about nine seconds: **you cannot put a duty in a piece of paper; a piece of paper holds a name against a date and does not hold the bit in the middle, which is the part where the person is allowed to stop; and the day somebody's name is against that sheet, then the day she stops, somebody is going to ask her why, not out of malice, because a name and a date is a thing that wants finishing.** And then: **a rota cannot hold a permission — you cannot scale a permission; a duty anybody can walk away from without filling in a box is a suggestion, and a suggestion with a list of names on it is the worst kind of list there is, because it looks like somebody is in it.**

**And the room makes a rota anyway, in about half an hour, on the back of a piece of A4, in the same bolt pen, two columns — who is on the mat, and who has the kettle — about nine lines, the nine doors in the order the knock goes in, no surnames, two of the nine wrote nothing and said so, no third column, and it took four seconds to decide and nine minutes to explain and the woman of fifty-four explained it and had not been asked to.** Frank Dunning says his sentence a third time and concedes that it is three. The registration officer then refuses to hand a form to a museum and asks nobody else to, **and she says the reason out loud: a record of that street in that building, in a file, is a list of people in a building that keeps records, and in four years there is a person with a job who has never met any of us whose whole function is that file** — and she says she is not going to be the one who asks, which is rule three applied to a form, by a person with a duty, for free, and nobody asked her to. She will write to her office on Tuesday; the letter will say a form is not available for the fourth rule and will not say why. **At about five the man of fifty-two puts a mark against his own line on the rota, which is not a signature and is not anybody's permission, and nobody wrote down what it was.** Ends: **nobody is keeping it, a rota is on a table, and who keeps it now has four answers that still do not go together.**

## Chapter 0092 — A Signature Is A Hook (Thu 13 May 2025)

A hall at 88 Sillick Lane, two plastic wallets, and the second wallet put there on Monday by the man of fifty-two, who copied the wall out in the same bolt pen and **had not signed it and said he was not going to be asked to sign it. Nobody had asked him.**

**Mara Iles comes on her own, as a registrar and not as the Museum, in a doorway with her coat still on, and says the cost out loud before she asks the question, because in April a man of fifty-two wrote a document nobody had asked him for and changed his mind at the end of it, and she would like the room to know she knows that happened.** The cost: **a name against a date on a piece of paper is the strongest thing in this country and the only instrument in this case that has ever caught anything — that is not a figure of speech, it is four or five separate things that have happened in twenty-two weeks and every one of them worked — and the same property is a hook.** She asks nine times, and says two things first: **this sheet says on the last line that nobody signs it, and I am asking you to sign it anyway, and the last line is on the sheet and I am not taking it off, and if you sign it the last line is wrong and it will stay wrong on the paper**; and **you may say no, and I will not ask why, and I will not write it down, and I will not count it, and if you say no there is nothing anywhere that says you were asked and nothing anywhere that says you said no.** She gives that away in about nine seconds and will not be thanked, because it is the third rule and it is not hers.

**Mavis Ludlow, 61, of number twenty-two, thirty-one years in that flat and before it the one above it with her aunt, a cook at St Anselm's until 2019, with the knee, reads the whole thing twice on the Monday and will not let anybody read it out loud.** She asks what happens to the signature afterwards and is told in about nine seconds, without a pause and without any attempt to make it better: **in about four years it will be in a file in a room in a building, it will not be in this hall, nobody will be able to take it back, and I cannot tell you which building and I would not tell you if I could, because if you knew the building you would start being careful about this and the whole point of it is that you are not careful about it.** She calls that the worst thing anybody has said to her in about two years, and signs anyway, for the reason she gives without being asked and which the registrar says is the best anybody has given in twenty-two weeks: **in December a letter came to number twenty-two addressed to the house, it knows where she lives and it does not know who she is, and she has lived there for thirty-one years and there is not one piece of paper in this city with her name on it and that address on it. She is owed a paper. That is all of it.**

Frank Dunning says the fourth rule and the room counts it. **And then somebody does the sum out loud.** Three of the nine signed. One refusal was spoken and not written. Four of nine accounted for, five not, and the registrar says in about nine seconds that she is not going to account for the five, **because either she would be writing them down or she would be asking people in front of each other, and that is the same instrument with the manners taken off** — a registration officer spent nine days at the end of April finding out there is nowhere for it to go that is not a list, and Mara has known it since her first week.

**The man of fifty-two then says the sentence the case has been walking toward since the fourth of December, in about eleven seconds: you are all treating that as a trap and it is not a trap, it is the only thing that works. Signing is not agreeing.** A signature on that sheet does not mean she agrees with it; it means that on 13 May 2025 a person called Mavis Ludlow was in that hall and did a thing at number twenty-two, and that paper is now the only record in this city of either, **and the day somebody comes with a clipboard — in four years, in nine — they cannot come to my door about a rumour, because there is a piece of paper with her name on it, and a piece of paper with her name on it is a thing you have to answer. A thing you have to answer is worth having. I have watched about nine people have nothing to answer with.** The woman of fifty-four, refused three times and not asked a fourth time, says the not-asking is the entire difference between that building and every other institution in this city, and has been waiting since 9 April to see whether anybody would do it again.

**And then the registrar says the uncomfortable half before anybody goes home: a piece of A4 in a plastic wallet is not a record, it is the possibility of a record, three names on it will not stop the nine blanks, and anybody who tells this street it has made a register has not been in her office. A field goes into a system — custodian, retention, disclosure rule, about four hundred people who can read it. A piece of A4 goes in a drawer.** Ends: **a plastic wallet, in a hall, in a drawer that is not locked, on a table under a newel post.**

## Chapter 0093 — The Practice In A Public Room (Sat 15 May 2025)

**A meeting room over a library, up about fourteen stairs, holds about thirty standing or eleven sitting. The chapter is told from inside the room, and the declaration is on the page in its second paragraph: a man of thirty-two was about four miles away, he did not come, nobody in that room is him, and the reason is the whole of it, which is that the two people who stopped this afternoon are not going to be named.** Booked in writing on Wednesday the fifth and used on the fifteenth, **and the form has one box and the box is for the day it is used, so the woman with the ring of keys wrote both dates out herself, and that form is the plainest object in the case and nobody will ever see it: a thing that cannot be held past six o'clock is the only thing anybody has got, and it is not a virtue, and it is the closest a practice gets to a place.** Mr Perrin has the keys and finishes at half past five and has never once had to wait in a doorway.

They take all of the library's own tape, because the tape and the pins are the library's and a pin left in that wall at half past five becomes a number and a job for somebody in a building on a hill. **The copy goes up crooked at about ten past three with four drawing pins and nobody straightens it, and it comes off at about twenty to six in one piece and leaves a clean rectangle, and the woman with the ring of keys says that will come off and it does, in about nine seconds, with a bit of elbow grease, and she keeps the bit in a drawer and has had to twice in about two years.**

**The man of fifty-two says the sentence the room was got for, before anybody asks him, because nobody ever asks him: a room like this is the closest this can get to a place without turning into a programme — not a museum, because a museum has a director and a schedule and a rope a hundred and forty years old, and the day this is in a building with a collection it is a programme and a programme has a keeper and a keeper is a person with a job; and not Frank's hall, because a hall a man owns is a man's hall.** It is a room with a landlord in it and a date at the end of it, the date is six o'clock this evening, and at six we go, **and that is the only thing about this room that is any good and it is a thing that is no good by half past six.**

The practice runs from three o'clock. **Nine households, about fourteen doors, three of the nine are two doors, the order is the order the knock goes in and is in nobody's system, and the rota has two columns and about a fortnight of pencil ticks in Frank Dunning's hand, which is not a record and everybody who has held that wallet knows it.** Mavis Ludlow knocks at four doors and says her own name at three of them, which she had not done the week before. The woman of forty-four says the thing about the money again, on a doorstep, in about the same words as in April. **Vera Pilling goes up the east side on her own, knocks at one door, says a man's name at it, somebody answers, she says nothing else, and nobody in that street asked her a single question about any of it and about four of them noticed.** At about half past four two people stop — one goes home, one says *I'll see you next week* — **and nobody asks either of them why, and it takes the room about nine seconds not to ask.**

They stop at about twenty to six **because the numbers on the doors on that stretch stop being readable at about ten to six in the last week of May in Low Ward, and the second rule is the light and not a clock, and the man of fifty-two said the time out loud and nobody argued with him about it.** At six Mr Perrin stands in the doorway, looks at the noticeboard and the corner, says nothing, is told *it's all right, it's come off*, says *right*, and goes away. **And then the woman with the ring of keys asks the object-or-place question out loud, in eleven words, at about five to six, with the tape in her hand, because she has to wind the alarm off at six: *Is this a thing or is it a place. Because I have to wind the alarm off at six and I would like to know whether I am winding it off for a thing or for a place.*** Nobody answers for about nine seconds. The answer is still no, and the man of fifty-two says he has had a month to think about it and has got no nearer than on the fourteenth of January, and he is fifty-two and he is not going to pretend. **It is the first time anybody has asked it with a reason that is not a moral, and it is the fourth refusal in nineteen weeks, and every one of the four was by somebody with a duty.**

## Chapter 0094 — What It Gave, And What It Did Not (Sun 16 May 2025)

**The acceptance, and it is the smallest event in the manuscript.** A room let again, five people, a kettle, and the boundary on the wall. The man of fifty-two reads all five lines out loud to himself standing, in about eleven seconds, and says he does that every week and has started doing it in front of people so that somebody will notice him doing it. **At about half past four on Sunday the sixteenth of May somebody knocks once on the door of the room.** The door is on a landing at the top of about fourteen stairs; the landing has one other door, the flat the shop lets separately, and the flat was empty because the shopkeeper told the man of fifty-two on the Friday that it would be empty and for nothing else. **Nobody opened the door for about nine minutes. The man of fifty-two said, in a voice that was not raised, *that'll be the door, then*, and nobody answered him, and the door was never opened at any point that afternoon or evening, and nobody has ever asked the shopkeeper about it, and the shopkeeper was on the ground floor with a radio on.**

**Two of five heard it and the other three did not say.** The man of fifty-two said *I heard that*, at about nine minutes past four, to nobody in particular. Elias Venn said *I heard it*. Nobody else spoke, and the woman of about fifty from the sheet said **"I did not,"** in a flat voice, **and that was the most useful thing anybody said in that room all day, because it meant there were two of them and not five, and she had made it two by answering.**

**And then the man of fifty-two tells Elias Venn to do less, and it is the only time in twenty-three weeks that anybody in this case has told him to do less: do not write that down. You write it down, then it is a thing that happened, and in a fortnight you will read it and it will be a thing you were glad about, and in about four years somebody will come to you with a clipboard and ask you to play it back, and you will be fifty and you will not be able to tell the difference between what you heard and what you wanted, and there will be nobody in this city who can help you, because you will be the only person in the room and everybody else will have been in the room for nine minutes on a Sunday.** He has had about nine knocks at his own door he could not account for and every one turned out to be a person with a parcel, **and a thing you cannot check is worth nothing and a thing you can check is worth everything, and that is the entire finding of this case and it took you twenty-three weeks and a woman of about fifty with a wall calendar to say it, and I am saying it again with the same words because I am not a registrar and I do not have to say it well.** Elias obeys a man of fifty-two for the first time in the case and does not think the man knows that is the first time. **The record he writes is the only page in that notebook with nothing on it except the fact that something was not written: no description of a sound, no adjective, no time to the second, no note about what it might have been, the page not torn out, and it is the only entry in twenty-three weeks he has written for another person to be angry about.**

**And then nobody asked what it was, and the chapter states the whole of the case's position as a list of things that did not happen.** Nothing came back. The nine empty flats are still empty and nothing anywhere says what they were. The about four hundred and twelve tenancies are still in a programme that finishes in this ward after the first of October. About four hundred doors a week in a fixed order is still not a record. **Nothing was restored** — no tenancy, no name, no card, no field, no knock answered at a door that stopped answering in December; the woman of forty-four is still owed money by nobody; the man of about fifty still has a round he is not on; Vera Pilling is still on a street she was kept off for thirteen weeks. **Four miles away a bell cast in 1902 under the name of a body that does not exist has said nothing for a hundred and fifty-four days, the fracture in its shoulder is open under the staple and is not going to close, the card is under a panel a hundred and seventy days old and the panel is the requester's and the requester will not be asked again, Annexe D is permanent, Annexe E is in force and stricter than condition four, and condition two failed on the twenty-third and twenty-ninth of December and has not been amended and is not going to be lifted by anything that happened in Low Ward this afternoon.**

The man of fifty-two then says what he thinks happened and what he thinks it was not, carefully, as a man putting down a thing he is not sure of: **I do not know that anything happened. I know that somebody knocked and that I heard it and that you heard it, and that nobody said a name, and rule one has two halves and we have had the first half for four months and the second half has been the whole of this case since the sixth of December. It is not a yes. It is that whatever this is, it has stopped at that door. It has come up the stairs and it has not come in, and there is a date on the end of that paper and it did not come in on the other side of it, and I do not know that it knows about the paper and I am not going to stand in a room and tell anybody that it does.** And then the other half: **a practice is a permission, and a permission is not asking for anything; if you build a thing in a building four miles away that expects a knock to be answered, then in about four years you will have built a programme, and a programme has a keeper, and we did all of that in April and it took a woman nine days and a form to find out the answer was no.**

**The paper comes off the wall at about half past five because the room is let until six and the woman of about fifty from the sheet is the person who has always taken it down and has never once been asked to and has never once said she would.** The fifth line is on the original and has been since 24 April; **the copy in the hall has it too, and the line is now wrong on that copy on purpose, because a registrar said out loud she was not going to take it off, and it is staying wrong on the paper.** About six people then go out from three o'clock, which was too late, and everybody knew it was too late, and they do about four doors in half an hour in the dark, **and the second rule is the light and not a clock, and Frank Dunning says so, and they stop.** Ends on the notebook: **I have noticed something about myself this afternoon and I am not going to call it anything yet, and I want that written down in the same breath as the thing I have noticed, and not in a separate line, and I have noticed that I keep wanting to write it in a separate line.**

## Chapter 0095 — Nine Flats, And Nothing That Says What They Were (Thu 20 May 2025)

**The list-of-houses question comes back, and the first time anybody has asked anybody a question in that room that anybody in the room was not there to answer.** A records officer of about fifty comes forty miles on her own time, refuses nothing because nobody offers her anything, and says the useful thing first: **a list of about nine addresses and the years that people were living in them is not a list of people. It is a list of houses. That is the whole of the difference and it is the only difference and it is worth more than everything else anybody has put on a table in twenty-three weeks, and it is the first thing this case has ever been able to offer a street.** She attributes the eleven-word form to a woman in a corridor in a building on a hill and says it is a registration officer's and not hers, and says the answer is not a form and never was.

**And then she says what it costs, and nobody volunteers, and the man with the notebook does not put his hand up and does not offer: it costs somebody their post, and the somebody is her.** The column is the district's own retained value of a field it has already overwritten, **carried on the front sheet of every morning's supplied-file archive, and the archive is a binder of every morning from the first of November to the nineteenth of January, and after that date there is no morning and no sheet and nothing.** It is retained because if a delivery fails and the field is empty somebody has to know what was in the field when the item went out, **and no human being has ever looked at it until she looked at it.** Used for anything else it is used outside its purpose, and the officer who uses it outside her purpose is the officer who has to explain it, and she has been asked once in six years and both times the request came from this street. **She would like it recorded that she said it, and that it is worth a great deal that a person in this city with a duty has said out loud that the answer is a list of houses and not a list of people, and that it will still be true in nine months when none of us are in this room, and nobody can take that off her.**

**And then she offers something anyway.** There is a column headed **OCCUPANT (PRIOR)**, the district's retained value of the field, about nine rows for that street, every one with a name, **and it is the only place anybody has ever written down who used to live at those addresses, and she has not been asked for it and has not been told she may not have it, and she is telling the room it exists and is not going to send it.** Mara Iles says the cost first, in about nine seconds, and refuses it: **if it comes into this building it is in a museum file, and a museum file is a list of people in a building that keeps records, and I told that room on the third of May that that is the one thing I will not do** — nine names in a museum is nine names a person with a job will have to look at one day for no reason anybody can give them. She refuses it again for the council, and then gives the worst sentence she has said in this case: **it is in my own building on Marsh Lane in a binder that stops on the nineteenth of January, and after that there is nothing, and nobody has ever looked at it, and that is the correct place for it and I know that.** The records officer nods once, asks that it be recorded that she offered it and it was refused, that she is not offended, and that she would make the same offer in four years to somebody else if she is still in post, **because the day somebody builds a form for this is the day it should be asked for and not before.** Clause six biting the person who most wanted to be helpful, and she brought it to the table herself.

**A letting agent of about thirty-nine says the only checkable thing anybody says all afternoon, in nineteen seconds, on her own time, with the book shut.** She has refused the book seven times and will not say an eighth. **For any household in blocks one to six, the firm will confirm in writing the date its notice was served and the date of the term end, on production of the notice, or any part of it, or a description of the notice. It is free. It takes four days. It has been available since the eleventh of February and her own firm has never mentioned it to a tenant. About four of the nine addresses are in blocks one to six.** The other five are not in a block, or the block has gone, or there was no notice, and she is not going to invent a date to fill a gap in that street. And then the thing nobody is going to like: **a notice is a document that says a landlord has decided something about a house. It is the one piece of paper any of you have that says you were there, and it went to the house, and the house stopped being anybody's, and the notice went with it. That is not a system failure. That is a letter in a box.**

**And then the list is made, in about fifty minutes, out of nine drawers, and it is the first true thing anybody has put on that street: every household on that street has had a card from a public museum through the door every Christmas since about 1954, a printed card about an exhibition, with a year on it and the address on it and never once a name, and about nine households still had theirs in a drawer, and nobody had ever compared two of them and nobody's job is to compare two of them.** Two columns, address and years, the years said out loud by the person who kept the card and written down by the man of fifty-two in his own hand, nobody reading them back. **No third column and no name, and when the man of thirty-two says the word *name* out loud at about ten to five the woman of fifty-four says no in about four seconds and does not give a reason and does not have to.** Two people stopped while it was being made; one kept one card and threw the rest away and would not guess at years, one said nothing and was not asked, **and the gap is in the list and nobody filled it in.** The letting agent is told about the cards in about eleven seconds and concedes one half of what she said in four sentences and holds the other half, **and the book is still shut.**

**The man of fifty-two says his no out loud in the same room on the same afternoon and does not soften it: a list of houses is worse than a list of people. About nine flats on the east side of this road have been empty since December and before December there were people in all of them, and the only trace of any of them is a field that got empty and a door that did not open, and that is still true with the cards in a biscuit tin, because what has been made is not a record of anybody. It is a record that some rooms were warm.** And both halves anyway: it is the first thing anybody has given this street, the two are both true, and he has been trying since January to get rid of one of them and cannot. **And then the man of thirty-two writes the years down in a room with about nine people in it, in about four minutes, having read the third line of the first page of that notebook in front of them while they were talking. He wrote down nine facts a person gave him in a room, and it is not a file and not a record and nobody will ever be able to check it, and the only reason he did it is that he has been in this case for twenty-three weeks looking for a record and there was one in the room and it was going home in somebody's coat.**

## Chapter 0096 — A New Location, And It Is Not A Win (Thu 27 May 2025)

**Four pages with a landlord's name on it and a date at the end of the term, put in front of nine households by a housing officer of about fifty-eight who has a duty, who asked each of them separately, and who told all nine before she asked any of them that the answer was allowed to be no.** The building is **Cauldwell Buildings**, off Low Ward: four flats on the first floor, one empty since December and two since about the middle of April, the owner in the ground-floor flat, letting the four of them weekly since about 2009, sixty-eight, her name on the fourth page. **It is a commercial arrangement at the weekly rent she has been charging, with no discount, no priority and nothing in it for anybody except that she has three empty flats and they have nowhere in this ward to be on the first of October. Four flats and about nine households that have nowhere to be, and she has not made an arithmetic error and is not going to and is not going to work out which four** — she will ask nine people, put the answers on a piece of paper, and everybody in the room will see the answers at the same time and nobody will be picked.

**The term is six months, from 1 June to 30 November, with a rent in pounds for each of the four flats, a weekly frequency, and a name against both.** Page three: **any one resident of the group may end the group's occupation on fourteen days' notice without affecting the occupation of the others** — not usual, asked for by the officer, agreed in about four seconds, and she says why: she does not want a group of nine that cannot leave, because a group of nine that cannot leave is a group of nine that stops paying in April. Page two, at Mara Iles's request, and she did not want to: **the owner is not a party to any practice, arrangement, custom or agreement between residents, and the residents are not parties to anything the owner does in the building** — not usual at all, and it can only be that if it is written down now, **because it is the only way a landlord is ever protected from a street.**

**Nine people are asked nine times, in a corridor on the first floor at Cauldwell Buildings, in about four minutes each, the same way nine times, and the officer writes down what she is asked to write down and asks first whether she might: your address will be on a form in an office and the form is a record, and you can say no to the writing as well as no to the offer, and she will not ask why twice.** Seven yes, two no, **and both of the two said yes to the writing.** One gives a four-sentence reason: **she has paid rent in this ward for nineteen years and she is not going to live in a building where four flats are shared between seven households and the rent is weekly, because a weekly rent can be stopped in a week and a monthly one cannot, and she would rather be somewhere she can be turned out of in a month than somewhere she can be turned out of on a Friday.** The second gives no reason and the officer writes **DECLINED — NO REASON GIVEN** and reads it back and the woman says it is right.

**And a registrar of twenty-eight stood in that corridor and watched two refusals be written down and said the thing nobody else in that building was going to say: that is the first refusal in this case that has ever been written down as a refusal.** There is a board in a flat in Ferry Bank with seven on it and eight requests and a woman of seventy-eight who says a thing with nothing in it is not a refusal, and she is right, **and the reason it is true for her is that nobody with a duty ever asked her on a form. The difference between that woman and the two in this corridor is a box** — a box and a duty, and somebody prepared to be told no and to write down that they were told no, **and that is not a small thing and it is not a victory and I want it said before somebody in this corridor starts calling it one.**

**It is not good news and it is not bad news and it is the only thing anybody in twenty-four weeks has been able to put in front of nine people and have all nine of them see at once.** It is a worse answer to a worse question and everybody in the room says so: about nine flats empty and a piece of A4 in nine hands with two gaps in it, against a building where a room is somebody's landlord's; four flats, seven households, and a woman who would rather be able to be turned out in a month than on a Friday, **and she is right, and it is not a small thing to be right about.** **The money is not answered by it and nobody has said it is and the man of fifty-two is not going to let it get answered by it: the woman of forty-four says she has been owed money by nobody since 2006, that this does not change that and does not pay for it, and that anybody who thinks four flats and a weekly rent answers nineteen years of rent has misunderstood her, and she would rather say that now than in November.** It means seven of them are not scattered across four miles in October, and that is all it does, and all it does is worth having.

**A registrar says the two dates out loud: the boundary on the wall in a room above a shop goes until the first of October because a man of fifty-two chose that day and it is not a covenant and nobody has signed it; the tenancy in Cauldwell Buildings ends on the thirtieth of November because the owner of freehold chose that day and it is a contract and four people will sign it; the two dates are sixty days apart and neither of them is the date anything ends, and anybody who says this arrangement resolves anything is wrong.** A woman of about fifty-four says one sentence — the arrangement is correct and the worst one anybody has been offered in four months, in that order — and will not say where she got it from, and nobody asks.

**And then the man of thirty-two is asked to help and says no.** The housing officer asks him properly, on her own initiative, with no form and nobody writing him down. **No. Because the day I am in that building on the first of June, then in about four years there is a man in a room who can say the practice at Sillick Lane was in Cauldwell Buildings on the first of June with a man of thirty-two in it, and he will be right, and I will have put him there. And because seven households are being asked to sign a contract with a landlord and one of the things that has kept this case honest for twenty-four weeks is that nobody in it has ever had a landlord. Put me in a building with a landlord and I am a man with a landlord, and in four years that is a handle and not a witness.** **It is the sixth thing he has said no to in twenty-four weeks and the first one offered kindly, and Mara Iles says it is the sixth time and every one was right, and writes nothing down, because the Registrar of a museum is not anybody's housing officer and there is no box for it.** Ends: **nobody has been given a home**, and the moral floor was heard seven days earlier in a room above a shop and **did not make the offer better.**

## Chapter 0097 — From The Bottom Of A Card (Wed 19 May – Fri 28 May 2025)

**The schedule of collections is the one document in the Museum that a member of the public may ask for, anybody may ask, no charge, a form at the front desk with boxes for the requester's name and address, the date, and what they want to see.** It is filled in on Wednesday the nineteenth of May by a woman who is not in this case and has not been named, and her reason is in the last box: **she is writing a history of the ward and would like to know what the ward's own museum thought was worth recording — a better reason than any professional in this case has given for asking this building for anything in twenty-three weeks.**

Mara Iles hands it to a Director and says so in the first line: **I am not going to pretend to anybody that what I am going to ask her for is a favour.** She asks for a note in the schedule **at the entry for plinth thirty-four**, saying that the maker's line on the public card for that object was struck through and dated on 25 February 2025, that the name struck through is the name of no body, and that the correction is beneath a requester's panel this Museum has no standing to remove — **and she says in advance that a note in that schedule is a thing she cannot take back, because every word she puts in it is a word she is putting into a stranger's hands on purpose, and that is the only door in that building that does not lock.** **And the rest of it: the correction is a document that nobody in the world can read. It is under a panel, and has been since the twenty-seventh of November, a hundred and seventy-three days, the panel is the requester's, the requester was asked about it in a public room on the twenty-first of January and declined in a public room on the same afternoon, and there is no standing anywhere in this building or in this county to take it off. And I am the person who will have to write it down, and I have said that sentence out loud three times in two months and I am about to say it a fourth time and it is the reason I am nervous.**

**Anja Ward reads it twice, says no to about a third of it, and gives the rest.** *Untraceable* goes, **because we did not look, and a note in a public document that says a name is untraceable is a statement about a search that was not made. We looked for a body. We did not look for a person who typed it.** It becomes *the name of no body known to this Museum*. *Removed at the request of a member of the public* goes, **because it is not true. It was taped on by her, and it stays because she asked, and those are different and one of them is a favour and the other is a refusal and I will not have a favour in a public document wearing a refusal's clothes.** What stays is the ugly part: the card has carried the name since accession, this Museum has been aware of the fault since 30 December 2024 and did nothing about it for seven weeks, **and if I leave it out then this note is a public document that is tidier than the truth, and I have sat in a boardroom for nineteen years watching a building get into trouble by being tidier than the truth.** And a fourth thing is deliberately left out: **the requester of the panel must not become part of this Museum's record of itself, and she has refused this building three times and the fourth thing that happened in this case was that this building did not ask her a fourth time.**

**On Friday the twenty-eighth of May at about one o'clock the Hall of Former Faiths is open from ten until five, the doors propped, about a hundred and twenty people through the front door between ten and four, about forty into the Hall. The mat is on the floor on its plastic sheet inside the cord line with a card on a wire stand that says *no name available* in a finisher's own hand, and there is a bell in a case at the east end on plinth thirty-four with a staple repair in its shoulder and the fracture open under the staple, and on the front of that case a sheet of A4 at a slight angle with tape that has gone the colour of weak tea.** The woman who filled in the form is in the room with a notebook, was not asked to come, and came. Mara Iles stands at the east end and reads the entry out of the schedule from the top at the volume she uses in a hearing, and the entry is correct in every line. Then she reads the note:

> **PLINTH 34. Note added 28 May 2025.** The maker's line on the public card for this object has been struck through and dated 25 February 2025. **The name that was struck through is *Municipal Works*, which is the name of no body known to this Museum, and it is the only name that has ever been on that line, and it has been on that card since the object was accessioned.** This Museum has been aware of the fault since 30 December 2024. The corrected card is beneath a panel affixed to the case. The panel is the requester's. This Museum has no standing to remove a requester's panel and has not attempted to. No photograph of the correction has been made and none will be. A finding card was cut on 25 February 2025 and is held at the front desk. It names no person. — And, last: the schedule also contains at its second entry an item that is not an object, dated 4 February 2025, not withdrawn.

**Nobody touched the panel. Nobody peeled the tape. The card underneath was not read by anybody, including by the registrar, including by the Director, and the two of them were standing six inches from it, and it is the most carefully kept document in this building.** The stranger asked whether she could photograph the panel and is told that a card on a public exhibit is not collections material for the building's own rule, that photographs of the room are fine, and that the person in the case underneath the panel is not to be photographed. The finding card is on the front desk in a plastic sleeve and names nobody, because a name on a card in a public room is a hook, and the woman who cut it has cut about four hundred cards in this room.

**And then in a corridor at about twenty past two a woman of fifty-four says the sentence in eleven words and nobody claims it: it is that a hole in a card lets whoever comes next fill it in.** Mara Iles starts to give it to a label-room finisher and stops herself: **No. That sentence was said in a corridor in this city in 1994 and by a registration officer on a doorstep in February and by that woman on the ninth of April, and I have said it in my own office to a colleague twice, and I am not going to claim it and I am not going to give it away.** And the rest of it: **you have put a hole in a card with a date in it, and that is the only thing anybody in this case has done about a false name in twenty-three weeks. A hole with a date in it is a hole somebody made on the twenty-fifth of February 2025. A hole with no date in it is a hole anybody can walk into, and you have put the date on it, and the date is the only thing you have that I did not already say in a corridor in 1994.**

**And the hand that wrote the fourth line is not named, and that is a decision made on the Wednesday afternoon in about four seconds.** There is a hand on the 1902 index card and a day book for the week of the casting with two entries for the label room, **and neither has been read by anybody since February because there has been no reason to read them, and if you read them this afternoon you will either find a name or you will not, and if you find a name it is a name of somebody who was nineteen in 1902 and is dead, and it will be in a public document in four years, and a person will be able to point at it and say that the museum decided that a man was a liar.** So it is not being read today, and if it is ever read it will be read by a registrar on a stated day with a minute, and not in a public room on a Friday, **and the finding will be recorded either way including the null.**

## Chapter 0098 — A Buyer, And It Is A Document (Wed 26 – Sat 29 May 2025)

**A letter comes in on Wednesday the twenty-sixth of May at about eleven in the morning in a padded envelope, four pages, from a firm of solicitors asking the Museum to confirm nine facts, and the ninth is the only one that matters.** The letterhead is **Rackham Searle, third floor, a building on a street in this city called Lantern Row** — a street that has been called that a good deal longer than the society has, from which the society took its name, with about nine firms on it, **and anybody who has ever told the writer the two are the same thing has been told so by somebody who wanted a connection there.** The nine questions: that the Museum held the Low Ward series continuously since 1961 · that it has no record of the removal of household effects from any address · that it holds no record of the occupancy of any address, in any period, in any register, roll or series · that it has never made and does not hold any inspection of any dwelling · that **no claim to any item removed from the said addresses between 1 December 2024 and 31 March 2025 is known to the Museum.** Two enclosures.

**The client is Dorothy Marsden, 61, of a first-floor suite on the same street as her solicitors.** Instructed in January 2025. Nine acquisitions between 15 December 2024 and 9 March 2025, at nine addresses, **because a purchaser of household effects who cannot show good title is a purchaser who will be asked about it in about four years by somebody with a clipboard.** The first enclosure is a printed schedule headed with the name of **the Lantern Society, founded in this city, a private network that buys and sells objects with a pattern in them and what their owners left behind them, with a members' list and a screening requirement and a countersignature.** Five columns: **ADDRESS · DESCRIPTION OF EFFECTS · DATE ACQUIRED · VENDOR'S TITLE · TRACEABLE PRIOR OCCUPANT.** The first three are filled in. The fourth is filled in nine times with the same eleven words. **The fifth column is filled in once, in the fourth row, and the entry in it was a name, and beside the name in the margin the word *ESTABLISHED*, and underneath the name in a different hand a line that read *NOT PURSUED*.** At the foot, a box: **SCREENED AND SIGNED BY MEMBER ______ · COUNTERSIGNED BY MEMBER ______** — the first blank has Dorothy Marsden in a hand that was not the hand of the table, **and the second has a four-digit number, and there is no name against it anywhere in the enclosure.**

**And the reason is on the sheet, in the letter, in nine sentences, and it is legible, and it is not the reason anybody would expect: my client's practice is to acquire only at addresses at which no traceable prior occupant can be established, and not to pursue an address at which one can. She has instructed me to say so plainly rather than leave it to be inferred. An object with a family behind it carries its history with it, the family will surface, the history will be disputed, and the object will be unavailable to her for the rest of her life. An object from an address with no traceable prior occupant carries no such attachment. My client regards this as ordinary care for the vendor's privacy and not as anything else.** Mara Iles reads it twice: **that is the whole of it in nine sentences, and it is not a lie and it is not a euphemism and it is not a code. She has written down, in a letter to a museum, a rule that says: I only buy where nobody can be found.** It is a preference, and a preference about privacy, and **the sentence *not to pursue an address at which one can be established* is the sentence of a careful person and I mean that as a description and not as a compliment.**

**The reply goes out by post on the Friday morning, and it is the only document in this case that is a description of a hole.** She reads the cost out loud to the Director before she writes a word: **every one of the nine answers is true and the reason every one of them is true is the same reason, and the reason is on page one of that letter and not on page four of mine, and a person reading my reply in about four years is going to see a museum that knows nothing, and they are going to be right, and the letter that proves the museum knows nothing is going to sit in a file beside it.** The substance: the Museum holds the Low Ward series, the series opened in 1961, and **holds a record of what it has done, which is to print and send, every year since about 1954, one printed card to each of about four hundred and twelve addresses in the district, and to hold a print of that run, and to hold a correspondence file in which the addresses of earlier years appear** · it holds no record of the occupancy of any address, no record of the removal of household effects, has never inspected any dwelling, **and has not been down the street in question in the period to which your ninth paragraph refers, and has not been down it at all in the memory of any member of staff now in post** · **no claim to any item is known to the Museum, and none could be, and that is not a statement of confidence. It is a statement of the size of our records.** The Director reads it twice, initials it, does not ask for it to be softened, and there is no minute.

**On Saturday the twenty-ninth of May at about eleven in the morning there are four people in a room on the first floor of a building on a hill, and the first question anybody asks is the whole of the finding.** A systems technician of twenty-five puts nine acquisition dates on the table: **the fifteenth of December 2024, the fourth of January, the eleventh of January, the second of February, and then the ninth of March, and there are four more, and I have not read them out because the first four are enough.** **Those are the days of a round. FLS-2 goes out on those days. About two hundred and ten addresses on a sheet that comes out of a rack, in an order, and a man goes down the order with a handheld and he does not skip one and he does not stop, and he has been doing it since about 2019, and the round does not change on a Tuesday in February for any reason that has ever been written down.** The woman of about fifty says she is buying off the round, and he wants that out of the room before anybody says it in a corridor: **what I can say is that nine acquisitions in three months all fall on days when a delivery round went down a street in this ward, and I have done the sum on that, and the sum is about forty million to one, and the sum is remarkable, and I am not going to say the next sentence in this room, because I have not checked what the next sentence is and I have been wrong about a number of things in this case by people who were not me.** Then it is nothing anybody can act on, and he would like it minuted.

**And then, for about ten minutes, with nobody disagreeing: a cycle date is the first day of each calendar quarter; a block enters programme on a cycle date; notices are served on the cycle date, at the earliest date permitted, always on that day, on paper, by hand, at the property, by a person whose name is on the notice; not one notice has been challenged and not one was late; that is a programme and it is lawful and it has a signature on it and the signature is a letting agent's who has been right about everything she has said in this case for four months. And the machine is a man with a handheld in the last four inches of it, the four inches are about a fortnight, and nobody in this city has ever been able to look at the four inches, because there is no form for what a man sees when he puts a code on a field at a door and the person at the door is out.** She is downstream of that and not downstream of anybody, and there is nobody between her and the door: **nine addresses, about nine households in those flats in November and not now, a field went empty and a letter went to a house and the house stopped being anybody's, and then at some point in the following three months the contents of a flat that no living person can be traced to were sold by somebody with a set of keys, and that is the whole mechanism and every step of it is correct, and I have been in this case for twenty-four weeks and I have never once been able to find the step that is wrong, and I have stopped looking, and that is the most frightening thing I have ever said in a room.**

**And then a registrar says the thing about the name, about her own file, and it is the reason nothing else happened: there is a woman's name on a piece of paper in that building as of about half past eleven that morning, it went into a file on the fourth floor, it is a file about a request for a document and not about her, it will be there for the retention period, and in about four years somebody can ask this Museum whether it holds a record of a member of the Lantern Society, and it will be able to say that it does.** It is the only instrument in this case that has ever caught anything, and **it caught her doing something lawful. There is no forum in this city in which a person may say that a landlord has taken the ability to be recognised as the neighbour of an address — that was said by a conciliation officer of nineteen years on the eighteenth of March and it is true today. There is no second forum for a person to say that a collector has decided to buy only where nobody can be found, and there will not be one, and nobody has refused one, and none has ever been designed.** And the last sentence of the morning: **a name on a document is the only thing in this city that makes anybody answerable, and I have just used one, for the first time in twenty-four weeks, on a woman who has done nothing wrong, because she wrote down her own rule and sent it to a museum, and if that is the only use there is going to be of it in this case then the instrument is real and it cuts both ways and I have not slept well and I am not going to pretend to have.**

## Chapter 0099 — Checked By More Than One Person, Again (Sun 30 May 2025)

**Cliff Sowerby, 74, of number fourteen on that street since about 1974, a fitter's mate at the mill who has mended the row's standpipe every autumn for fifty years for a pint he would not take, is in a Museum personnel file of his own from 1974, is a line in a hardback book four miles away, and is the name on a letter that has been in a coat pocket in a rented room in Low Ward since Friday the thirtieth of October 2024.** Nobody chose him, and the reason nobody chose him is that everybody had him filed as a name, and a name is not a person who can be asked. **You can ask anybody in this room. I am the only one of the seven of you who has not been asked anything since Christmas.** I have been in this case since the fourth of December as a name. There was a letter about a line on a public schedule and I did not know there was a schedule. **There is a name of mine in a building four miles away on a posting sheet for eleven years with a building's address on it and my name on it and neither of them is me.** And about four months ago a woman of yours asked a room full of people what they wanted and I was not asked, because I have never been on a mat and never wanted to be. **I am here because a man of thirty-two has got a page in a notebook with a year in it and he has told me the year is a fact about a house, and I have been at number fourteen since 1974, and there is one address on that street where I have never once had to knock.**

**Thirty. Thirty is the one with the wrong year, and it is the one he has never knocked at, because the stopcock is under the stairs, it has never had a lock on it, he has had the run of those three yards since about 1975, and you do not go round knocking when you are going into three back yards for the water.** He has knocked at about nine doors on that row over fifty years because they were empty and you have to knock when a door is empty, **and he has never once knocked at thirty, and the reason is that there has been somebody in it every autumn for as long as I have been on that road.** And then, in about nine seconds, and it does not require anybody in that room to believe a word of it: **when one of you tells me a family went into that flat in 1982, I am telling you that the year before that there was somebody in it, and the year before that, and I can go back a fair way, and I am not doing it for a piece of paper.** How far: **I am not going to say a year, because I am seventy-four and I do not trust a year out of my own head, and there is a woman in this case who is a records officer and would tell you that a year is a fact about the world and a year out of a head is a fact about whose head it came out of. But I will tell you three things that a year is made of. It is not 1982. I have knocked on that row every autumn since 1974 and there has been somebody in that flat every autumn since the year before I started, and I have never once knocked at it. And nobody in thirty has ever been in a position to tell me when it started, including the people who have lived in it.**

**The page from the twentieth of May is read out loud in front of six people, by the man who wrote it, including the part where he wrote down that he knew he was not supposed to, and there is no copy and no camera and nobody made a record of the reading.** He refuses to summarise it, because a man of about fifty read nine pages out loud in a label room on the twenty-third of April for an hour and ten minutes, and it was the best hour of his life. **At the row for number thirty the woman of about fifty puts her hand flat on the table: that is me, that is my mother's years, I did not make them up, I have got a tin with cards in it and I have never once looked at the years on them because I did not think of it as a thing with years on it. I said them out loud because somebody was writing them down and it seemed daft to make a person stop and look at a card when you have got people in a room who were there.** Her mother died in 1998 and had the years off a council letter about the rent, about the account being transferred, which means it was in somebody else's name before, **and she never had that letter in front of her again. So the 1982 is a council letter about a rent account and not a year anybody was in that flat.**

**And then a man of thirty-two says the other half of it, in about nine seconds, and nobody in the room understands what he is saying: I wrote it down. I was in a room with nine people in it and I read the third line of the first page of that notebook while they were talking, and the third line is *do not write it down*, and it is in my own hand, and I wrote it down, and it is wrong. I wrote down nine things that nine people told me in a room, and eight of them are right, and one of them is wrong by at least eight years, and the one that is wrong is wrong because a woman of about fifty said a thing her mother told her and her mother was told it by a letter about a rent account, and not one of those three people did anything wrong and not one of them would have known, and there is no form anywhere in this city for checking it and I could have checked it in about nine seconds by reading it back to her. I could have read nine addresses and about thirty numbers back to nine people in about four minutes and nobody asked me to and nobody is going to now, and I did not, and that is the entire finding of twenty-five weeks and it is about me.**

**And a registrar of twenty-eight says what kind of thing this is, in about eleven seconds, and uses a word that had not been used in a room in this case before: memory can be planted. Not by anything. By a person who is fond of you, in a room, out loud, without any intention at all, on a Tuesday afternoon.** That has been taking place in this case since the sixth of December, and it took a man of seventy-four to make it into a finding, and she wants it minuted by somebody, and there is no minute, and it is going in a plastic wallet instead. By whom: **by nobody. That is the whole of it and I have been here five months. Nobody can plant a memory except somebody who has one, and the person who plants it is almost always the person who loves you, and the way to find out is not a form and it is not a system and it is a second person reading it back.** In April a records officer of about fifty said: if I can be wrong about a day of the week off a wall calendar then anybody can be wrong about a day of the week in a notebook, on purpose or by accident, and the only way anybody finds out is if a second person reads it. **She was talking about a day of the week. She was talking about this and she did not know and I have been carrying her sentence about in my bag for a month and it was about this the whole time.**

**And the man of fifty-two says the thing he said on the twentieth of May, in the same words, and this time it was not him that needed saying — a list of houses is worse than a list of people — and then the half that matters: the year is not mine and it is not hers. A thing that was never checked is worth nothing, and that is not a moral, it is arithmetic, and I said the other half of it on a Sunday afternoon a fortnight ago to a man with a notebook, and he wrote it down, and four days after that he wrote down the wrong number about a house instead.**

**And the list is amended in the room, by the woman who said the years, and the amendment is visible and the wrong year is struck through and not erased.** She asks for a pen, strikes through 1982, writes *before 1982, to be confirmed* over it, stops, and asks whether she might put *I do not know how far back* on it, and the man of fifty-two says she might, and she does. **Nothing has been erased, and the wrong year can still be read by anybody who comes along and wants to see what a woman of about fifty believed on the twentieth of May 2025. That is what it is for: you have a list with a wrong year on it and you have not erased the wrong year, and in about four years that is the only thing in this case that anybody can check anything against.**

**And then a man of thirty-two gives a man of seventy-four a letter that has been in a coat pocket since the thirtieth of October and it is not opened in the room.** He carried it seven months and did not read it and was not asked to, and gave it over at about half past three on a Sunday afternoon without any preamble, and nobody made a thing of it and nobody wrote it down. **That is yours. It was posted on the thirtieth of October and it went into a box and I took the box. — Then I am not opening it here. — No. — I have lived in this house for fifty-one years and I am not opening a letter from a museum in a room with six people in it, and I will open it on a Monday and I will tell nobody what is in it and you can ring me in a fortnight and I will say that I have opened it.** The man of thirty-two notices that he has carried that for seven months, does not say thank you, and it was not unkind. **He lost nothing: no page, no name, no evening, no order of any of it, and nothing about the ninth of December is different from what it was on Wednesday, and he has checked, and he has checked by asking somebody else, and the somebody else this time was not a professional with a wall calendar. It was a man of seventy-four with a standpipe key. And I am not glad. I have been not-glad about three things in twenty-five weeks and this is the first one that was my own hand.**

**And the chapter's last entry is the only place in the manuscript where the word is used: an echo is one room, a thing at one object, from one point of view, bounded and safe and able to be wrong only by him. What he has been doing for a fortnight is not that. This is a thread. And a thread is not a line — a thread is a thing you can lose the end of and somebody else can pick up, and if I hold it by myself I am the only person in the room again, and I have been the only person in the room five times in twenty-five weeks and every one of the five was this case's fault and not mine. So I am not going to hold it by myself. That is the whole of it. I am not calling it anything else and I am not going to write it down twice.**

## Chapter 0100 — A Register Somebody Has To Keep (Mon 31 May 2025)

**The volume's final image, and it is not a door opening.** The room is let from half past three until six, **four pound notes in an envelope and no name on the booking, and the shopkeeper on the corner has now let it seven times and has asked nothing seven times and told nobody what it is for.** About eleven people, a piece of A4 on the wall drafted on the twenty-fourth of April and on that wall and off it about nine times since and on it at half past three on the thirty-first of May, and **nobody had ever left it there, and a woman of eighty-one had brought a cloth bag.** The boundary is read out, all five lines, at the same volume, by the woman of about fifty from the sheet, who has read it every week since the fifteenth of May and is the person who has always taken it down. **Three names are on a copy in a hall in Sillick Lane. Nobody is keeping that piece of paper and nobody has ever been asked to, and the fifth line has been on that wall since the twenty-fourth of April and has been true of everything anybody has done on that street in twenty-five weeks, and it is now wrong on one sheet on purpose and staying wrong, and nobody in that room proposed to change either of those things.**

**And then the woman of eighty-one opened the cloth bag.** A hardback notebook of about a hundred and fifty ruled pages, a pound and ten, and it is not complete. Inside the front cover, in a hand taught in a school in about 1938 and worse since, in ballpoint, dated: **KEPT BY V. PILLING, 6 SILLICK LANE. FROM 31 MAY 2025 UNTIL THE FIRST OF OCTOBER 2025.** The man of fifty-two reads it, says **a name against a date**, says nothing else about it, and sits down.

**And then she said why, and it took about a minute, and she had been asked by nobody and had told nobody: I am eighty-one and I am not a registrar and I am not a records officer and I am not anything. I have got a clinic sheet with four lines on it and one of the lines is a length of time.** In April a woman of hers asked her to write a man's name on a card about a house and she said no twice, because in about four years somebody will come to that door with a clipboard and a woman in that flat will have to say no about a man she has never heard of. **She has thought about it every day for six weeks and has worked out what she actually meant: I am the only person in this room who knows exactly what a piece of paper does to a person after it has been made. That is the whole of my qualification. It is not a skill and it is not a job and it is not a virtue. I was the only one of you who had been on the other end of a record, and I did not like it, and I have kept a copy of a pay sheet in a hand of my own in a tin since November 1974 because of it.** And why not somebody else: **because everybody else in this room wants it to be a good thing** — the man of fifty-two has been waiting since 28 February for somebody to make him say a fourth rule and still has not been asked; the woman with the rota wants the rota to be something; the man with the notebook wants a document. **I do not want a good thing. I want somebody who has been on the wrong end of a piece of paper to be the one holding it, and there is one of those and she is eighty-one and she is not going to be here in four years, and I have said that out loud and I would like it written down.**

**The first page is four lines, and the fourth is the whole argument:**
> **This is a record of what was done on a Saturday.** It is not a list of people and it has no names in it except mine on this page and the name of the street in the headings.
