Anybody may stop at any point and nobody is asked afterwards why, so where somebody stopped there is a gap, and nobody is to fill a gap, ever, and a gap is not a fault and is not going to be counted. **If you are reading this and you are not me, then you are the second person, and that is the only reason it exists.**

The man of thirty-two reads the fourth line out loud twice and nobody improves it and nobody asks who the second person is. **Four lines, drafted in her head for a fortnight, not clever and not hers either: a rota cannot hold a permission, because a rota has to say why people stopped and nobody is allowed to ask, and a book that cannot hold the permission is not much of a book, and it is the only kind that is correct. That is a registrar's, and I was told it, and I have written it as mine and it is not mine, and I am eighty-one and I am not going to worry about that now.**

**Four terms, and one of them is the refusal.** One: it is mine, it lives at number six in a drawer, it does not lock, on purpose. Two: **nobody reads it without me and I am not going to be asked to say yes and I am not going to be asked to say no and I will say the same word to both.** Three: **the day I stop, it stops, and it does not go to the Museum or a council or a solicitor or a man of fifty-two, and I am not appointing anybody, because appointing anybody is how the fourth of January turned into the twenty-eighth of February.** Four: **there are no names in it. Not the nine. Not the hundred and forty. Not a person's name at a door, and not the year that was wrong, and not whatever is in a file four miles away with a woman's name on it. It is dates, and times, and door numbers, and how many stopped. And I have been told that that is useless, and I have been told it by a records officer on a bus and by a man of thirty-two and by a woman of fifty-four and by me, and the last of those is the one that counts.** **And it is for the fourth of January: if anybody in this city ever wants to know whether a thing happened on a street, there is one piece of paper that says so, in a drawer, with a date on it, and it is not a list of anybody, and it will be there on the first of October, which is the day the boundary stops, and that is the same day on purpose.**

**The first entry is four lines long: Sat 29 May. Three o'clock until about twenty to six. Fourteen doors. Nine households. Nobody stopped on the first round and two stopped on the second, so: two stopped. Gaps left. The boundary went up on the noticeboard at the library and came off at twenty to six and left a clean rectangle. Weather: no.** And on the third page, in the same ballpoint: **This is not complete and it cannot be made complete.** It begins on the thirty-first of May 2025 and there is nothing in this book about December 2024, or November, or any of the about nine households that were in the flats on the east side of that street in November and are not now, **because you cannot take a list of a thing nobody kept, and because a book that began on the thirty-first of May is not a record of December and anybody who says it is wants it to be one.** And the gaps are the other half of it: **there are about nine of them in three weeks and there will be about four hundred in four months, and one of them is a year that a woman said out loud and a man of seventy-four says is wrong, and it is not filled in, and it is not to be filled in, and a gap is a person who stopped and it is the only honest shape a stopped person has.**

**And then somebody asked whether this was a religion, and she answered it, and it is the last thing she says. — Is this a religion. — No. There is nobody in this room who has to believe anything, and nothing has come back, and nobody has been given a house, and the flats on the east side of that street are still empty and there are still about nine of them and there is still nothing in this city that says what they were except a piece of A4 in nine hands with a wrong year on it. I am keeping a book. I have not been given a miracle and I am not owed one and I would not know what to do with one, and I have been on this street since 1961 and I have seen a great deal come back into this ward and none of it stayed. — Then what is it. — It is a book.** She shuts it, puts the elastic band round it, and the bag goes back on the table under the piece of paper on the wall.

**Four miles away, in a public room on the first floor, a bell said nothing, and the whole of the institutional position is a list of things that did not change.** One hundred and sixty-nine days. The Hall open ten until five, about four hundred people through the front door, nobody in that building has a duty to listen for it and nobody in that building heard it, **and a note in that building has never been a record, because the day book at the front desk is the record of issue and return and it has one line per device and a printed head that says so, and nobody has ever written a sound in it and nobody ever will.** The fracture open under the staple. **The card on the front of the case is under a panel of A4 and tired tape that has been there since Friday the twenty-seventh of November 2024, a hundred and eighty-five days, the panel is the requester's, the requester has not been asked anything today and has not been asked anything since the twenty-seventh of April, and nobody in that building has the standing to lift it and nobody is going to.** Annexe D permanent, Annexe E in force and stricter than condition four, condition two failed on the twenty-third and the twenty-ninth of December and not amended and not going to be, the Hall open, the rope line where a clerk of works re-hung it in November 1935, **the rope is not on the schedule and it is furniture**, nine carried patterns on open plinths, a box of minutes on the ground floor at the back saying the word CIRCULATION about a route from one door to another door, and a registrar who stood in that store on a Thursday in February and refused to claim the rest of it, and she was right. **And the fourth line of the card under the panel says *Municipal Works*, which is the name of no body, and which went into a public document on the twenty-eighth of May at about one o'clock in the afternoon, in a schedule of collections that any member of the public may ask for, and nobody has been named, and the hole has a date in it, and nothing in this city has moved in either direction tonight.**

**At ten to six the room is given back.** Frank Dunning says the thing he has said four times, in the same words, at the door, and it is the last thing said in that room for the last time: **that is the correct arrangement and it is the worst arrangement in the world, in that order.** The boundary comes off in one piece, the tape comes off the plaster and leaves a clean rectangle, and nobody looks at the rectangle for longer than it took. **Five pounds comes out of the envelope and the shopkeeper takes it and says the room had been let for one day and shuts the door.** The bag goes down the stairs after her. **And this is the whole of what there is: a room let by the hour, a wall with a clean rectangle on it, a boundary that nobody signed and nobody keeps, and a hardback notebook of about a hundred and fifty ruled pages in a cloth bag going down about fourteen stairs to a flat at number six, where it goes in a drawer that does not lock, with a name and a date on the inside of the front cover, and about nine gaps in it, and about four hundred more to come, and nobody is going to fill them in.**

The last entry states the volume's whole finding in four hundred words: **the bill goes out, the name comes back** · what a name field is for · **to the door, and not to the book** · that is the instrument, you knock first and then you say the name, out loud, to a person, and nobody writes it down, and **everything else in twenty-five weeks has been a way of writing it down and a way of not being able to** · **a room, a boundary, and a record somebody has to keep, in that order, on a Monday evening, in Low Ward, in about four thousand words, and none of the three is a miracle and one of the three is a woman of eighty-one with a drawer that does not lock.** The last line of the volume is not a question.

# CHAPTER SUMMARIES — VOLUME 02 CLOSE INDEX

**Chapters 0001–0100 all have summaries above. This index exists so that a writer can find the ten chapters they actually need without loading a thousand lines.**

| What you need | Read |
| --- | --- |
| **The handoff.** The volume's fixed image, the last notebook, the two clocks, and the list of things that did not change. | `chapter-0100.md`, and its summary immediately above |
| **The first volume 03 chapter opens two days after this.** | `chapter-0100.md` in full, and `chapter-0099.md` in full |
| **The acceptance, and the thing that is not a bell.** | `chapter-0094.md` — one knock, on a door, at about half past four on Sunday 16 May 2025, two hearers out of five, nobody opened it, nobody asked what it was |
| **The memory-planting mechanism Volume 03's cost is built on, and the second person reading it back.** | `chapter-0089.md` and `chapter-0099.md` — nine pages, seventy minutes, one wrong day of the week, and no form for *is there a second person* |
| **The term, the four flats, the two clauses, and two refusals on paper.** | `chapter-0096.md` |
| **The false name in a public document, from the bottom of a card, with the panel taped over it.** | `chapter-0097.md` |
| **The buyer identified by a document and not by a person.** | `chapter-0098.md` |
| **The rope, and the object-or-place question refused for the fourth time.** | `chapter-0079.md`, and `chapter-0088.md` for the registrar's procedural refusal |
| **The reversal, as a reversal of a reading and not of a monster.** | `chapter-0075.md` |
| **The pinned line, and the five canon figures that verify it.** | `state/continuity.md` under `VOLUME 02 CLOSE — THE SECOND PASS`, § 3 |

⚠ **Two things a Volume 03 writer must not inherit as canon, both verified in the chapter files and both unrepaired.** **Chapters 54 to 57 are not in date order** — every date is right and the sequence is not. **And Chapter 100's register gives two different days for its own beginning**: the **stated start appears three times and all three agree** (`:29`, `:88`, `:135`, all 31 May 2025) and the **first entry, dated `Sat 29 May` at `:78`, is two days earlier** — 29 May 2025 is a pinned Saturday and 31 May a pinned Monday, so each is right and a book cannot do both. ⚠ **This used to be described here as *a fact stated twice with different values*, which is wrong about the shape: the three statements agree with each other, and the first entry is the *earlier* statement, so the class-one rule that the later statement is the wrong one would send a repair pass to edit the three that agree.** Corrected 2026-09-26. **A Volume 03 chapter may refer to the register and must not restate its start date** — that instruction is unchanged. Full diagnosis in `state/continuity.md` under `VOLUME 02 CLOSE`, § 4c item 4.

**Movement 1 of 4 of `outline/volume-03.md`. Tuesday 1 June 2025 to Sunday 4 July 2025, twenty-eight weeks and a day, on the pinned line (real Gregorian plus two days on the weekday). Ten chapters, 45,186 words, `wc -w` over the ten files, re-derived after every pass. Every weekday in the batch was derived from the pinned line and not from a phase prompt; the sweep is at the foot of this section and its unparsed count was not measured, which the sweep's own row says and this sentence used to contradict.**

**Where a summary and a chapter disagree, the chapter governs.** ⚠ **A Volume 03 chapter may refer to Vera Pilling's register and must not restate its start date** — that instruction was inherited from the Volume 02 close and has been honoured in all ten chapters. **The register appears in Chapter 110 five times — four as a *hardback* and once as *the register* — and its start date is not stated in any of them. The count was four and is five; the substantive claim was checked and holds.**

## Chapter 0101 — The First Of June

**Tue 1 June 2025.** The term at **Cauldwell Buildings** begins at ten past nine in a corridor on the first floor: four flats, seven households, a weekly rent, rents of thirty-two, twenty-nine, thirty-four and twenty-five pounds on the fourth page — a hundred and twenty a week — a term to **Tuesday 30 November 2025**, and a name against each rent and each flat. **Two of the four flats are empty at four o'clock and the households in them have paid rent that morning for a flat they have not slept in**, and Hazel Cray, a housing officer of about fifty-eight with twenty-two years in post, says she chose one date for all four rentals rather than four dates and says why. **Two of the nine said no in May** and are not on the fourth page; they are on a form in her office with DECLINED on it, and one gave no reason and she wrote NO REASON GIVEN, and the registrar's line from May is restated by the woman of about fifty: **that is the whole of the difference between them and the seven of you and it is a box in my office and I cannot make it any bigger than a box.**

**Both clauses are in force from the first hour and neither has been tested.** Clause 7 of page two: the owner is not a party to any practice, arrangement, custom or agreement between the residents, and the residents are not parties to anything the owner does in the building. **Enid Skelham, sixty-eight, owner of freehold, ground-floor flat, has let the four of them weekly since about 2009, reads the clause out to an empty corridor, and says it is the reason she has never been up those stairs and the reason she is not going to start.** Clause 14 of page three: any one resident may end the group's occupation on fourteen days' notice without affecting the others. **The housing officer says the flats are hers again on the first of December and she will let them to whoever she likes and has not decided and will not be asked to have a plan before November.**

**A river notice goes onto a board in that stairwell with a number on it.** `CFO/25/118`, City Flood Office, issued 30 May, candidate period 12 to 22 June, **Ferry Bank (registration district) and the lower terraces of Low Ward**, and it says in its own text that it is a warning and not a flood and that nothing in it should be used to decide whether to enter or leave a building. It went into about nine stairwells and onto a library noticeboard and outside a laundrette on tape, and **nobody in this case put it there.**

**⚠ The close is widened and the widening is declared in the second paragraph.** Elias Venn is not in that building at all until three o'clock. He asked Hazel Cray on the Friday whether there was anything he could do and was told no in about nine seconds, and the reason is the same reason he said no to a seat here in May: the day he is in that building, then in four years there is a man in a room who can say the practice at Sillick Lane was in Cauldwell Buildings on the first of June with him in it, and he will be right. **So he is in a room above a shop in Fisher Lane from three o'clock until twenty to nine — the first time in twenty-seven weeks he has been in a room where something is happening and no record of it exists anywhere, and he is the only person in it with a notebook, and he does not write anything down for five and a half hours.** The boundary goes up and comes off and leaves a clean rectangle; the shopkeeper has let the room eight times and asked nothing eight times. **At about a quarter to nine, at the last door, a woman who has lived there nineteen years says out loud that there is a number on the bottom of the warning and she cannot work out what it is for, and the man of fifty-two says that is the first piece of paper anybody at that door has ever been handed.**

---

## Chapter 0102 — The Box On Page One

**Thu 3 – Fri 4 June 2025.** A flood claims office on the second floor of a building on Wharf Street, and **Marianne Cope, about thirty-four, nine years in the trade, the person who sends the instruction wallets out, reads page one of a two-page instruction issued in 2011 and gives the mechanism away on the page, and refuses, three times, to say it in her own words.** The instruction: nine numbered items on page one, of which **item 4 is DATE OF LOSS, mandatory, cross-referenced to Part 5 section D.** Four hundred and eleven adjusters, contractors and clerks in this city were issued it between 2011 and November last year. **She has asked four people whether they read page one, knows the answer for two, has stopped asking, and has started a pencil note in the back of her own copy — thirty-one lines, not a record, goes in a bin when she leaves.**

> **PART 5. EXCESS SETTLEMENT — SCHEDULE AND ATTACHMENT**
> **A.** An excess is payable **once in respect of each household** in respect of each event.
> **B.** The household for the purposes of A is the policyholder **at an address on the date of loss**.
> **C.** An excess is attached to an event by reference to a date and not otherwise.
> **D.** **Where the date of loss is not evidenced, the settlement cannot be attached and the claim is referred.**

**She reads that out and then stops, and when he asks what the box is for she gives the sentence this manuscript has been refusing to be told for twenty-five weeks: *the form answers what the field is; you are asking what it is for; and nobody knows* — she has asked two people who used to be senior to her, both are gone, and one guessed in a room where she was junior, which is how you guess.** She will not do one single thing about it and says so in advance, twice.

**Nine hundred and four claims were opened between 9 and 19 December.** She will not convert that into a number of households or people, and she will not say how many are waiting on the box; "about forty of them are a box on page one" is her number and it is pencil in the back of a plastic wallet.

**A household that has been through this in December and does not know it is the same system.** **Kathleen Orrey, sixty-one**, over a shop in Fisher Lane, reported 12 December 2024, reference `DL/24/10207`, water in the ground floor in the second week of December. She did everything the office asked twice. She put the ninth of December in the box and was told a photograph is not a document; she found a letter and sent it and was told it was not the right kind of letter; and in the end **she left the box empty rather than write something in it that was not true, and sent it anyway.** On Tuesday 3 June a second letter arrives from a different department about a flood in June, with June on the first page, and she has not had June — she has had December three times. **It is a chase and not a new claim, and the standard enclosure list is why: the office sends everybody the instruction because the form points at page two and page one is in the same wallet.** ⚠ **And at the end of that telephone call she says the thing nobody has said in this case: the two pages came with the *first* letter, in December, and have been on the shelf under her counter ever since with a rubber band on them, and she has not opened them, and she handed them to a man with no post to take back to the office they came from, because they are hers and not theirs. *I will bring them back myself. — You will not, and that is not a criticism. Nobody comes back*, and it is the whole of what she has learned about a piece of paper since December.** **That pair of pages is now a physical object in the case and not an abstraction: it was in a shop in Fisher Lane from December 2024 to Friday 4 June 2025, it has been in a man's keeping since, and a promise was made out loud by a woman of sixty-one and refused out loud in the same breath. Tracked at `state/continuity.md` under `VOLUME 03, BATCH 0001` § 3 and in `state/open-threads.md` after Volume 03 Batch 0001.**

**And a registrar of twenty-eight has read page one, on the Wednesday night, in about an hour, and was not asked to.** In a stairwell on the fourth floor: the instruction is good work, whoever drafted it in 2011 understood the problem and put a cross-reference where a cross-reference belongs, and **section B is the best sentence anybody in this case has read in twenty-six weeks** — and there is nothing on either page that asks how many people were in the house. She has written to the issuing office for the consultation behind section B, has put no name of his anywhere on the letter, has told him the answer takes four weeks **so that he cannot go and count**, and has refused, out loud, to say what she thinks the box is for: the person who owns it is a woman on the second floor of a building on Wharf Street who has done nothing wrong, and a registrar from a hill does not have the standing.

---

## Chapter 0103 — Two Crews And A Docket Book

**Sat 5 June 2025.** Two lorries with tail lifts in a street on the low bank in **Ferry Bank**, a named registration district of about 1,900 tenancies, from about half past seven in the morning, and about two hundred people watching from upstairs. **A docket is written while the work is happening, which is why this is a Saturday, and the crews come back on the Monday for the removals.**

**Colin Bray, about forty-four, nineteen years in the salvage trade, two crews, four vans, a weighbridge at a yard eleven miles off the ring road, and a docket book per crew.** He pays everybody on a Friday, he has missed two this year, he told them before he missed them, and both times he told them it was his own fault because he put two vans on a street in March. **He is owed fourteen thousand one hundred and eighty pounds since the eighth of January by a managing agent whose head office is a floor above a man who took a call in February and said the same thing**, and he says it in a street, unasked, in about nine seconds, and nobody makes it a virtue.

The trade arrives **before** the water because it is paid by the tonne and is only paid by the tonne when there is a loss. A docket has seven columns and six of them can be checked from the kerb and the seventh is the money:

> **SALVAGE DOCKET — CREW 2. Docket no. SB/F/06/0441. 31 Cutler's Rise, Ferry Bank. Sat 5 Jun 2025. Crew: 2 (Slade, K).**
> **ESTIMATED REMOVED — 14 cwt / 7 qtr / 0 lb.** (= 714 kg) **DISPOSAL: yard. TICKET NO. left blank.**

**Dermot Slade, thirty-four**, writes in block capitals because it gets wet, and says he writes what the client tells him and does not ask them to prove it, because the alternative is a scale in a front room on a Saturday and a man out of work on a Monday. **Nobody weighed anything. There is no scale and there is no return anywhere in the trade that says what came out of a house, and about nine hundred December addresses are in the book, and the number of the lines where the number in the column is what a person said rather than what a lorry carried is the only figure the contractor would give anybody and he has not got it.**

**The first refusal of the batch, and it is right.** **A woman of seventy-nine on a step at about ten past eight says no to a crew in her house** and gives the reason before anybody asks: a clearance is a loss manufactured in advance; if the water does not come she has given a lorry her mother's things, her cooker, her front room and her sideboard and there is no form in this city that takes a lorry back; if it does come she has given it away anyway. **Ivo Pell, twenty-five, puts it in one sentence: the trade is paid by the flood, everybody on this kerb knows it, and the only person it does not suit is the man who sent the vans.** And Colin Bray: **it is my fault, and I would like it recorded as such by a man with a notebook, because in about four years somebody is going to stand in a room and say nobody could have known, and I could, and I did.**

**Ivo is back on the page and his condition of 1 December 2024 bites.** He came for the drainage invert at the Museum's loading bay, which is about fourteen inches below the 1974 drawing. He wanted a photograph of a docket-book page. He did not take it: Dermot said no, and Ivo would not photograph it with somebody's permission either, and he said exactly why in a street — *a docket book is not collections material and I have known that since the second of December and I would like somebody with a duty to say it in a room so that I can stop thinking about it at seven in the morning on a Saturday.* He wrote the column out on the back of a docket, photographed **that**, countersigned his own initials with the time, and wrote underneath: **COUNTERSIGNATURE UNAVAILABLE — ONE MEMBER OF STAFF PRESENT.** He said it would be a problem for somebody in four years. He is right.

**And a man of thirty-two asked a contractor for a page of his book in front of two crews and was told no, for the right reason, and said no to asking again out loud**: if there is ever an inquiry into nine hundred dockets the contractor wants to be able to say nobody outside the trade has ever had a copy of a page. The one honest line in the book is a docket that says a crew did not attend and removed nothing, and the contractor says it is the best line in the book and it is worth nothing, because if that woman had been robbed tonight the docket would read exactly the same.

---

## Chapter 0104 — The First Claim, Refused, Correctly

**Mon 7 – Tue 8 June 2025.** **Roisin Mellor, thirty-four**, and her grandmother **Blanche Mellor, eighty-two**, claim `DL/24/10455` for the contents of a ground-floor flat at 2 Cutler's Rise, Ferry Bank, opened 13 December 2024. The grandmother was got out of that flat at about two in the morning on the tenth of December by two men from the floor above with a torch and a rope, and she has been in a first-floor flat in her sister's since the fifteenth. **Elias Venn is allowed into the office for four hours and it is the correct instrument: Roisin asked him to come, nobody wrote his name down, and there is no return in that office for a man in a coat, so there is no record that he was ever there.**

The refusal is four paragraphs and the second is nine words: item 4 is blank, item 4 is mandatory, it is cross-referenced at Part 5 section D, **unless the item is evidenced this claim cannot be assessed and no payment can be made on it.** The claim stays open thirty days from 5 June and then closes and may be reopened on production of the item.

**She can produce the date. The ninth of December, and it is the right date, and the date is not the thing in the way.** The document that would evidence it was in a tin on top of a wardrobe in the front room. There is two feet of silt in that room. A photograph of the meter taken on 2 November is a photograph and the instruction will not take a photograph, and about four hundred claims were refused on that point in December. **And Marianne Cope says, in advance, that if Roisin writes the ninth of December in the box it will be true and the claim will be assessed and paid, and that about nine of four hundred claimants telling the truth look exactly like the one who is not.**

**The reason is correct and the person it is correct for is not the person it is about, and she says so out loud in about ninety seconds.** Section D exists because in November 1998 a man claimed on one policy for the same water at two addresses eleven miles apart, both dates true, both paid, about nine thousand pounds, out of a fund about four hundred households were waiting on, and they waited eleven weeks longer. **She has refused nine claims since the eighth of December for want of a date of loss. Eight of the nine are like this one. The ninth is a man called Petrie who claimed in his wife's name for a flat in Ferry Bank he has never been inside.** The rule works. There is no answer to it and she is not going to look for one while she is sitting in a chair with somebody she has liked for four minutes, **because the moment she starts working round her own rule it is a favour, and a file with a favour in it is a different animal.**

**The moral floor's second storey, and nobody has a box for it.** She asks Blanche Mellor whether she was in that flat on the night of the ninth, and the old woman says she was, and on the tenth, and was got out at about two in the morning. **Marianne Cope did not write it down, and that is on the record in one line of a handling file and it is the only line in nine hundred files that says a thing an office did not do.** And then: *I am not going to be able to do one single thing with that, and I have wanted to say that out loud in a room since December.*

**Seven hundred and fifty pounds, once, per household, per event, and it is about the cost of a boiler.** It is what the whole apparatus produces and in nine years she has never once seen it go to a kitchen. Roisin needs it for a boiler and about five weeks of arrears. **She leaves the box empty and the claim closes on 7 July**, and the decision is recorded with no reason, because none was given and none was asked for, and the form has no box for one either.

**And in a corridor at about ten past three on the Tuesday she tells him the thing that puts a wharf on the map of this case, as a fact about her own week and not as a warning.** The low bank claims were re-routed in the middle of January because the district office had about a foot of water in its basement and the paper went somewhere dry, and somewhere dry is a **rented bonded store on a wharf four miles downriver off the Cattle Market end**, the council holds a lease, she does not know what is in it, and she has not been able to get at her own December file since the fifteenth of January. **I am telling you because you are a person and not an insurer, and because in about four years somebody will want a list of the paper that was in that flood, and I am the person who knows where the paper is, and I have told nobody.** She also refuses him a number twice, and gives the reason the second time: the second there is a number of people like her, every one of the eight stops being a woman I sat in a chair with and starts being a proportion, and a catastrophe has a rate and an injustice has a person in it.

---

## Chapter 0105 — A Reach, A Rope, And A Number

**Wed 9 – Thu 10 June 2025.** Nine miles upriver to **Braeswick Lower Lock**, in a van, with **Gwilym Petherick, fifty-five or fifty-six, thirty-one years a river engineer**, who is going because the reach board wants re-hanging, and **Ivo Pell**, who is going because the drainage invert at the Museum's loading bay is about fourteen inches below the drawing, and **Elias Venn, who gives no reason and is not asked twice.**

**The reach board is a wall with a scale painted on it.** A metre and a half of painted board above the door on the river face, graduated in ten-centimetre blocks, **dated 1911**, and it is at Braeswick because that is where it has been. **Emrys Tasker, about sixty-one, at that lock house since 1988, before that the one above it**: *it is not a gauge, it is a ruler. You read it with your eyes and you write it on a pad and you take the pad away, and if you lose the pad the year is gone. There is a gauge on the pier two hundred yards down and it is the water authority's and it is the one you want if you want a number anybody else will take.* And: *because it is here, and it has been here since 1911, and because you cannot telephone 1911.*

**The rope, described at last, in a working place, by the person who uses it.** About eleven metres of tarred manila the colour of a wet brick, three-quarters of an inch, tarred when it was new, **a proper splice at about four metres, a lead line for sounding still attached, and a canvas tag machine-stitched at one end, the stitching rusted, reading `BRAESWICK` and `1968`.** 1968 is either the year the rope was made or the year the board was hung; Tasker has never known and asked the water authority eleven years ago and was told it did not matter and was not entirely right. **If 1968 is the board then the rope is thirty-nine years younger than the thing it hangs, and if 1968 is the rope then somebody made a tie-line for a 1911 board, which is ordinary, and if 1968 is the board then somebody rehung a 1911 board and that is a record in a day book about a hundred miles away.**

**The knots, and they are not irregular.** Eleven in the tie-line between the splice and the head, at intervals that are not intervals. Elias counts them twice, gets eleven both times, and apologises to the room for counting. Tasker says he tied them, and said it flatly, in about two seconds, before anybody had asked: **every season at the start of it, I get a ladder out and I put the board back up and I look at where the water got to, and I tie a knot in the line to mark it, and then I do not have to remember the number. You are not writing it down. You are putting it somewhere you can get at with a cold hand and no light.** And the pad in a drawer is a pad and is not a record and is not kept.

**The chapter's one piece of real work, and it takes about an hour on the outside table and nobody talks.** Petherick lays the rope flat against the wall under the board and reads each knot against the painted graduations with a folding rule:

> **Knot 1 — 44 cm · 2 — 96 · 3 — 71 · 4 — 121 · 5 — 58 · 6 — 133 · 7 — 89 · 8 — 105 · 9 — 36 · 10 — 60 · 11 — 112**

Nine of those are marks and two are a whipping and a seizing he has known about since 1994 and the man who tied them did not know. **Sorted, the nine are 36, 44, 58, 60, 71, 89, 96, 105, 112, 121, 133 — and every one of them lands within a centimetre or two of a line painted on a board that has been outside since 1911, and there is no reason at all why nine knots in a rope should land on nine painted graduations and one very good reason why they should.** And then he writes one more line and underlines it:

> **These are heights. They are not levels.**

**A height is a fact about a rope. A level is a fact about the river. To go from one to the other you have to know what the doorstep is, in the system the river is measured in, and that is a datum.** Not wrong. Useless. A different thing.

**The table in his wallet has two columns and they do not agree.** `BRAESWICK REACH — REDUCTION TO DATUM`, printed about 2016 and laminated: Table A heights above Newlyn, Table B heights above gauge zero at Braeswick pier, and a note at the foot — **the relationship between Table A and Table B at this reach is not constant; do not convert one to the other by adding or subtracting a fixed figure** — and note 4: the offset between a sea datum and a river gauge zero changes as the bed between them changes, and over 1998 to 2016 it moved by about 140 millimetres. **So the two numbers do not agree, have never agreed, and the reason is a hundred and forty millimetres of riverbed that has moved, and neither number is anybody's fault and neither is going to be repaired.** He says which he trusts and why, and does not deliver a verdict in a room: **he trusts B, the gauge zero, because the knots were tied by a man reading a gauge and a knot has no opinion about the sea** — and the other one is the one that is right, because it has a definition and a chain of custody and a document anybody else can act on has to be in Newlyn, and the difference between the two is between a hundred and thirty and about two hundred and seventy millimetres depending on the year. **And then he says the thing he should not have said: he has got the only conversion in this county, two pages, out of nine years of his own readings, and he is not giving it to anybody, because the second it is in a hand that is not his it becomes a standard, and in about four years somebody will print a number off it on a reach where the bed is different and will not know that.**

**The fifth time the question is available in a room in twenty-seven weeks, nobody asks it, and Elias says why out loud to Ivo in a yard:** *it is a worse question in a working place. A man who hangs a board every spring and ties a knot in a line is not doing it for a reason, and if I go up there and ask him what the pattern is, then for the rest of his life he has a thing he has to have an opinion about that he did not have before.* And he says he said it to Ivo and not to Tasker, which is cowardice.

**The photograph that is not taken, and what it costs.** On the Thursday afternoon at the wharf, Ivo does not photograph the tide line on the quay wall because he has telephoned the Director and been told no, it is not collections material, take it — and countersign it means somebody else standing there at the point of making, and there is one member of staff on a wet wharf and it is him. **Anja Ward, fifty-three, said she will countersign four exposures on Wednesday morning and that he may take them on Wednesday afternoon, and that if it needs doing before Wednesday she will come and stand on a wet wall herself, and that she will not enjoy it.** The Wednesday is the twenty-third, and 23 June 2025 is a Wednesday on the pinned line and a Monday on the real one, which is how the first draft of this sentence went wrong. **Neither of them says anything else about it.**

**And the rope goes into the dry store at South Sluice, up eleven steps above a pump floor four inches higher than the floor outside, on a hose hook, and it is a working thing in a working store.** Not accessioned, not carded, not on a schedule, not in a case. **Fenn's sentence of 25 February 2025 governs and is referred to in the notebook and not improved on and not attributed to the man who is thinking it.** In the same room, in a cage on the wall, the **third ward-bell**, re-cracked in the autumn, a quarter of a tone flat, rung on 20 December and in April because the reach is at a warning level, and struck at about ten past five on the Wednesday by somebody who is not a keeper, **because it is a bell and you ring bells** — and nobody in that room listened to it for anything but that.

---

## Chapter 0106 — The Tide

**Mon 14 – Tue 15 June 2025. The chapter is short and the water is not in it.** The weather on the fifteenth was unremarkable, the wharf was dry, the tide was out at about twenty past two, and the whole of what happens happens on paper and in about eleven inches of tide line forty years old.

> **CITY FLOOD OFFICE — RIVER FLOOD ALERT. `CFO/25/131`. Issued 14 June 2025, 08:40.**
> **Forecast peak level at the Northglass pier gauge: 3.86 m above Newlyn, between 01:00 and 03:00 on 19 June 2025. Confidence: medium. This alert supersedes the warning at `CFO/25/118`, which remains a candidate-period notice and is not withdrawn.**
> **In the named districts of Ferry Bank and the lower terraces of Low Ward, residents should act on the warning at `CFO/25/118` and not on this level. A level at one gauge is not a level at any street.**

**Petherick says every word of it is correct and then says the thing that governs the next three weeks: a level is not a height, a height is not a level, the difference between three point eight six at Northglass and whatever happens at Ander's Row is a slope and a channel and a bend, and nobody in this country has printed a table that converts one to the other for that reach, because the Northglass gauge is the funded one and has been since 1911.** And the alert says so itself, in its fourth line, and it is not a courtesy: a body saying out loud that it cannot give you a number for your street and would rather you knew that than had one.

**And what a number on a wall means, in four pieces, and the fourth is the one he has not got.**
1. A number on a wall is a measurement if you know what it is a measurement of, and a memory if you do not, and there is no third thing, and everybody who has ever looked at a tide mark on a house has pretended otherwise.
2. The quay line is not a mark anybody made; the wall was re-rendered in 1986 and the line crosses both finishes, so it is at most thirty-nine years old and at least one, and only the sixty millimetres of lime at the top of it can be dated, and that is four years. A chalk line in a house is a memory. It is a person remembering where the water stopped, and the pencil is on the wall, and the person is not, and the wall cannot say whose memory it was.
3. This wharf has a gauge with a printed zero on a bolted plate with a date on it and about a hundred and fourteen years of chain of custody behind the zero. **And Ferry Bank has a flood wall that is older than the district's records** — in a 1780s survey with a name on it and no levels in it, while the district's registration records begin in 1951, and the document that joins them is not in this city. **So for about 1,900 tenancies there is no printed table that says how high the water has to be at that wall before it is inside.**
4. The conversion table exists, is two pages, is in his wallet, and he is not giving it to anybody, and he would like to be asked again in about four weeks in a different mood.

**And a woman of sixty-one on the quay has a photograph of the December water on her front-room floor with the ninth of December in the corner of the frame, and a dado rail and a radiator and her mother's chair upside down on the table.** She has got a date, which is a fact about a camera, and a level, which is a fact about a building, and the two of them are in one picture and the two of them are about four inches apart in the room and about a fortnight apart in the world. **Petherick: a level is a fact about a building and a date is a fact about a camera. A claim wants a date of loss, and a date of loss is not the date on a photograph, and there is nowhere in this country that anybody has ever written down why the two are different, and the reason nobody has written it down is that it was never necessary until about 2011.** She asks whether it will get her anything and he says he is a river engineer and has no idea and would not guess in front of her, and she says that is the first honest thing anybody in this business has said to her since December.

**And his last sentences, on a quay, with the tide coming back:** *in about four days somebody with a duty is going to ask me for a level for Ferry Bank and I am going to have to say no, and the reason I am going to say no is not that the water is uncertain. The water is the most certain thing I work with. The reason is that I would be handing over a number with a place on it, and a number with a place on it stops being a measurement and becomes a claim about land, and I have watched a flood wall come down on a good survey and I am not going to be the man who printed a height for a street that has not got one.*

---

## Chapter 0107 — THE FLOOD

**Fri 18 – Sat 19 June 2025. High water in the small hours of the nineteenth.** Overtopping at the north end at about twenty to eleven at night, peak at about twenty past one in the morning, and by half past one about eleven inches in a ground-floor front room on Ander's Row and about nine in a hall. **About nine households. A chest of drawers, a pram, a dog, a box of papers, a television, a folding wheelchair, about four hundred pounds of shopping. Two doors not opened, neither forced, nobody hurt anywhere on the low bank.** The chapter says that paragraph out in full and says that it is making it dull on purpose, because that is what it was, and because a chapter that made it beautiful would be lying about a night shift. ⚠ **The clock in this chapter is the night crew's own board — `21:00. RIVER ALERT IN. 22:40 OVERTOPPING N END. 01:20 PEAK.` — and every other time in the chapter is now either that board or downstream of it. The first draft had the overtopping at three different times across two chapters and the peak at two more, and the repair of 2026-09-26 set them to the board's figures.**

**A bonded store on a wharf four miles downriver took water at about twenty past twelve and is not opened in this chapter and is not going to be opened in this book.** Four reasons, and every one of them is correct, and that is the whole of the chapter. It is bonded and the licence holder's agent closed at noon on the Friday and answers a telephone with the same four sentences. Nobody has a key and the duplicate is in a safe in an office under four feet of water. The doors are the only strong thing on that quay and opening them would have put a two-foot head across the quay and lost the pump and the boat and the light, and a fire officer of forty-one with twenty-two years in said that at about half past one on a wet quay and said she would sign the report and that anybody who complained would be wrong to — and added: *I know. That is not the reason and I would like it noted that that is not the reason.* **And the fourth reason is that nobody could say whether there was anybody in there, and there is no form anywhere in this city that asks, and the crew spent about two hours not doing the one thing a person wants them to do.**

**The freeholder.** **Prudence Halloway, seventy-four**, has been in the building once, in 1987, when her predecessor showed it to a prospective tenant and died that August. *I have not been in it since 1987. I have never been in it alone. I have had the rent four times a year for nineteen years and I have never asked what was on the other side of my own wall, and I have thought about that a great deal in the last twenty minutes and I would like it recorded that I thought about it and got it wrong about six hours too late.* And on the Saturday morning: *if anybody in this city wants a villain for what is happening on my wharf tonight then they are going to have to invent one, because the villain is a box on a lease and it has been there since February.*

**The most frightening sentence in it is a professional reading a volume off a lease schedule on a mobile telephone at about half past two in the morning**, from four hundred feet by sixty of floor and eleven foot of racking and about eleven inches standing on that floor at the peak — **about six hundred and twenty cubic metres of water, plus about twenty tonnes of paper already in it, which is the six hundred and forty** —, and *it is not going to be a floor of paper, it is going to be a soup of about six hundred and forty tonnes of water and paper and I have never seen one and I am telling you the shape of it and not pretending to know the answer.* Nobody spoke on the quay for about nine seconds and the freeholder said *oh.*

**A man of fifty-eight who was in it.** **Jim Tarrant**, twenty-two years a removals man, went in at about eleven with a torch and a key for the office two doors along and no key for the store, whose side door was off its latch, to start moving the store's own boxes off the bottom tier because he could see the water coming. He did not ring anybody. He was in there four hours and got out at about twenty past three through a loading door on the landward side, over a yard in a boat with a ladder, a man in a wetsuit and an electrician's torch, and was not hurt. He moved about nine boxes and stacked them on the top tier and did not open one. Asked what was in the room with him: *Tons of it. Files, bundles, boxed. I didn't look at any of them, because I was moving them.* And then the true version, which is worse: *If you'd asked me that at eleven o'clock I'd have said the same, and I'm not going to say something now that I didn't see. That's the honest version and it's worse than a guess. A guess you'd have written down.* **He is the only living person who can say what was on the bottom tier of that racking, and what he can say is: tons of paper, in files, and he did not open one.**

**And the forecast was wrong by three centimetres, and nobody said anything about that either.** The alert said 3.86 at Northglass and the gauge passed 3.86 and stood at three point eight nine within about a minute of each other, and stopped there ten minutes later at the peak. Petherick rang the flood office at about ten past one and the duty officer read the number and then said it again in a slightly different way. *That is three centimetres. Three centimetres is the whole of the difference between this and a bad night. I want that written down in a place where somebody finds it in about four years and I do not care what is written around it.* ⚠ **The first draft had the gauge standing at three point nine one, which is five centimetres, beside a line in which a professional says three.**

**And the only good news.** On the Saturday morning the freeholder writes a four-paragraph letter whose first line is that she has never been inside the premises alone since 1987, so that nothing after it can be read as a denial, and whose fourth line is that **the doors may be opened on Thursday 24 June at nine o'clock**, attended by herself, her agent, a licensed officer and two surveyors, with a schedule of the contents to be produced that day. A night crew supervisor says it to two men in a shed and then asks a man with no post to stop it being a thing two men know about, because if the wharf clerk writes it down it is on a schedule with a date on it and not a thing two men know about. **I am not a person who minutes anything. Then would you mind not letting me say it to about six people in a row.**

---

## Chapter 0108 — The Morning After

**Sun 20 – Tue 22 June 2025.** The water has gone, which is the whole of what anybody finds cheering about. A council bowser at each end of the low bank and about forty men in orange waistcoats working a road they had not worked the day before. A man of thirty-two is told he is not on the list, and the man with the clipboard says **the list is Thursday's and it is not a list of people who are all right and it is not a list of people who are not all right, and if you are on it and you are not on it you are going to have a bad morning in about ten minutes**, and then does not ask who he is with again and writes nothing down. **Elias counts the doors between the end of the street and the wall, gets to about forty, stops, and is embarrassed, and nobody saw him do it.** The joke of the day, at about ten o'clock, is the only joke anybody makes all week: *if anybody finds a good sofa, I am not the man to say no. I have got a book with a weight column in it and if you want a sofa in it you have got to tell me the weight of the sofa, and I do not know the weight of any sofa in this city.*

**The salvage crews come in on the Monday because a crew works a Monday.** Quarter to seven, crew 1 and crew 2, Colin Bray in a high-visibility jacket, a pump and a generator from Ferris at South Sluice. *I said Monday, and I said it in March, and nobody believes a contractor about a date. The Monday is the removal. The Saturday is the clearance. The water is supposed to be in between, and this year it was in and out inside a day, so we have got a clearance we did not do and a removal we did, and that is not a thing anybody planned.* Nine addresses, six with something in them, three with not, and three dockets saying nothing removed and he could not tell you one thing about the three. And he asks a man with a notebook to write it down, is told there is nowhere to write it where anybody can check, and says: **then write it down in a place where you cannot, which is what you have been doing since March, and which is why I keep talking to you, and I am aware of how that sounds and I have thought about it.**

**He has not written down one household since the eighth of June. Fourteen days, the longest in twenty-seven weeks, and he did not decide to.** The notebook's third line is *do not write it down* and he broke it in a room with nine people in it in May and wrote down that he had. ⚠ **This was written and left as *he has written nothing down since the eighth of June*, which four notebook entries in the same batch contradict. Corrected in the verification pass of 2026-09-26: the claim is now about a household, which is true, and which is the volume's own definition — a name at an address on a date.**

**A woman of about forty-three is on that street on the Monday evening at about half past nine with a carrier bag who is not waiting for anybody.** She answers his one question about whether the council had been to her mother's address, and then, in about nine seconds, without preamble, puts a bag into a doorway and says there is a building in **Bankfoot** where the water went in on Friday and there is nobody anybody can ring, and it is not on his list because there is no name on it, and she knows that because she has been in it. **He asks her how she knew and then does not ask her a second question, and the second question was the one he wanted, and that is why she is not in this book with anything else in it.**

**And on the Tuesday he gets a ward map off a library board, finds the line, and walks to Bankfoot: about four hundred addresses, on the other side of the ring road, in a ward this case has been nowhere near in twenty-seven weeks. Nobody in it has ever said that out loud and it is the most damning sentence available to me this month so I have put it here instead of in a room. There is no list. There has never been a list.**

**14 Ashgill Row: nine flats, a ground floor and two above, about a hundred and ten years old, a railings, a bin store, a strip of grass two feet deep with a palisade fence round it, and a tide mark about fourteen inches high on the outside of the ground-floor door, stopping dead at the left-hand door and going across the right-hand one, the difference between the two being a step.** He knocks, low, twice, on the wood, and nobody comes for about eleven seconds and he counts them and is embarrassed about counting them. The door is on the latch. He says he is not from the council and not from anywhere and that he has three things and she can shut the door at any point, including halfway through the first one. She has it open before the end of the sentence and says: **you are going to want to come in and you are not going to, so say the three things from the step.**

**The three things are his own third line, said on a step in the rain, and he says plainly that they cost him.** One: he is not going to write her name down, not today, not in a book, not on a form, not anywhere, and the rule is the third line of his own first page, written in January after a postman told him the truth about something. Two: he is not going to tell her that anybody is coming, because nobody is, and he has been on two streets since Friday saying that sentence and about forty people have believed him, and it is not true, and he has stopped saying it. Three: **in about a month I will not be able to say that I came here, and that is what the not writing costs.** And then the fourth thing, which is the chapter: **I am not going to ask you whether there was anybody in this building on Friday night — because if I ask it, then in about four years there is a question in the world with an answer in it, and the answer will be whatever you say, and what you remember will be in a file somewhere with a date on it, and you will be a person in a file that says she was or she was not. And I have been the other end of a file. Somebody in a building four miles from here said no to a museum twice about a dead man's name on a card and she was right, and I have never worked out how to say thank you for that to somebody I do not know.** She says: *then say the other thing.*

**What was said at the door was a carpet and a boiler and a decision about a kettle, in about eleven minutes, and it is not the moral floor and nobody said the moral floor.** A carpet from about 1994, gone — not wet, gone, with the underlay a black thing and the boards under it black and a man coming on Wednesday about the joists. Her mother at number six, eighty-nine, with a bed in the front room of her mother's front room, and neither of them has said a word about it. **A boiler from about 1978 that is not wet, and a man has looked at it for a minute and said it will last the winter, and she has decided to believe him because she cannot pay for a new one and the number for the excess is seven hundred and fifty pounds and she has not got that either, and she does not know who to ring.** He gives her the address of a woman on the second floor of a building on Wharf Street, and says: *she is not the one who decides. She is the one who will tell you the truth about what decides it, and she will not be able to do anything about it either, and I would rather you had the second one than the first one.* She makes a cup of tea he did not want and tells him it is a bad cup and hot and he has been standing in the rain since ten past four, and he says yes.

**A council contractor asks her name on the path and she gives him a number and a floor and nothing else**, he says sorry, and that is the whole record of 14 Ashgill Row, and it will be destroyed on a retention cycle, and there is no version of this case in which anybody can produce a document in which that woman was asked whether there was anybody else in the building on Friday night.

**And on the Monday morning, in a corridor of a temporary office on Ferry Bank at about eleven, Ferris, a man of about thirty-four who runs a pump and has been on that bank for nineteen years, says the sentence that puts a sealed store on the map of this case, as a fact about his own job and not as a warning:** *if you cannot establish where a person was on a night, then the only record in this city that says where anybody was on a night is the register the Drowned Registry keeps, and that register is city records of people recorded as drowned in this city's floods, and it is kept because a drowned person's estate could not be settled without a death record, and it is in a building on a wharf that was shut on Saturday morning and has not been opened since. There is no copy and no photograph and no book and no family. There is a register in a shut building and about a hundred and forty years of somebody deciding to keep it.* And then: *I am a pump operator and I have just told a man with no post the location of a city record on the second day after it went under four feet of water, and I have done that because you were on the quay and because I have been thinking about it since two o'clock on Saturday morning, and I would like it noticed that I thought about it for two days first.*

---

## Chapter 0109 — Schedule F, And A Power In A Box

**Wed 23 – Thu 24 June 2025.**

**Four photographs, countersigned at the point of making, thirteen days late.** Ivo telephones at nine on the Wednesday morning and does not have to explain anything, and the Director of fifty-three countersigns four exposures on a mobile telephone at about eleven past ten on the quay, and it is the first thing this case has produced on a camera since the eighth of December 2024. **What he photographs is not a line.** The June water went through a ventilation, settled into the top of the wall as it went down, and left a stain with a top edge about a foot above the tide line, and the tide line is still there under it and is on the inside of the grille as well and Wendy Ockley can put her thumb on both.

**Wendy Ockley, about fifty-four, one van, four people, twenty-six years in the trade, on the quay since about seven:** *the tide is going to go above the June mark again inside a fortnight, and everybody in this city knows it, and the forecast on Monday said the same thing in a sentence at the bottom that nobody read.* And, to a man of twenty-five who did not take the photograph he came for: **somebody in a building on a hill has cost you the clean one — and the two of you got it right. The rule is not stupid. It is a man in an office four miles away who has never been on a wet quay being quite sure that whoever says they took a photograph was not asked to take it by somebody with an interest. It cost you the clean line and it stopped about four hundred exposures that would have been taken on a wet quay at four in the morning by a man with a clipboard. I have seen about nine of those and every one of them was a lie and I could not have proved one of them.**

**Nobody sent the officer.** A preliminary view sheet has a line on it that says *inspect premises at the wharf, stores and outbuildings, and report on condition of bank*. **Yvette Carrow, forty-eight or forty-nine, about eleven years in the post**, walked about four hundred feet along the quay and stopped at a wall, and spent fifty minutes on it with a torch rather than nine, because *if you take nine you will write a preliminary view and if you take forty you will write a different one, and mine is free and yours is not.* Two courses of a stone revetment gone, a void behind, and about nine centimetres of horizontal movement in a joint, and both of them looked at it and neither of them said anything for about eleven seconds.

> **CITY FLOOD OFFICE — SCHEDULE F, FLOOD-EMERGENCY ACCESS SUSPENSION. `CFO/25/SF/0194`.**
> **Premises: the bonded store and all outbuildings, yards, quay and revetment adjoining, [wharf], district of Ferry Bank. SUSPENDED.**
> **FROM: 24 June 2025, 06:00. PERIOD OF SUSPENSION: to 8 July 2025, 23:59** — *box for a period*.
> **GROUNDS: (a) recorded bank movement at the revetment adjoining the premises, inspected 23 June 2025; (b) forecast tide levels exceeding the June 2025 mark within the period; (c) loss of access for emergency services.**
> **DATED: 24 June 2025. SIGNED: Y. Carrow, Flood Emergency Powers.**
> **This suspension may be lifted early on written application. It cannot be appealed. Applications under this Schedule are not persons and are not recorded.**

**The instrument, and the box, and what Elias says about it in about nine seconds — the ninth correct thing he has said in four weeks: the period is the only box in it.** There is no box for a person, no box for a claim, no box for a document, and no box for a freeholder of seventy-four who has never been inside her own building, and on the eighth of July the door comes off the latch again and nobody in this city has any idea what is going to be behind it. And Ivo's half: **the eight other people who have signed something in this city since December have all left a box for themselves in it, and the reason they did is that they are not stopping anybody from a building. She is stopping herself.**

**At nine o'clock on the Thursday, six people on the quay, five documents, and the door does not open.** **The six are the freeholder, her agent, a licensed officer, two surveyors and a flood-emergency officer who came unasked; the five are theirs plus hers, and the officer who signed the suspension is the one person on that quay with no document at all, which is the whole argument of the chapter in one count.** **Ama Osei, thirty-six, a licensed officer, eleven of these done:** *I am not going to cut the shutter. I want it on my file that I said it at about nine and not at about eleven, and that I said it before anybody argued with me, and that I have been asked to attend and have attended and I am not able to enter. A bonded store is a seal broken in front of me or not at all, and I cannot be on the quay and not in the building, and there is no version of this where the two things are compatible.* She measured the joint herself with a tape and got nine centimetres and would not argue with the nine centimetres. **Asked whether she is on the same side as them, she says: I am on the same side as the bank. I am not on anybody's side, and I would like it recorded that the two of those sentences are different and that I have thought about the difference for about a minute and a half and I am still not sure which one is true.**

**Prudence Halloway reads the order standing up in the rain with a surveyor's clipboard held over it**, does not say the word outrageous, apologises for putting a city document on the wrong clipboard and corrects herself at once, and asks the only question anybody asked that day: *Two weeks. Somebody in an office has put two weeks in a box. What happens on the ninth of July if nothing has changed.* Carrow answers in about eleven words: **then somebody in an office writes a second one, or does not. There is no hearing and there is no appeal and I will be the person who signs it, and I have not decided, and I will not decide on this quay.**

**And she said the cost out loud twice before she signed anything, and nobody in that building was going to do that and did not.** Fourteen days in which nobody lawfully enters that quay: not the freeholder, not a fire officer, not a surveyor, not a bonded-goods officer, not a person from that office — about nine hundred addresses' worth of salvage paperwork in an office on the quay, about six hundred and forty tonnes of water in a room on it, and about a hundred and forty years of somebody's record of who drowned in this city. And the half that will get quoted: access to about sixty ground floors on the low bank is suspended while the bank is watched, so no assessor goes in, so no claim gets a cause of loss confirmed, so about a hundred and forty households that have been waiting since December wait a further fortnight for the same visit with a different person. And then the second thing, which is the reason she does not like the instrument and is signing it anyway: **she has no power in that form to see anybody's claim, tenancy, letter or photograph. So she is not suspending access to a building. She is suspending access to a building without ever having to know who is in it, and in about ten minutes she had discovered that this is the easiest instrument she has ever signed, and she asked somebody to explain why that is a bad thing, and nobody explained it to her.** And at the door afterwards, to a man of thirty-two who had not asked: **I know what this is doing. I am doing it. I want that in your book if you keep one, and I am not frightened of what you are going to write because there is not a line in this form about what you are going to write, and if there were one I would have to decide about it.**

**And a Director of fifty-three was asked by that office on the Wednesday afternoon whether this Museum holds any record of the premises and said no, and was thanked, and then said out loud that in about four years a building that holds nothing is going to be described as a building that was careful, and that there is a great deal of nineteen years in that which consists of being careful about the wrong things.** She did not say good. She said thank you and wrote something down and stopped, and it is about eleven weeks since anybody thanked her for a piece of paper she did not have.

---

## Chapter 0110 — What A Date Of Loss Is For

**Mon 28 June – Sun 4 July 2025. The batch's turn, and it is a question and not an event.**

**A church hall on Ander's Row, let for the evening for eighteen pounds with the money in an envelope, a booking sheet with the name of a woman who works at the church and is not in the room and does not know what the room is for. It has been let eleven times since the nineteenth of June and the woman who lets it has asked nothing eleven times and told nobody, and if anybody asks her what she has been told she will say she has been told it is a meeting about a river, which is what it says on the form.**

**Ten people, and the list is the whole of what this case has assembled in twenty-eight weeks in one room:** a claims handler, a registrar, a salvage contractor, a housing officer, a woman of eighty-one, a man of fifty-two, a man of about sixty-five, a woman of thirty-four with a refused claim, a woman of sixty-one who is owed a paper, and a man of thirty-two with no post. **And Marianne Cope chairs it, having told the district office in writing that she has no authority to chair it and been told by nobody that they have read the letter, and having said the cost out loud first, in about nine seconds, because on Tuesday the eighth of June a woman of thirty-four who had lost the ground floor of her flat asked her a question about a box she had been sitting in front of for nine years and she did not have an answer, and the reason she did not have an answer is that she was in a room with about nine other people and no cost in it. I can take notes. I cannot take decisions. I cannot pay anybody, move a date, release a claim, or get anybody into a bonded store before the eighth of July, and every one of those five is outside my post and inside my conscience, and that is the whole of the trouble.**

**The first thing on the table is a document obtained on purpose, and it is three days old.** Roisin Mellor asked on Tuesday 22 June for a copy of the decision and of any document the decision was based on, which she is entitled to, and it came on **Friday the twenty-fifth**, after eleven minutes on hold and a truthful woman on the telephone. Eleven sheets: a decision of 5 June, the two-page instruction on a separate sheet with a printed line saying it came with the enclosure list, a schedule of what the office held, four photographs, a list of what the office had asked for and not had. And on the schedule, **item 11, in a column headed HELD BY CLAIMANT / HELD BY OFFICE:**

> **Any record of the number of persons in the premises on the date of the event: none held. No form is used to obtain it.**

Read out, and then the other half, which is not in the file and is not going in the file: **nobody has ever asked it. There is a box for a date and a box for a policy and a box for a name and a box for a picture, and there is no box for a person, and that is in a form that has been in use since 2011.** And Hazel Cray, housing officer, twenty-two years: **there is no form in this city for that, and I am the person who would know, in this trade, and I did not look until the eighth of June and I have been ashamed of the date since.**

**The docket question, and the answer that is exactly right.** Colin Bray, in a room since 1988: **the weight is the money.** The crew writes what it is told. About nine hundred December addresses are in the book; he can tell you how many lines are on the pages and **cannot tell you how many of the nine hundred are lines where a number in a column is what a person said instead of what a lorry carried, and that is the only number in this business I would give anybody and I have not got it and I have been a contractor for nineteen years.** He says it to a room full of strangers on a Monday night and asks somebody to write it down and is told the man with the notebook cannot give it anywhere anybody can check, and Vera Pilling says: **write it down anyway. It is not the same thing and it is not nothing.**

**And at about eight o'clock the question is asked, out loud, from a chair, as a question and not as a complaint, and there is no minute.** Cope reads section B of the instruction out in a hall with the windows open — all of it, the only two pages anybody in this case has ever read out in a room — and then says: **that is what the box does, and it does not say what the box is for.** And she says the sentence she has used as a wall three times, and then says it is a wall and is not an answer, and then asks: ⚠ **There is one count in this beat and it is four, and the first repair of this batch wrote two. She says in the scene that the question has been put to her four times in three weeks and that she has refused it every time *with a sentence*, so the sentence has been a wall four times. What is new is the room and not the number: she says it out loud in front of nine people for the first time, says that it is a wall and is not an answer, and asks the question anyway. The notebook's *three* is now *four*.**

> **What is a date of loss for.**

**Nobody answered it for about nine seconds.** Elias is not looking at the question; he is counting, because that is what he does when he is frightened — ten people, ten chairs, a docket book, a folder, a carrier bag, a book in a cloth bag — and he gets to two hundred and something and stops and puts his hand flat on the table and does not finish the count. The chair rules that the question was taken, that there is no minute, and that the question stands.

**The man of fifty-two answers a worse thing and says so.** *I want everybody to notice that nobody answered her and I am not going to answer it either.* A date of loss is a date on which a household was a household at an address, and the only documents that prove when a household was a household at an address are a letter about the rent, a meter, a bank, a doctor, a tenancy, **and every one of those lives inside the building.** So a household whose whole house moved is the only kind that cannot produce a date, and a household that cannot produce a date is the only kind that has lost all of its paper. **The people who most need to be paid are the ones who have least ability to say why. That is not a scandal. That is arithmetic. It has been in two pages since 2011 and nobody has been hurt by anybody and everybody has been hurt by the form.** Asked what the form is then for: *the form is for the bill and the date is for the excess, and the excess is the only thing about this flood that anybody in this room has ever seen. And I am not saying that because I know it. I am saying it because it is the shape of it and I have had six months and I am fifty-two and I am not paid and nobody asked me.*

**And Vera Pilling is asked the same question at about half past eight and gives the same word to yes and to no.** *I have got a clinic sheet with four lines on it and one of the lines is a length of time, and I am eighty-one, and I have been asked that question four times in a fortnight by people who are being extremely polite about it, and I am going to give you the same word I give to yes and to no, and I would like it noted that I have decided that in advance.* **No.** And she will not open the cloth bag, and the register is a hardback with about nine gaps in it and about four hundred more to come before the first of October, and a gap is a person who stopped, and the day she stops it stops and she has not appointed anybody. **And a form does not know what it is for either. That is the answer and it is the only one I have and it has been available to me since about half past seven this evening.**

**The last fifty minutes are about access, a period and a bank, and there is nothing supernatural in it.** Hazel Cray says what happens to nine households with nowhere to be on 1 July (seven of them) and how many there will be in October (all of them), **and does not say what happens in November or December, because nobody asked and she is the only person in the room who could.** The registrar says four sentences about what the Museum holds about a wharf and a district and a flood, which is nothing, and that a Director sent her and then rang at four o'clock to say she did not have to be, and had not taken it back when she said she would stay. Frank Dunning says the correct arrangement and the worst arrangement in the world are the same arrangement and that he said it four times and is not going to say it a fifth time in a hall. **And at about half past nine Cope says there is a thing on the wall of her office that people say out loud about once every few months and that nobody ever wrote down, and that in twenty-eight weeks she has been in a room with a person in it three times and has not said it in any of them, and that she is not going to say it tonight either, and that the reason is that it would make the question sound answered.**

**And on Sunday the fourth of July at about four in the afternoon, in a corridor outside a shut hall, Mara Iles has a registrar read all eleven sheets of somebody else's claim file out loud to Mavis Ludlow, sixty-one, of 22 Sillick Lane, who has lived there thirty-one years and has been owed a piece of paper since the twentieth of May, and tells her the truth about it: this flood produces one kind of paper, a paper with a date on it, the only date it wants is a date of loss, a loss is a thing that has happened to a thing, and she has not lost a house and neither has the registrar, and so there is no version of this in which she is written down at twenty-two Sillick Lane in June 2025.** Mavis works out the rest herself in about nine seconds — about nine hundred households have been handed a piece of paper with a date on it, every one of them is about what they lost, and not one of them is about where they are, **so I am not going to be given one. Not this year** — and asks not to be lied to, and is not lied to, and then says the loudest thing in the manuscript without being asked, because that is why she is going to say it: **there is a hardback in that bag with about nine gaps in it and about four hundred more to come, and it has no names in it at all, and it is mine, and it is in a drawer that does not lock on purpose, and it stops on the first of October because a man of fifty-two put that date at the end of a sentence on a wall in April and I chose the same date on the inside of the front cover about a fortnight later, and neither of us has ever said out loud in a room what happens on that day, and I am going to keep not saying it, and that is the only thing in this city I have got that is not a claim on anybody.**

**The batch closes on a question and not an event, and the notebook says the answer out loud for the first time and only to itself: it is for the excess. It has been checked twice, against a printed page and a refusal letter and a number of pounds, and it is correct, and it is still not certain, and a man of fifty-two put the shape of it in a hall on Monday night and did not know it and said so, and Elias did not tell him, because he could not be certain, and that is the first time in twenty-eight weeks he has had something in his notebook that another person in this case could have used.**

---

By location: 11 in the two-paragraph bold logline block that opens each chapter, 5 in a notebook block, 4 in body prose, 2 in a section heading. ⚠ **Characterised, because a raw count overstates the fault here: 2 of the 22 are section headings, and this volume writes its section headings in the notebook's own voice, so *the whole of this chapter* in a heading is a house style and not a production note. Chapter 101 also carried a production note with a warning glyph, which was not a convention and is gone; the glyph and the note are the only instances here that were ever a defect in the prose rather than a house style.** |
