Strip single-asterisk spans before counting and `state/` returns 7, and all 7 are real.** ⚠ **The raw test is a heuristic and it has been misread in every pass that has run it, so read this row as a recipe and not only as a number. A legitimate bold run containing an italic span has an odd raw count, because the two italic asterisks are not adjacent and cannot pair — and a code span containing asterisks is a second such case, because a code span renders its contents literally and the test cannot see that. The instrument that works: on each line, remove single-asterisk spans, then count double-asterisk runs. On 2026-09-26 that returned seven genuine lines — 2 here in the Volume 01 section and 5 in `state/continuity.md` — all pre-existing, all in Volume 01 or Volume 02 layers Batch 0001 did not write, and therefore logged here and not edited. The other 14 are false positives, and that number is not a property of the files: it moves every time a record quotes a marker sequence in order to describe it, which is the only way these files talk about their own markup, and this row is one of the records that does it.** **Seven is the figure worth carrying. The false-positive count is a measurement taken at a moment.** |
| `trust` | **5, all read.** Four are Gwilym Petherick choosing which of two columns he trusts, in Chapter 105, and one is Elias writing in Chapter 106 that he is taking a survey on trust from a man he has known four days. **No chapter says the word about the relationship, and none may.** ⚠ **The first line of this row said 4 and read *three* Petherick; whole-word `trust` over the ten chapters is five. Corrected 2026-09-26. The qualitative claim was checked and holds.** |

| Sillick pay figures spelled in words | **11 hits, all read.** Every one is somebody being sixty-one years old. **The rent in Chapter 101 was ninety-four pounds a week in the first draft and has been changed to a hundred and twenty, and the contractor's outstanding balance was fourteen thousand two hundred and sixty-one pounds and has been changed to fourteen thousand one hundred and eighty, because a figure doing two jobs is a defect class this file has already logged once.** |

It first carried 44,834, the count of the batch as drafted. The review-fix pass re-derived it to 45,199. **The five real mismatches the first run returned, all of them in prose, all of them repaired, and the shape of three of them is the reason this record exists.** **Chapter 102 said a letter came on *Tuesday* the third of June; the pinned weekday of 3 June is Thursday.** **Chapters 107 and 108 said the bonded store was to be opened on *Wednesday* the twenty-fourth of June, twice, in a letter and twice in a notebook; the pinned weekday of 24 June is Thursday, and the real weekday of 24 June is Tuesday, so this is not a real-calendar slip either — 25 June is the real Wednesday and 24 June was given it by a writer doing arithmetic from memory.** **Chapter 110 said a freeholder asked a claims handler a question on *Friday* the nineteenth of June; the pinned weekday of 19 June is Saturday, and the event itself was on the twenty-fourth and was put to a different person, so the sentence was rewritten and now refers to Monday the eighth of June and to Roisin Mellor, which is what actually happened.** **And Chapter 110 said a document was obtained in five days when the Tuesday the twenty-second to the Friday the twenty-fifth is three, and the pinned Friday in that week is the twenty-fifth and not the twenty-seventh.**

**Movement 2 of 4 of `outline/volume-03.md`, and it closes Movement 2. Monday 5 July to Sunday 8 August 2025, thirty-four days. Ten chapters, 53,220 words in `chapters/volume-03/chapter-0111.md` through `-0120.md`, `wc -w` over the ten files, measured after the last edit and not before one. Every weekday in the batch was derived from the pinned line (real Gregorian plus two days on the weekday) by script and not from a phase prompt. Where a summary and a chapter disagree, the chapter governs.**

**Movement 2 is thirty-four days and does not divide into five weeks, and the day map shows it: the last pinned week carries one chapter and one long chapter and the middle three carry two each. Chapter 120 is three days long because the calendar says so and not because of anybody's appetite.** The register may be referred to and its dates are not restated; it is referred to once, as item six of a list of six, in Chapter 120.

## Chapter 0111 — For The Excess

**Mon 5 – Tue 6 Jul 2025.** A claim reaches the thirtieth day it was allowed, and because the claimant asked for a review in writing on Friday the second of July, four people sit in a small room on the second floor of a building on Wharf Street from about half past ten until about twenty past twelve: **Marianne Cope, Sinead Molloy (47, same handling team, twenty-two years), Roisin Mellor (34), and a man of thirty-two with no post who is not a party to anything and for whom there is no box, so nobody wrote his name down and there is no record he was there.**

**The answer, said in her own words for the first time in nine years, in about forty seconds, and not using the words *excess settlement* once.** A date of loss is for attaching an excess to a household. The date is not wanted by the bill; the bill wants a list of things and what they were worth. **An excess is a flat sum, payable once in respect of each household in respect of each event; the household is the policyholder at an address on the date; the attachment is by reference to a date and not otherwise; where the date is not evidenced the settlement cannot be attached and the claim is referred. Seven hundred and fifty pounds.** Then the sentence about what it does: **a household cannot be attached without a date, and a household cannot produce a date when the only documents that would evidence it are inside the building, and the building is the thing that went** — the tin on top of the wardrobe, the wardrobe on its side, two feet of silt in the front room, everything inside eleven inches of water under the stairs.

**And the count: four hundred and eleven copies in this city, about two hundred and fifty sent by her own hands since December, an archive of the previous four prints in a cupboard on her floor, and there has never once been a meeting about it.** She has said the purpose out loud to about four people and two of them have left the industry. **Sinead Molloy has read page one, in about 2016, called section B elegant, and has never been asked a second question about it.** The man of thirty-two says the thing nobody wanted: **she has known it since March 2016 and nobody asked her until the twenty-eighth of June**, and she answers that nobody thought of it, because everybody in that building reads the form as a form, and she has not noticed that she stopped asking because there was nothing to notice on any particular day.

**She can do one single thing about none of it, and she lists six things, and every one is true**, and one of them is that there is no fund for what the decision has cost a household and she has looked twice. She asks Blanche Mellor-style questions for the first time: whether there was anybody else in that flat on the ninth, and the answer is no, and **she does not write it down**, and that is now the second line in a handling file that says an office told somebody the truth.

**Tuesday morning: a closing list of twenty-six claims, and two lines in it.** `DL/24/11031 · 22 CUTLER'S RISE · 4 (DATE OF LOSS) — the 9th dec` and `DL/24/11044 · 14 ANDER'S ROW · 4 (DATE OF LOSS) — 9 OR 10 DEC`. Neither is a date the instruction can use. **And the second of them is an itemised claim of seventeen hundred and forty pounds with no excess election, so the date is worth that household nothing** — two households that know exactly what happened to them and cannot put it in a box. ⚠ **The word is *not* printed in these ten chapters and the page records it. The moral floor was used and not said, and he did not say it, and the notebook says so at the foot.**

---

## Chapter 0112 — The Weight Column

**Wed 7 – Thu 8 Jul 2025, and it runs past twenty-three fifty-nine on the Thursday night, when the suspension expires, and nothing opens.** The only lawful way to get a page of a salvage docket book is to pay a claim the page supports, so **Marianne Cope asks Colin Bray for forty-nine dockets in a four-line letter** and he sends scans in about nine minutes, and gives the reason he has said no four times in seven weeks: a stranger would use it, and he has never in nineteen years known what anybody was going to use a page for. He also tells her, unasked, that he is owed money for December across about forty addresses by a managing agent whose head office is a floor above a man who took a call in February and said the same thing, and **she could do nothing about that either, and he did not appear to expect her to.**

**Four hours of arithmetic on forty-nine lines against the yard's weight tickets. Nine do not agree.** The yard weighs the lorry; the docket's number is the client's number; there is no scale in a front room on a Saturday. **The nine are 8 Cutler's Rise, 14 Ander's Row, 2 Cutler's Rise, 31 Cutler's Rise and four more she will not give the street of.** She keeps them in a pad in a locked drawer because if she finds out about them they stop being a line in a column and become nine women, and then the arithmetic becomes a decision, and she is not the person who decides.

**Four reasons none of the nine can be prosecuted, all correct:** nobody lied to the contractor; nobody lied to her, because the weight is not on her form at all and reaches her only after payment; there is no offence, because the act is a man writing what a woman said and the purpose was to get to Monday; **and if she pursues it she is pursuing nine households, every one of which is owed the same seven hundred and fifty pounds and none of which has ever had it.** The money is not stolen from those households, it is taken from a settlement pool about four hundred claims deep, and the four hundred waited.

**⚠ The turn, and it is three addresses inside two hundred metres of one another.** 14 Ander's Row is on the pad of nine weights that are not what a lorry carried, **and** is being paid seventeen hundred and forty pounds on Friday on an itemised list, **and** its fourth box says *9 OR 10 DEC*. 2 Cutler's Rise is on the pad **and** closed on Monday morning for want of a date. The machinery is one machinery pointed both ways: **both are a form wanting a number out of a person standing in a wet room on a bad day** — either the person cannot produce it and is refused, or the person produces the number the form wants and is paid, and the number was a guess, and the guess was not the person cheating either. It is not a fraud and an injustice being the same thing and us being harder or softer. **It is one habit.**

**Thursday night: the suspension expires at 23:59 and a notice is generated at four minutes past midnight by the office's own system, and it is not signed, and it says *no person is appointed by this notice*.** An expiry is not a permission and a permission is not a power and a power to enter a bonded store is a different instrument in a different building. **The tide came over the coping at about twenty past nine that morning, which is ground two, and ground two was still true at the moment the instrument stopped.** Yvette Carrow will sign a second instrument or not, in an office, on a Tuesday, and not on a quay, and she has told them in advance. **Ama Osei: nobody ever designed a piece of paper for the morning after, and a morning after is not a period, and it does not have a box in it.**

---

## Chapter 0113 — The Ground Floor, And Four Words

**Mon 12 – Tue 13 Jul 2025. Bankfoot, on the other side of the ring road, twenty days after the doorstep. Mara Iles (28), registrar, and a man of thirty-two on a step, and nobody writes a name down.** Her reason for coming is material and not moral: **a card wants a thing, a maker, a date, a material, a provenance and a place, and every line that has a person in it is somebody who agreed to be on it, and there has never been a line anywhere on a card for a person who was in a room on a night and did not agree to anything.** The Museum has twice told a body with a duty in writing that it holds no record of the premises, the district, the street or the people, and the answer was correct both times, and she has been sent to write down what the Museum would hold if it did hold something.

**The going is a carpet from about 1994, joists that a man looked at on Wednesday the second of July and said were sound at a cost of about eleven hundred pounds, and a boiler from about 1978.** And the telephone: **she rang Wharf Street on the twenty-fourth of June, and was asked whether she was in the flat on the eighteenth, and said yes, and the handler did not write it down, and was sorry, and said the eleven hundred pounds is not hers.** She has been extremely angry with that woman for three weeks and has now got off the telephone.

**The question, asked plainly: was there anybody in this building on Friday night besides you.** The answer is **four words** — *And I looked properly* — and it is not a yes and it is not a no, and it is the first time in thirty-one weeks that anything about a person being in a building on a night has been written down. The registrar reads it back; the woman makes her correct one correction, and the correction is the chapter: **you have written *found*. I did not go looking for anybody. There was nobody there. That is not a search and there is nothing in it to find.** It is changed in her own hand, initialled and dated.

**What she does with four words, over two days, and the three refusals.** Not a card (a woman in the label room with a surname only: you can card a bell, you cannot card a rope, and a person is worse than a rope because a rope does not read its own card back). Not a photograph. **Not a claim file** — because there is no settlement, no reference, no policy, and if it were accepted it would become item eleven on a schedule and in four years somebody would want to know who she was and there would be a name at the top of a page, **and she did not ask for a name and is not going to.** It is a sheet of A4 in a folder on the second shelf of a registrar's office, reference **`RG/26/4`**, the name line left empty on purpose with the form saying so, and **it is deliberately not in the schedule of collections anybody may ask for, because a person who has not asked to be held is not a collection.** **This is the first time in this case that a record has been made about a person, and it cost one person nothing.**

---

## Chapter 0114 — A Page Is Not A List

**Fri 16 – Sat 17 Jul 2025, nine miles upriver to the parish of Braeswick.** **Averil Shand, about sixty-eight, registrar of burials for the parish, forty-one years, a hardback of interments about four inches thick on a shelf in a vestry above a radiator with a dehumidifier under it.** A reach and a lock and a weir and a churchyard moved twice. **The registrar of twenty-eight says out loud inside four minutes that she is a registrar and he is a registrar and there is no relationship whatever between her book and hers, and that four of the nine objects she keeps records of are about people and none of those four are the people themselves.**

**The request is a list. The refusal is a page, and the reasoning is the manuscript's own argument in another mouth: a page is a record of what happened on one day at one churchyard, it has a date at the top, the names go down it in the order they came in, and the order has a shape a list has not got; a list is a set of names anybody can read in order, anybody is the whole of the problem, and a list cannot be accounted for.** She has given eleven lists in forty-one years. The first went out in 1986 to a woman forty miles away looking for a man drowned in 1949; the list was in date order; the woman worked from the front and did not get to it and told the family he was not there; **Averil Shand found the entry on the fourteenth of June 1990, on the page for the eleventh of March 1949, and the family have had a different story since and it is theirs.** Two of the eleven are in her minute book with dates and she has not got a column for what she lost.

**She will not let anybody photograph the book.** She photocopies one page on a multifunction printer, tears the stub off, and accounts for it. The page is **the ninth of December 2024**: four interments, and the fourth is **E. SOWERBY, 91, of The Paddock, Nether Braeswick** — *No memorial to be provided. Family cannot.* Eleven words in a column that is for a stone, and the registrar of twenty-eight says it is the first line she has ever read that tells her about a family in this city that could not pay for a stone, and that a column is doing a job about a person and does not know it. **And she can do nothing with it, in four words, which is the first time in this case that a competent person has said that in four words.**

**Emrys Tasker at the lock house, two hundred yards away, has known her for forty years and has never asked her the question. The churchyard wall has come down four times and every time a page goes missing out of her book.** He says he has been trying to say what she says since about 2014 in a worse way, to the water authority and to a man from the council, and never to anybody who was writing anything down, and then: *I want a knot in a line, which is the same thing, and is the reason I have got one and she has got a book.*

**⚠ And the thing nobody told her about.** A shut building **thirteen** miles downriver with about four hundred boxes in it. ⚠ **The review-fix pass of 2026-09-26 corrected this. Braeswick is nine miles upriver of the city and the wharf is canonically four miles downriver of the city, so from the parish the store is thirteen miles downriver and not four, and the flood district on the low bank is nine miles downriver of the parish and not forty. All four occurrences are now consistent and the store sits four miles below Ferry Bank, which is what Chapter 106 says.** **Mara Iles decided not to tell her, and the reason took about eleven seconds: a woman who has been asked for a list eleven times is the last person in this city who should be told there is another one.** Nobody improved on it.

**And the reading is put on the table, in a room, with a day on it, and the day is not this one.** On the Saturday morning, in the church hall: the note in the schedule of collections at the entry for plinth thirty-four, added the twenty-eighth of May, naming no person, saying *Municipal Works* — **and the two things nobody has read, the hand on the 1902 index card and the two entries in the day book for the week of the casting.** Shand names the decision: **a thing is read in a room, by people who are all in the room, on a day that is written down, and a note is kept, and the note is kept by somebody with a duty and not by the person who is frightened of it, and if the answer is that there is nothing on the card then the note says there is nothing on the card, in those words, and it is dated and signed.** A sheet is written for **Thursday 19 August 2025 at ten in the morning in the registrar's office, Civic Hill**, with a minute and two witnesses, and it goes into a bag in a cupboard in a vestry. **Neither unread source is read in this batch.**

---

## Chapter 0115 — The Instruction, And The Person Who Adopted It

**Mon 19 – Tue 20 Jul 2025.** A registrar of twenty-eight wrote on the fourth of June, was due on the second of July, was not chased for four weeks, was chased on Thursday the tenth, and the answer came on the nineteenth at twenty to ten. **It is a null, minuted as a null, on a printed form.**

> **CITY FLOOD OFFICE — CLAIMS INSTRUCTIONS. RETURN OF ENQUIRY. FORM CI/4.**
> **1. Is the section the product of a consultation? — NO. The section was not drafted for this instruction.**
> **2. Is there a drafting history? — NONE HELD. Adopted verbatim from the General Claims Manual (Domestic and Commercial), Part 9, in force from 2009 and not reissued. A general manual is not drafted in sections.**
> **3. Adopted into the instruction on 6 March 2011 by N. BRAITHWAITE, technical liaison, policy instructions, Claims Instructions Library, Wharf Street. No other officer adopted it.**
> **4. The adopting officer has been asked whether he holds any record of the purpose of the section. His answer is appended and is signed and dated.**
> **Form CI/4 is a return of enquiry and is not a record of any person. — issued 19 July 2025, 09:40.**

**Neil Braithwaite, fifty-eight, nineteen years in post, is not a villain.** He did not write section B and has never written it; he adopted it from a manual where it had stood in the same words for about six years, into a new instruction for a product expected to run three years. He read Part 5 as a whole once, in 2011, and has read section B since about six times. **If the words are wrong then the words are wrong in a manual that about four hundred offices use for about nine other products, and the correct place to raise that is the manual's owner, and it is not me and it is not you.** Asked whether he agrees with it, he says **no**, and says he had nineteen seconds to find a better sentence and had not got one.

**Marianne Cope, standing at the back in her coat because a claims handler has no business in a museum, gives the chapter's shape: the sentence is not his and not hers, and the answer is a man in a room who is responsible for the procedure, and the procedure has an owner in a building none of them has ever written to — and she could write this afternoon and is not going to, because a reissue would make four hundred and eleven wallets out of date and nobody who has had one since December would ever know.**

**The registrar's card, `RG/26/5`, and the instrument catching a person who has not asked to be caught.** She writes *alive* and *in post* and *no wrongdoing identified* and *no action proposed against any person* and *the section remains in force*. **Her reason for writing the name: the only instrument anybody in this case has ever had is a name against a date; if she does not use it on him she is only saving it for somebody worse, and she does not want to be a person who keeps it.** She did not photograph it, accession it or put it in the collections system, and says so in the record.

**And the trap, said out loud by the man of thirty-two, and not improved on: a sentence with no author cannot be argued with by anybody, because there is nobody to argue with.** If Braithwaite had written it there would be a person to ask whether he thought it was right. **And the notebook says he has broken the third line of his own first page to write a name and a date, and did not break it for the woman of forty-three in Bankfoot, and does not know whether he was right.**

---

## Chapter 0116 — A Table, Asked For Again, In The Wrong Mood

**Fri 23 – Sat 24 Jul 2025. A yard on the ring road, eleven o'clock on a Friday morning, a folding rule on the bonnet of a van. Thirty-eight days since **the fifteenth of June**, and the chapter does not pretend the gap is nothing.** ⚠ **The review-fix pass of 2026-09-26 corrected the anchor from the ninth of June to the fifteenth, in the section heading and in the dialogue, because thirty-eight days before Friday 23 July is Tuesday 15 June and forty-four days before it is the ninth, and Chapter 106 is dated Monday 14 – Tuesday 15 June. The thirty-eight days were right and the date they were counted from was not.** Elias Venn counts it and says it out loud; the engineer corrects the sentence he had been given — *I did not want to know when somebody was late. I wanted to be asked again.*

**The wrong mood is not rudeness, it is need. Yvette Carrow (48) comes in standing, with the form in her hand, because the second of the three grounds on her first instrument was a forecast tide exceeding the June mark within a period, the period has run out, the fact has not, and on Monday she has to measure a revetment joint and write four lines about whether a second instrument is necessary.** And there is no printed table anywhere that says how high the water has to be at a wall in Ferry Bank before it is inside. **She says in advance that this is not a favour to be refused: the mood is not a favour, it is a statutory officer with a bank to say something about and a form with a box in it, and the box gets filled in on Monday whether or not he says a word today.**

**The four reasons, in the order he has given them three times since March, and he says the order is the argument:** it becomes a standard and about four years later somebody prints a number off it on a reach where the bed is different; the offset moved about 140 mm between 1998 and 2016, the sheet says so in note four, and about nine people who have been handed it have not read note four; the only other two numbers for that reach are a painted board from 1911 and a government gauge with a hundred and fourteen years of chain of custody, and a committee will throw away the one with the man in it; and a number with a place on it stops being a measurement and becomes a claim about land. **And the fifth reason arrives, and he says the first time anybody has heard it: the sheet is not a document, it is a job, and he updates it about twice a year out of nine years of his own readings, and the second it is a document it stops being a job and becomes a thing that was true in 2016.** He says it is the only true one of the five, and that the other four are about what other people would do with it, and that he has let the man of thirty-two believe otherwise three times and should not have, because the fourth of the four is about a flood wall and it is not his to be afraid of. He gives one of six reasons and keeps five.

**And he does not say which way he goes.** The condition is not about the form: it is *tell me what happens to a street if the number is wrong* — because if a person carries it the sheet is a tool and he will send it tonight, and if nobody carries it and the number just sits in a form until somebody signs it, he wants a fortnight, and he says out loud that he has made the question harder on purpose so that he has time, and Carrow says **that is a trick**, and he says it is a man who wanted to be asked again in about four weeks. The wallet goes on the bonnet, latch side up, unopened, and back into a buttoned pocket.

**The pattern question is available for the sixth time and is not asked, and he says why, and Petherick says: you have nearly asked me that question about four times now, in three different rooms, in three months, and you have not asked it once, and I have watched you not ask it four times.** **And on the Saturday morning Carrow rings the yard and gets a woman in a hut who says he is out on the reach and will not be back until about two and does not tell anybody where, and leaves a nine-second message about the condition, and the chapter ends with it on a form on the back of a form and nobody knowing.**

---

## Chapter 0117 — A Shrine Record, And Somebody Who Asks A Question

**Mon 26 – Tue 27 Jul 2025. Hesta Rell, sixty-one, from a river parish, comes down a hill to a room above a shop in Fisher Lane — the first person in three volumes of this case who is not a municipal employee, and the first who asks Elias Venn a question instead of answering one.** She came because **Averil Shand wrote to her before sending the facsimile, one question in an envelope: does she recognise a name. She did not recognise it — she had telephoned the registrar herself on the eighth of December and told her a man of ninety-one had died at The Paddock with no next of kin inside four miles — and her four conditions include that if the page goes anywhere she is to be asked first.**

**The Last Cup is a room with a door and a table and a list of the four taped to the door frame, nineteen years, and before that three.** What happens in it: somebody who is dying and has nobody goes and is sat with, for as long as it takes, and one washes them and one tells the house, and if there is no house one walks about four miles to a register and gives the information. **It is that walk on the eighth of December that put a man of ninety-one on a page, and she tells them not to print what she said about the four days before he was found, and waits about nine seconds for them to walk round the line, and they do.**

**She will not give the list of the four, and the reason is this case's argument said by somebody who has never read this case: a list of names at an address on a date is a household; a household is a thing that can be attached to a schedule; a schedule pays a flat sum to a household; and four people who open a door on a Tuesday have no form, no reference, no office and no way of being summoned, so there is nothing to refuse you with.** A door on a Tuesday, a table, and four people who are not employees of anything, is the whole of the protection, and she says it is not more than that.

**Her question, six words, at about ten past four on the Monday: is a place allowed to say no.** Not a mystery, not a trap, and he has no answer and does not pretend to: **he has been refused four times in thirty-one weeks by people, a person refusing you is a shape he has got, and nobody has ever refused him a place, and he has written twice in his own book that a building cannot answer, and both of those were convenient.** She says it is the first honest thing anybody has said to her about it since March. **And she tells him she has got a second question, worse than the first, about a room with about eleven in it who do not agree about anything, and she is not asking it, and she wants about three weeks of watching him not know the answer to the first.**

**Tuesday: the shrine's own book, and the reading does not go well.** Dates down the left, what was done in the middle, **nothing on the right, and no names at all, and not as a protest** — the first time a name went in, one of the four wrote it in and then the four could not have a conversation about a Tuesday without somebody being frightened; the second time it was a house, and a house with a name on it and a Tuesday in it is the exact shape of the thing ruining this flood. **Mara Iles reads two pages and says it is better than anything the Museum produces and she can fill in five of six lines of a card and the maker is four people who have not agreed to be the maker of a record, and there is no line on a card for a room, and she has known that since 2019.** She then asks one question too many about the man of ninety-one, is told the truth correctly, and does not like it: she is told she did not ask his name and that is the worst of both positions, and that Shand should be asked again in September and not before, and that she came on a Monday to a room above a shop and did not once ask about a religion. **Neither of them uses the word they have both been avoiding since January, and the page says so in his own hand at the end.**

---

## Chapter 0118 — A Number, And Who Is Allowed To Have It

**Fri 30 – Sat 31 Jul 2025, in two rooms of a temporary office on Ferry Bank, eleven people, and the escalation is the paper.** **Rukhsana Iqbal, forty-seven, resilience coordinator, six years, convened it because it is her job and because three offices have each told her in writing they are producing something and because a parish registrar upriver refused a list in writing a week before.** She says the cost first and says that if the decision is to do nothing she will be the person who decided to do nothing, in writing, with her name on it, and she has never put her name on a *nothing* before.

**Four numbers on a whiteboard and three of them are nobody's business: about nine hundred addresses in salvage dockets (a book, not a list, on a quay nobody can get at); about forty claims at the point where item 4 is the obstacle (a handler's pencil number); Ferry Bank, about 1,900 tenancies, whose flood wall is older than the district's records; and a hardback of interments, about a hundred and forty years, not released as a list.** In pencil across the top: *these are four numbers and they are about four things and if anybody joins them up in a document I will resign.*

**The argument against a list, by a housing officer of fifty-eight in about nine sentences, every word correct: a list of nine hundred addresses in a district under flood emergency powers is not a list, it is an instruction with no verbs in it; every address is a place a person can be found at; three live instruments in this city work by address.** Her alternative is better than anything else in the room: **one sheet, one person holding it, no circulation, a name given out only in answer to a specific question about a specific address, with the name of the asker and the date recorded and no reason recorded, because the form has no box for a reason.**

**Shand's refusal of 22 July is read out in four sentences — two of them an offer and a boundary, and the offer is accepted and the refusal is not going to be appealed.** Then the sheet has to be built off the docket round, because that is the only source with an address and a date on a line — **and the docket round is the households who called a man with a clipboard.** A woman of seventy-nine came out onto her step in a cardigan on the fifth of June and said no to a crew in her own house in about forty seconds and was right, and the only line in nine hundred and six that records her is *declined by client, nothing removed*, which the contractor has twice called the best line in his book and worth nothing. **He was right then and he is right now, and it turns out that the best line in his book is good for exactly one thing: proving that a household was at risk and did nothing.** So the household is on the sheet *because she said no*, and in about four weeks somebody will knock with a clipboard, and there is no form that takes a knock back. **Cray says she has known since Tuesday night that she was going to be wrong about somebody, that she probably is right, and that in twenty-two years she has never had a column for a household that refused a man with a clipboard in a fortnight when it was frightened.**

**Colin Bray, invited because a person with a duty wrote to him for the third time in six weeks: a line of his is evidence of an event and a line of his is evidence of a person who was frightened, and he has been carrying both in the same column since December and nobody has ever asked him about the second.**

**The decision, on the Saturday at about eleven, is a *nothing*, signed by the woman who convened the meeting: no list is to be made, circulated, printed or entered into any system; one sheet held by Hazel Cray, not delegated; the parish refusal accepted and not to be appealed; the four numbers not to be joined in any document. What has been lost: nine hundred people, who can now not be asked about by anybody, and there is a piece of paper saying so on purpose.**

---

## Chapter 0119 — What The Form Does Not Ask

**Tue 3 – Wed 4 Aug 2025. Four professionals at one end of a table with page one of the 2011 instruction: Marianne Cope (34), Mara Iles (28), Hazel Cray (58), Yvette Carrow (48), and a man of thirty-two who says no before anybody asks.**

**He says no in a corridor on a Friday, in advance, and the reason is the one he has used since the first of June: the day he is the man who knocked on the four hundredth door, then in four years the practice at Sillick Lane and Cauldwell Buildings are the same place, because he will have put himself there.** Cray writes *not asked. says no first* on a pad and turns it round so eleven people can see it, and says on the Friday that he is useful and that there is no box on earth on which he is a party.

**Nine boxes, in order, and each decides something about a person.** **1** decides who may start a claim, and a notification cannot come from a neighbour or from a person who was in the building. **2** decides that the person filling it in is the person it is about, and that is the only identity operation in the business. **3** decides whose money it is, and is the only box anybody who has never set foot in the district can read, and the person filling it in does not have to know a single thing about the water. **4** is the date and it is answered. **5** and **6** choose the system and the figure. **7** wants a receipt for a wedding hamper, and the whole form runs on a person's recollection typed into a box, and nobody has ever said that out loud. **8** pays. **9 closes** — and Cray's sentence is the one the chapter carries: **a claim closes when the settlement is made, a settlement is made when the file is complete, and a file is complete when a person has stopped asking.** And the fact under it: **seven households on a weekly rent in four flats with a term to the thirtieth of November and no tenancy at all**, no document tying any of the seven to any of their own addresses, so box two could be filled and box four could not. She tells eleven people, and one of them is him, and she says she did not arrange that.

**They count, and the counting is the chapter: ten items, every one of them attributed to a named professional, none of them a view, all of them pulled out of a box.** Whether the person filling box two is the person who was there; whether anybody else was at that address; whether the household moved its paper out of the building; whether the address was occupied at all, in a district where a vacancy rate exists and is not on this form; whether a person at that address could be traced at all; whether anybody has ever asked; whether anybody was inside the premises on the night; when a household stopped being a household as against when it lost something; whether a person has agreed to be on the page; and whether a form for any of the above exists anywhere, which is no, in four hands.

**The moral floor is not said by anybody in the room, and the page says so.** Cope has a phrase and says it belongs to two women who are not in the room and is not hers. **Carrow's contribution: the reason Schedule F was the easiest thing she has ever signed is that she has no power in it to see anybody's claim or tenancy or letter, and the form for a household's claim has no box for who is in a building, and the form for who is in a building has no box for a claim, and the two do not touch. She puts her name against item seven — the first time anybody in this case has put their name against a gap.**

**Three decisions, three of them about stopping: the ten items are not circulated and one person holds them and no copy is made; nobody writes a form, because a form has an owner and a system and a mandatory field and in four years nine hundred claims will have it filled in by somebody who guessed; and the two forms would have to be the same form, and they belong to two bodies, and the boundary between them is about a hundred and forty years old.** He is the one person in the room who produced no copy, and he noticed it, and he wrote down that and only that.

---

## Chapter 0120 — Who May Speak For A Community

**Fri 6 – Sun 8 Aug 2025, three days, and the chapter is long because a question and a room do not fit into a day. The movement's turn, and it is a question and not an event.** About eight people in a room above a shop in Fisher Lane with the windows open, chaired at the end by Rukhsana Iqbal. **Yvette Carrow has a statutory publication to write in September with a paragraph beginning *the Recovery Forum was consulted by*, and the Forum has four names on it — a councillor, a parish clerk, a man who chaired a meeting in a hall in June, and a woman of eighty-one who keeps a hardback at 6 Sillick Lane — and not one of them is a household, and she had been unable to get past that for an hour.**

**Who may speak for a community.** It goes on the air at about nine o'clock on the Friday and stays unanswered for about eleven seconds. Cray's first answer is a councillor, because a councillor is elected — and then she takes it back in about nine sentences: a councillor can be written to, cannot refuse, and if a council is the voice of a community then every person in it is a person a councillor must speak about without their agreement. **And the sentence the chapter is built on: a community is not a thing, there is about one of them in this flood and about nine hundred others, and the only reason it looks like one thing is that it is on a map with one colour on it — and in twenty-two years she has watched about four hundred meetings where eleven people in a hall agreed for two hours and went home to four different streets, and the minute said *the meeting agreed*, and the meeting had not agreed, it was tired.**

**Item six of a list of six things the case has not done, read out at about half past nine.** The Museum holds nothing and the Director has said so in writing twice and nobody has written the nothing down; a second instrument is in a drawer on a floor above a shop in Fisher Lane; a letter to the owner of a general claims manual is unwritten; a boundary on a wall drafted in April with a bolt pen with a fifth line that is wrong on one sheet and is staying wrong; a decision about a person of about forty-three in a ward the case went into once; **and a hardback, about nine gaps and about four hundred more to come, kept by V. Pilling of 6 Sillick Lane, who is eighty-one, in a drawer that does not lock on purpose, with a date on the inside front cover and no names in the book at all.** Carrow says the first of October is fifty-six days away, that a man of fifty-two put that date on a wall in April and the keeper put the same date on a hardback about a fortnight later, that neither has ever said in a room what happens on it, and that she is the fourth person in this case to notice and the first with a statutory power and she is leaving it. **She is not here and is not going to be asked to be. He is the fifth, and has noticed since the twenty-eighth of June, and asks for the fifth line on the wall to be read out, and it is, and it is wrong, and she has said in a room that she is not going to take it off.**

**Hesta Rell, on her second visit, is asked, and gives an answer that is a way of behaving and not a person: the one who is there when it is inconvenient. And then she says, before anybody decides it is an answer, why it is not one** — about eleven of us in three parishes who do not agree about anything, and in about four the person is four miles away, and in about four more it is a woman of eighty-one who will not come, and in the remaining three it is her, and she is sixty-one with a bad knee and is not a community. **She has been asked this question three times in her life — a magistrate's clerk in a church hall in 2011, a woman from a council in 2019, and this — and has not answered it on any of the three, and the only improvement in fourteen years is that she has got better at saying no.** And she says she has got a second question, worse, about a room with eleven in it, and is not asking it tonight, and will get up and go down the hill if somebody asks it. Nobody asked her a second question.

**Six other answers, all competent, all refused, each by somebody competent: write to every household (nine hundred letters and about nine hundred returns and the first thing anybody does with a form is put a box in it); add a fifth name from the ground floors (a name of a woman who was at her door when a man with a clipboard came, and there are nine of those, and a name is a thing you are given); the Museum supply a name (a building being careful is not a record); the contractors' book; nothing be said and the paragraph read *the City Flood Office has consulted with the residents* (a false statement, and the typist will not type it); and his, which is nothing, and the reason — that if he says who may speak for a community then in four years a man in a room can say the man who said it had no post, and he would be right.**

**The ruling: the paragraph will name nobody.** It will say the office has not established whom it may speak for, that it is asking, and that the address for that is a post box on Wharf Street and **not a form**, and nobody writes a form, because a form becomes a standard and a standard is read by people who were not in the room and about four hundred and eleven people were handed a photocopy of a page of a manual in 2011 and none of them read note four.

**Saturday: six sheets, read out loud in a corridor on Wharf Street to a man of thirty-two and not to the claimant.** ⚠ **The review-fix pass of 2026-09-26 replaced this section. As first written it was a registrar of twenty-eight reading a claim file out loud to a woman of sixty-one who is owed something, and telling her the truth — which is Chapter 110's closing construction, seven chapters earlier, and the batch card had forbidden exactly that repetition. The file is now read to Elias Venn, who is owed nothing, and what the section turns on is that the claimant cannot be told: the office did one decent thing on 8 June, it is on the office's own handling file and not the claimant's, and putting that line in front of her would hand her a page of a file she is not entitled to, and every other page of it, including a decision with two lines on it that says nothing at all and that she would read as a no.** Six sheets, a decision of the fourth of June with *item 4 not completed, no evidence sought, claim to be processed as an open claim at the next scheduled review*, **which is not a refusal and is the most expensive kind of thing there is — a form left open for nine weeks and two days while the claimant arranges her life around assuming it is coming.** And a note in a handling file in the handler's own hand, dated the eighth of June: *applicant advised re item 4; no reason recorded.* **The only line in about nine hundred files that says an office told somebody the truth, on the office's file and not the claimant's, and the claimant does not know it exists, and there is no form for telling her.** The section ends on a knock: somebody has to decide on Monday whether to ask her for one thing, and asking a person for one thing is a knock on a door, and there is a sheet in a drawer in a temporary office with about nine hundred knocks on it and a housing officer of fifty-eight's name at the top of it.

**Sunday morning: the two pieces of paper.** Elias Venn puts them on Kathleen Orrey's counter in Fisher Lane at about ten past eleven, folded in half four times, unopened, and says so before she touches them, and says plainly that they were never evidence, never a plan, and never going to be in anything, and that he has had them in a coat for nine weeks. **Then the answer, out loud, in her shop, to the only person in this city who is owed something and who has asked him nothing about it:** a date of loss is for attaching a flat sum to a household; the excess is seven hundred and fifty pounds once per household per event; the date she can produce is the ninth of December; the office will not take a photograph; the letter was not the right kind of letter; the document that would have done it was in a tin on top of a wardrobe in a ground floor with water in it; **so she is owed seven hundred and fifty pounds and is not going to get them, there is no appeal, and the reason is not anybody's fault and he cannot fix it and neither can the handler.** She asks him what changed and says she does not want an answer, because if he tells her she has to decide whether it was a good reason. He says it was not a good reason: it was that she was owed them, nobody comes back, and he did not want to be the first man in this city to find that out for himself. **She tells him to set them down, go on, and not come back, because she has watched him twice and the second time he came with the thing he said he would not come with, and she would rather he went and stayed away.** He says a different woman told him the same in a different month and he did not do it then either, and she says he will have to learn it twice and she is not going to teach him.

**And the minute, which is the first in thirty-five weeks and the first document in this case that records something that did not happen.** Mara Iles telephones a registrar of sixty-eight at about eleven o'clock on the Friday night and asks her to take it; Shand writes it up on the Sunday in a minute book that belongs to a parish, records that the question was put by Y. Carrow at about nine o'clock and not answered by anybody, that H. Rell gave an answer which is a description of conduct and not of a person and said herself it was not an answer, that six other answers were offered and each refused by a person competent in their own trade, and that the chair ruled that the paragraph shall name nobody — **and item four of it says she has not been in the room, has been told nothing else, and would like both of those read out to whoever reads it and read out to her, and that she knows it already, which is not the same thing.**

---

Every weekday in the batch was derived by script from the pinned line (real Gregorian plus two days on the weekday) before a chapter was written, and the derivation is on the page in Chapter 121's notebook with the whole of August and September spelled out. Where a summary and a chapter disagree, the chapter governs.**
