## Chapter 0121 — A Second Instrument, Or Not

**Mon 9 – Tue 10 Aug 2025.** The wharf, the joint, the tape, and a woman of forty-eight deciding in an office on a Tuesday. **Monday morning on the quay: Ama Osei with a steel tape and Dinah Vassallo, 41, a structural engineer appointed under the June instrument.** A movement joint in the revetment coping reads **13 mm at the widest on 9 August** and read **9 mm on 24 June**, same tape, and the finding is the rate and not the number. A crack about two metres above it, a telltale on it, and a surveyor's card pushed into the same crack at about two o'clock on the afternoon of **Wednesday the fourth of August** by somebody nobody can name. **The man of thirty-two is a stranger on a quay and is moved off the tape line before anything else happens.** He asks Vassallo one question and it is not the object-or-place question: whether a person writes on a wall on a weekday morning because something is happening or because something has already happened. *A person writes that on a weekday morning because something is happening, and I have never once written one because something had finished.*

**Monday afternoon, the first notebook entry of the batch, and the batch's live thread is in it:** a message about a man's condition has been in a hut on a yard since the morning of **Saturday the twenty-fourth of July** and nobody knows whether it was collected, and he does not ring him, **and the reason is that the man asked to be asked again in a different mood and a mood is not a thing a man of thirty-two is entitled to ask for twice.**

**Tuesday morning, four lines in a file, read out loud, and the fourth line is the volume:** *there is no printed table in this county that states the level at which water enters the Ferry Bank district behind this wall, and the wall is older than the district's records.* **A statutory officer cannot turn a moving wall into a number a street can be judged by, and the man who could has said he will not print a height for a street that has not got one, and she is right, and she has written it down.** She says the cost out loud twice and asks to be stopped and gives them about nine seconds. She signs `CFO/25/SF/0231` at 11:41.

⚠ **THE TURN, AND IT IS THE CLAUSE.** The second instrument names **the bonded store and all outbuildings, yards, quay and revetment adjoining, wharf, district of Ferry Bank; and the retaining wall at the wharf frontage and the frontages behind it for a length of about four hundred metres**, in force **from 06:00 on Sunday 15 August 2025 to 23:59 on Tuesday 31 August 2025**. **Clause 4 — no person shall enter any premises comprised in this Schedule except under a licence issued by this office — has been in the form since 2019 and has never been about a person, because for six years it has only ever been about a building.** A registrar of twenty-eight says it: *it is not that you have signed a worse instrument; it is that you have signed the same instrument about four hundred metres instead of one building and the form noticed before either of you did.* **A licence is about a person. There is no knock in it, no letter, and no box, and a household cannot apply for one, because a household is a person at an address on a date and this form has no box for a date.** Item seven of the sheet of ten, which a housing officer of fifty-eight put her name against on 4 August, is now a clause in an instrument.

**The store is shut, the padlock is new, the shutter on the office two doors along is down, and nobody on that quay wanted the doors opened and the person who would have opened them has been in a boat once already.** The notebook carries the full derived day map for August and September 2025, the sentence *derived, and I am writing down that I derived it*, and the note that the reading of the 1902 card is on Thursday the nineteenth of August at ten in the morning, which on that list is a Thursday.

---

## Chapter 0122 — A Roll Is A Name And A House

**Wed 11 – Thu 12 Aug 2025. Marlbank Primary, built 1931, two storeys, on the low bank, about three hundred and eighty children.** The reason the two of them are in a school on the Wednesday morning is clause four, signed the day before: **their frontage is four hundred metres back from a wall and the wall has moved four millimetres since June and nobody knows what four millimetres means, and a premises re-survey of nine buildings on that strip cannot be booked into the four hundred metres without a licence.** The council estates officer is **Douglas Nairn, 44**; the school business manager is **Ina Mordaunt, 51**, nineteen years in the building and sixteen typing the roll.

**She puts the roll on the table face down and says before anybody asks for it that she is not going to give it to anybody, and the reason is not modesty: *a school roll does not go anywhere. It has not gone anywhere in sixteen years. Nobody has asked me for it in sixteen years, not the council, not an inspector, not a police officer, not once, and I have never had to say no, and if I hand it to a room like this one then in about four years there will be a document somewhere that says three hundred and eighty children were on a low bank in June, and nobody will remember what we used it for, and neither will I.*** ⚠ **She is the person who refused, and the man of thirty-two is not the person who refused and is not going to be.**

**And the two of them cannot be made to talk, and the reason is not that either is wrong.** He has four hundred and six lines, one per building, with a level on each, and a matching tool that would do the join in about nine minutes and may not, and the rule is not his and its exceptions are in a schedule in somebody else's office. She has three hundred and eighty lines, every one of them a name and a house, typed out of a form a parent filled in when the child was between three and four. **And a registrar of twenty-eight gives them the answer in a school and says afterwards that she does not know whether it was a kindness or an evasion: the join is a household, and a household is a person at an address on a date, and a child at an address is a dependant, and a dependant cannot claim, so the most legible record of who lives on a low bank in this district is a record of about three hundred and eighty people who cannot claim anything.** Nobody asks her to repeat the second floor and nobody in the room says it.

**The caretaker, Colin Prowse, 63, tells them about the wall unasked, in about nine sentences, in a doorway.** A line of white emulsion on the gym wall, twenty-three inches off the skirting, with the date written under it in his own hand on **Friday the ninth of July 2025**. *You cannot take twenty-three inches off a floor and put it on a river. You would have to know where that floor is and I have never been told and I have asked twice, and both times I was told it is not my building, and it is not my floor, and I am the caretaker.* **It is the only date that school has and it cannot be checked and the man who could check it will not.**

**On the Thursday morning she offers to do the match by hand on the Thursday afternoon with a volunteer and a housing officer of fifty-eight refuses in one sentence, and the sentence is that it would be a list of about four hundred children at about four hundred addresses in a drawer in a school office, and a school office is a room a person can walk into.** Then she does the thing anyway, having known since ten past two that she was wrong: **one question at one door of eleven households, *is there anybody at your address who is not on our roll and who we should know about*.** Four said no, three gave a name, one refused to be written down, two were not at home, and one said on the doorstep that nobody had told her she did not have to say anything since the flood. **Eleven answers in a drawer in a first-floor office and there is no form in nineteen years for a drawer, and the school cannot answer a subject access request because it is not a data controller of anything.** Nobody asked a child anything in two days.

---

## Chapter 0123 — Nineteen O'Clock, In A Room, With The Windows Open

**Wed 18 – Thu 19 Aug 2025, and the reading is on the second day, at the hour it was written down for.** Wednesday: a bag comes out of a cupboard in a vestry nine miles upriver, and the registrar of sixty-eight has gone to move her car and left the door on the latch. **On the shelf above the radiator, four inches thick, a linen tape and a pencil in a loop and nothing on the spine: the hardback of interments, about a hundred and forty years, which nobody in that parish knows about except her. The man of thirty-two reads it for about four minutes and does not touch it, and it is the plant for Chapter 128 and it is not spent cheaply.** He gets everybody, in no order, and cannot tell one thing about anybody, and can tell that somebody in that parish was very tired in about 1931 and has no way of knowing whether that is true. **He stops it at about two minutes in by putting his hand flat on the shelf and deciding he had read enough, and that took about nine seconds, and it is the first time in thirty-six weeks he has been able to stop a reading at a moment of his own choosing, and he did it without a word, and he has no word and nobody is going to give him one.** A registrar of twenty-eight says *tell me the reason and put nothing else on the page* and he does not write it down, and she does not argue, which was new.

**Thursday at ten, in a registrar's office on Civic Hill, whose telephone line has been disconnected since about 2019 on purpose.** Two witnesses: **Fenn**, of the label room, surname on a badge, who reads handwriting for a living, and **Emrys Tasker**, who came nine miles on the first bus and has known the parish registrar for forty years and has never asked her the question. **Elias Venn is there because the registrar wrote on the sheet in advance that a man with no post was told he was required to attend, and nobody laughed at it.**

**The finding is a null in seven numbered items and item five is the one that took four attempts to word: *no comparison of the hand on this card with the hand on the card at plinth thirty-four has been made, and none can be made in this room. The comparison is not made. It is not declined. It is not available.*** Four characters of a name survive and are not read aloud and are not reproduced. The figure below them is two letters, no year and a number, and a Museum accession reference carries a series year. **The two day-book entries for the week of the casting: one complete, one with no return, and a column headed *Witness* that is empty in both lines.** Tasker: *a day book is a record of what came back and no record at all of what did not.* Fenn puts a guess on the page and marks it a guess and says **he would rather it was on the page than in his head.**

**The chapter's real content is the minute.** A registrar of sixty-eight who has kept a minute book for forty-one years and has never minuted a meeting she was not at gives two reasons, and the second is better than the first: *the first reason was about me. The first reason was: I am not going to be asked for a whole of a meeting and get nothing, and that is a reason about a woman of sixty-eight and what she is owed. The second reason is that a minute is not a thing you do for the person who asked you. A minute is a thing you do so that a person who was not in the room, and will not be born for another fifty years, can be told what was decided and on what day and by whom.* And the reason she knows: in 1986 she gave a woman forty miles away a list and the woman told a family he was not there, and Shand found the entry on 14 June 1990, and the family have had a different story since and it is theirs. **She keeps both reasons in the minute, because the person who does it next in that parish is about thirty.**

**Thursday at nineteen hundred, in the same room, with the windows open, because the day had been hot. The minute is read out four times and a registrar of twenty-eight says it is not what the registrar of sixty-eight would have written if she had been in the room, and gives the reason, and refuses to say the boundary.** The man of thirty-two nearly says the moral floor and does not, and the page says in his own hand that he has wanted to say it since eleven o'clock on the Wednesday morning with his hand on a shelf.

---

## Chapter 0124 — The Householder And The Form

**Fri 20 – Sat 21 Aug 2025. A room on the second floor of a building on Wharf Street, a claims file, a form, a claims handler of thirty-four, a loss adjuster of thirty-nine, a housing officer of fifty-eight, and a man with no post.** `DL/24/11044`, **Priscilla Nunn, 66**, and her brother **Gerald Nunn, 59**, at 14 Ander's Row, eleven and twenty-two years, not married, **a policy that has been in his name since 2011 because he was the one at the insurance office when they came round.**

**The opening sentence is said before anything is opened: your claim is itemised under section five, you have not elected excess settlement, and item four is mandatory, so the date of loss, if you produced one, perfectly properly, today, would attach a settlement of nothing to your household.** And the reason: *the box is not the reason you have had nothing. The box is standing in the way of a decision, and the box would pay you nothing, and both of those are true at the same time, and I have said them in that order to about four people in a room since the fifth of July and never to a claimant.*

**The whole apparatus works exactly as printed.** Box two wants one name and a household is two, and the one who is not the policyholder is not a person to the excess and a person to everybody else in the building. **A page-four contents schedule is a piano, a sewing machine and a wedding hamper, seventeen hundred and forty pounds, paid on Friday 11 July, no excess election.** A housing officer of fifty-eight gets to page four and says the sentence the chapter is built on: **a page four is a list of what a person thought of, and it is not a list of what a house contained, and it never has been, and if you ever write one you are asking a person standing in a wet room to go through every room in a house.** And then: **page four does not contain a nine-year-old's room, and it could not, because nobody has ever asked a person what was in a room, and there is no form in this city for a list of rooms.**

**And the water came twice.** He says the ninth and she says the ninth and the tenth, and the tenth is the piano. **Section A pays once per household per event; section C attaches an excess to an event by reference to a date and not otherwise; there is one box.** A claims handler of thirty-four says the whole of it and says she cannot record the tenth anywhere because there is no excess to record it against, and that deciding the two events were the same event is a judgement about water and not about paper, and **a judgement about water is the one thing this form was designed to keep out of it.** And then the sentence that is worse than everything else in the chapter: **if you had elected the excess, and if you could produce a date, and if the date were the day you wanted, then I could pay you seven hundred and fifty pounds for the ninth and seven hundred and fifty pounds for the tenth — and I have said that out loud in a room and about four hundred people in this city have just lost the ability to produce a date at all.**

**Saturday morning, the thing obtained on purpose that is not a name.** The household asks **R. Vellacott of number sixteen**, eight years in the house, to write down what he heard, because a woman in an office said on Friday that a date has to be evidenced. He writes two lines at his own kitchen table. The first is a piano going over at about half past seven on the evening of the tenth of December through a bit of missing wall under the stairs. **The second line is his and nobody asked him for it: there were three people in that house from the eighth to the fourteenth of December, two of them the Nunn family, and the third is a child of nine who is at her grandmother's in Northgate and who was in that house every night of the flood, and he fed her a boiled egg on the Wednesday because there was no gas.** He would not let the paper go and would not let them have a copy, and said *that is not yours to give to anybody and it is not mine either*, and the household carried it back at four o'clock and put it in his drawer. **It is the only document anybody in this city has ever obtained about this flood that says a child was in a house, and no office can take it, and it has a man's name and a man's address on the bottom of it and he did not ask to be held.** She comes to a door in Low Ward at about half past six to tell him so, and will not come in. **He does not say the moral floor with four witnesses on a doorstep and writes down that the reason was a room and not a rule.**

---

## Chapter 0125 — What A Signature Is For

**Mon 23 – Tue 24 Aug 2025. The first licences under the second instrument, and a form with seven boxes and a promise in one of them.** **A licence is not a claim. Seven boxes: applicant, premises, purpose (a)–(e), period, a recording duty, a boundary, and *a licence is not a person and is not recorded as one*.** Clause 6 — it confers no right of entry on any person other than the applicant — is the line a registrar of twenty-eight puts her finger on: **a household cannot be an applicant, cannot be a witness, and cannot be a person in receipt, because a household is a person at an address on a date and this form has no box for a date**, and the person who rang an office on 11 August and was told to find somebody with a duty to be the applicant has been doing that for eight days and has not found anybody. **Box (e), *other, describe*, closed one of those in eleven days on Friday 1 August, and the note that made it work is in the office's system and not on her form.**

**Tuesday morning, the chapter's centre. Yolanda Speke, 41**, a first floor at 18 Ander's Row, nine years, and her husband came home from the hospital on the fourteenth of June. She has read the form, which she says nobody does. **She signs the receipt line and asks what it is for, and a claims handler of thirty-four gives the answer that is the batch's whole argument in about forty seconds: a signature is so that if anybody in about four years says this was never done to this house there is a person named on a piece of paper who did it and can be asked whether it was.** And the half nobody could finish: **if anybody says she did not let him in, then there are two names and somebody has to decide which of them they believe, and that is the whole of what a signature is, and I have said it to about nine hundred officers in training and not once to the person it is about.** She asks what it is for, and is told, and says **that is worth having**, and writes the reference on the back of her hand with a biro because she has nothing to write it with. **And she says, unasked, that she has signed for two men in nine years and never knew there was a line to sign on, and that a woman of fifty-one had told her she did not have to say anything and that was the first anybody had said to her since the flood.**

**Tuesday afternoon, a man of twenty-five who is a systems technician and not a flood officer, in the room for nine minutes before he speaks.** **Ivo Pell's condition of 1 December 2024 — every photographic exposure of collections material by a member of staff countersigned at the point of making — is the same instrument as a witness box**, and he says what a second person is in nine sentences: somebody who can be asked afterwards, in a room, with nothing in front of them, whose answer is the only thing that will hold anybody's account up. **And then he says the thing nobody in the case has said to Elias in thirty-eight weeks: that he has said no to five things in five weeks and every one was a thing that would have made him useful, that he has made himself into a man who cannot be used in the one place where being used is a legal instrument, that he does not think he is wrong, and that he wants it minuted by somebody who does not like him.** And: **nobody can change the form, and the one thing you can do about a box that is going to be filled in by somebody who does not know what it is is that the second person on the first one is somebody who does know, and you leave a note where it will be found in 2031.**

**The man of thirty-two refuses box eight. Seven times in six weeks, and the reason has not changed since the first of June, and it is the third line of his own first page.** He says the reason is that he saw a knock on a door on the sixteenth of May and did not write it down, and that if he signs then he is a second person on a licence in a city where about nine hundred people were on a book in June and are on nothing now, and he does not think he has the standing to be the person who is asked about a form. **Ivo Pell does not argue.** Hazel Cray writes four words on a pad, does not turn it round, then turns it round. **And she adds the half she has been carrying for four weeks: a goods-out docket in October last year had a witness line that said *present at removal: name, grade, time, signature*, that line went unsigned for a fortnight and got signed, that she has signed about nine thousand of them, that she could not have told you for one of them what the signature meant, and that if you had asked her in October she would have said it meant she was there — and it means she was there and it means she can be asked, and the difference is the whole of what a man of twenty-five has been talking about, and it is about four hundred years old.**

---

## Chapter 0126 — The Public Room, And The First Attempt

**Fri 27 – Sat 28 Aug 2025. The first time this case has done anything in public, and it fails in public at twenty past eleven on the Saturday morning, in front of eleven people, and nobody in that hall is a fool.** A table at the end of a hall in a **branch library on Ferry Bank**; **Yvonne Petch, 51**, branch manager, twenty-six years, four conditions, one of them that nobody under about sixteen comes to that table, and **she said on the Friday morning that she would take a child to the side desk herself and would rather hear the objection now than at eleven o'clock on Saturday morning with forty people looking.**

**Friday afternoon, five people break a rule signed on 31 July, and say so out loud before they break it.** A printed sheet with three boxes — *your address, as now and as in June* · *a day and a month on which your own front door stopped being a way in and out* · *one thing that was in the house and is not now; a thing is a thing, a name is not a thing, a person is not a thing* — and a cardboard box for one thing you have found and do not want. **A housing officer of fifty-eight says the sentence she has been waiting five weeks for: that a sheet with a box that says *your address* is a list of addresses being made, printed, circulated and entered into a system, and item one of the minute she signed says it is not, and she signed it, and she read item one when it was read out and said the minute contradicts itself and the chair said the minute stands as drafted, and she has been carrying that for four weeks.** And she puts her own name at the bottom of the sheet. **Rukhsana Iqbal, from a car: *if the paragraph will name nobody then the sheet must ask nobody, and I will not be in a room where it does, and do not put my name on it — and if you do it, write down that you did it, because you will be the one who has to live in it.*** That sentence is in a mobile telephone in a car and is not in any file.

**Saturday. About thirty-one people through the door in four hours. Nine sheets.** A woman of about fifty comes back twice. The first time to ask whether the reference at the foot is a claim number, and is told it is not a claim and is not a form and that there is no claim. The second time she puts the sheet down and says *I have put my sister in the box and I want to know if that was stupid*, and a claims handler of thirty-four says **it is not stupid, it is the only box on that sheet with anywhere to put them, and I printed that sheet and I did not print a fourth box, and there is no fourth box on any form in this city, and the reason there is no fourth box is that a person is not a thing** — and cannot give her the paper back, and will not ask her one further question, and asks for it to be written down in a book somewhere that a woman of about fifty put her sister into a contents box in a public hall because a man of thirty-two printed her a sheet.

**Eight of the nine are a household that already exists in a book somewhere. The ninth is a ground floor at 12 Ander's Row with five people in it on the tenth of June.** Then the box. **A branch manager will not have thirty-one unknown objects in her corridor, and she is entirely right, and the reason is a fire door and a man from facilities who empties it on a Monday without reading anything on it.** Eight people take their own back in the ten minutes after two o'clock. Twenty-two stay. A registrar of twenty-eight takes the box, and says the reason out loud: **a thing somebody found in a box in a branch library has no maker, no date, no material, no provenance and no place, and there is no line on a card for it, and about four hundred years of practice says it goes in a box and not in a case, and the reach rope is on a hose hook for the same reason.** Among the twenty-two: **a door key on a fob with a plastic ring and nothing written on it, which nobody claimed and nobody can be asked about.**

**And the batch's worst-affected and least-culpable man writes it out himself: he took the three boxes off a list of ten, printed them for about eleven pounds, and nine addresses went into a coat pocket on the Saturday afternoon because he was the only person who could hold them without creating a record, and that is a list, and he is the list, and the minute says one person holds the only sheet and she is a housing officer of fifty-eight and there are now ten of us holding ten things and one of us is a man in a coat.** A woman of sixty-six comes to a door at about ten past six and says **you have a door on your mother's shop and no post, so you are the man who holds the box, and there is nothing in the world that says a man with no post may not hold it, and nothing in the world that says anybody else may.**

---

## Chapter 0127 — A Box On The Back Of The Form — **THE MIDPOINT REVERSAL**

**Mon 30 Aug – Wed 1 Sep 2025, three days, and it is the centre of the volume.**

**Monday, the specimen.** A registrar of twenty-eight asks for a blank form twice in the same letter — the least remarkable request anybody in this city makes — and gets one, with a note on the envelope saying that the reverse of the office's own photocopy was blank in the office's file and **that this is not evidence about the form, since paper of that age has a habit of losing ink on one side.** She put the second one in a drawer at ten past ten and did not sleep. **The form is printed on one side. The other side is printed too, on the same press, with the same form number in the corner:**

> **RECORD STATUS — DISTRICT REGISTER. Ferry Bank. To be completed by the district register at Northglass. Not completed by this office.**
> **STATUS AT (date): ( ) OCCUPIER RECORDED ( ) NO OCCUPIER RECORDED ( ) NOT ASSESSED**
> **PERIOD AFFECTED: from ............... to ...............**
> **This block is completed by the district register. It is not a determination and it may not be appealed. It is a status. A person may hold a status and a person may hold two statuses at once.**

**Nobody says anything for about eleven seconds and the eleven seconds are not disbelief.** And the bottom line is a reassurance printed for a duty officer in April, and it means that a household can be recorded as occupied on a Tuesday and as not occupied on a Wednesday, and both are true, and the register does not think that is a problem.

**Tuesday at nine, the reading changes out loud, in a room above a shop in Fisher Lane, and what changes it is a piece of stationery and not a person.** Eight people: a resilience coordinator of forty-seven who convened it because the block affects her district and who read it on the Friday and said she was not going to pretend she had not; the woman of forty-eight who signs the front; a registrar of twenty-eight; a claims handler of thirty-four; a housing officer of fifty-eight; a typist who would have to write the September page; a duty officer of forty-seven from Northglass with nineteen years in post; and a man of thirty-two with no post. **And the reading that was wrong, in the mouth of the person who was wrong: *I have been reading a body of this city for three months as a body that keeps things, and every single thing it has done has been about a person, and a body that keeps a record is not a body that keeps a person.* She wrote two pages on it and threw both away, because both of them said what it is, and a reading is the thing this case has got wrong three times.**

**Then the thing that makes it a reversal and not an accusation.** She asks for the back of one she signed. There are four Schedules in the file and she has never turned one over. **She says it out loud: she said a version of this in a corridor in June, to a man who had not asked — that she has no power in the form to see anybody's claim or tenancy or letter or photograph, and that she is not suspending access to a building, she is suspending access to a building without ever having to know who is in it, and that this is the easiest instrument she has ever signed and she would like somebody to explain why that is a bad thing. Nobody explained it to her. And she has a better sentence now and it is worse: *I did not need anybody to explain it to me. I asked for an explanation because I wanted to be told I was wrong, and nobody in this case was going to be rude to a statutory officer, and a woman of fifty-one in a corridor was rude to me about something else and I have not stopped thinking about that either.*** And she says the sentence at the foot of the front of the form is the most accurate sentence on it, and that it is a statement about the front, and that it is also a warning addressed to her, and that it says the definition of a person changes on the other side of the paper.

**And a duty officer of forty-seven tells them the true thing, and the true thing is worse than the false thing anybody might have expected her to say.** A status is not a determination; it goes on a frontage, and a frontage is a line on a map, and it goes on nothing a person can see. **It is for computation.** A flood emergency generates events, and an event generates claims, and a claim attaches to a household, and a household attaches to an address, and a frontage with four hundred open claims on it is a frontage a computer will not settle a district boundary against. **Nine used since 24 June; she has signed eight; she will sign the ninth on Wednesday. The front of the form chooses the frontages, and the person who signs the front does not know a status is attached to each address behind the premises, and the block says so on its own face.** She says *I am not the villain in this* and a housing officer says *you are the woman who made the form wider*, and that a man of forty-four being right about a column of numbers and a man of fifty-five being right about a height is the same shape, and the shape is a person doing their job with a box in front of them.

**Wednesday, the bank, and she is right about that too.** A contractor reports the coping at the north end dropped about eleven millimetres since 4 August. **She says she has now been right about the water four times and the water has never once been the thing anybody is fighting her about, and she has started to find that she does not know what to do with being right.**

⚠ **And the second half, which is Volume 02's second half in a new key, and it is the reason the first half is survivable.** There is a household in this flood that nobody is owed anything for. **It is owed nothing, it has claimed nothing, it is not on the docket round because it did not call a man with a clipboard, and its only trace anywhere in this city is one line in a contractor's book that says a crew did not attend and removed nothing.** It is not in a claims file, not on the recovery page, not on the Forum, not in a parish register, not on a school roll. **It is behind four hundred metres of wall, and no body in that room can name it, and therefore no body in that room can object on its behalf, and that is not a failure of the meeting, that is how it is designed to work.** And the man of thirty-two notices, for the seventh time since 28 June, that a man of fifty-two and a woman of eighty-one put the same date in two places and neither has said in a room what happens on it — because the block has a period in it, and a period ends, and the first of October is thirty days away — **and he does not ask, and a woman of forty-eight with a statutory power says out loud to nobody in particular that she is still leaving it.**

**And in a corridor at about six in the evening he decides something nobody asked him for.** Nobody in this case will ever be able to say he was asked. **He is going to look for the household that no form can name, by reading a thing that nobody owns, in a room above a shop in Fisher Lane, and a registrar of twenty-eight tells him from a doorway that she is not going to tell him not to, and that she knew about it on Friday the twenty-fifth of July and said nothing for three days, and would like both of those in his book and neither of them to be an excuse. He did not write it down. That was the third line, and it held.**

---

## Chapter 0128 — A Door Key On A Fob

**Thu 2 – Fri 3 Sep 2025. The first time in his life that his power has injured a living person, and it is not supernatural and it is not a curse and it is not a god. It is a man believing a feeling.**

**Thursday at about eleven at night, the room above the shop in Fisher Lane, a grey box in the middle of the floor and twenty-two things in it.** The fan on, his back to the open window, his shoes off, which he has never done and does not know why he did. **He counts nine minutes without touching anything by the bus.** A brass doorstop, a spectacle case with one lens, four hair slides on a card, a child's shoe, a tea towel, a purse with about two pounds, a tin of screws, a man's belt, a dog collar, a plaster, a photograph frame with the back off and no photograph in it, eleven keys and a plastic ring — **and a door key on a fob, silver, worn bright at the shoulders, on a moulded plastic fob with no number and no initial and no tag, on a split ring about two inches across, with nothing written on anything in it.** And the reason he is allowed to touch it, and it is not a supernatural permission: **a thing that is not a record cannot be given to a person and therefore cannot be denied to one, and a man with no post is the only person in this city who may hold it without it becoming a case.**

**About eleven seconds, and it does not come in gradually, which is what frightened him.** A hall. A torch on a stair. **A woman's hand on the other side holding a key up at the height of a shoulder, and the hand is not frightened, and that is the first thing and it is not a fact.** And three words, the clearest thing that has ever come out of an object in this city: ***I'm Marguerite. Go on, then.*** And the door going, and rain on stone, and somebody out. **And then the part he did not see coming, which took eleven seconds off him and put a nosebleed on the counter: the key being turned from the inside by somebody who came back in out of the rain, and the bolt, and a hand flat on the inside of a door, and a woman checking that a door is locked and then going away from it.** It is the warmest thing that has ever happened to him. **No memory gone, no minute lost, nothing taken out of him at all — a nosebleed, about forty minutes of not hearing a room properly, and a mark in his palm from a split ring. That is the cost, and it is cheap.**

**He does not write it down, and the reason is new, and it is the first time in thirty-seven weeks he has got something right for the wrong reason: a book has one reader and a room has forty.** He says the name out loud once, to an empty room, to find out whether his mouth will do it. It does. He tries the moral floor four times into a fan and reaches the word *house* and stops, **because a house is a building, and he has a sentence about a building and not about a house, and the second half of it is about a person and a person who has not asked to be held is not a collection — and he has noticed that the sentence is at its worst when there is no witness, which is the exact opposite of what he would have expected, and he has written that down because he does not yet know what to do with it.**

**Friday morning, half past nine, a hall at the bottom of Ander's Row, about forty people, nineteen of them because a leaflet went through a door, six from the district register, a parish clerk who had come to be helpful, and four children eating biscuits whom nobody asked anything.** The Registry's **provisional schedule of persons provisionally recorded, district of Ferry Bank, event of 18–19 June 2025**, read as a working document and not a finding, because in about four years somebody is going to read it and think we made it up. An hour of names of people drowned in 1948 and 1953 and 1961 and 1977 and 1989, and the hall is respectful and slow and about eleven people are crying and nobody minding.

**At about twenty past eleven it comes to the June entries and the last one has no name in it, and the column is the only column on that schedule that could not be filled in from a record, and it has been empty for about six weeks.** Somebody at the back says *there's a woman went in.* And then a voice says, quietly, in the tone of a man reading a reference number off a docket: **I've got a name.**

⚠ **He says it. Marguerite Sowle. Both halves, in a room, in front of about forty people, in about four seconds — and he had about nine seconds in which to say the other thing and used them, after.** He tells it all: the address, the column, that she is at home, that he did not know it was her, and that he cannot undo it, at ten past four in the afternoon, before anybody asked, and nobody thanked him and that is the correct treatment. The duty officer writes it, reads it back, and a woman at the third row says *twenty-seven Ander's Row, that is Marguerite Sowle, she has been there forty years*, and a small noise goes round a room of forty people. **And the reason he believed it is not the warmth: there was nothing in the world he could have used to disbelieve it with, and the nothing is a line in about nine hundred claim files, and a sheet of ten in a drawer, and four words on a sheet of A4 with an empty name line. In a hall with a column that wanted a name, he was not choosing between two beliefs; he was choosing between a name and nothing.** The only check he ran was a telephone call to a branch manager of fifty-one at half past nine, who said no, and said she was sorry, and said there was no way of telling.

---

## Chapter 0129 — The Woman Carried Out

**Fri 3 – Sat 4 Sep 2025, and Friday the third is carried by this chapter and by the one before it, and the hinge is Friday.**

**At about half past twelve a duty officer of forty-seven with nineteen years in post telephones a house in Ander's Row and a woman of sixty-nine answers it, and she says three things.** She has not been carried out and has not been in a boat in her life and has been at number twenty-seven for forty-nine years. **And the second thing: a name that has been on a provisional schedule and then taken off it stays on it, because a schedule of persons provisionally recorded is a record of what people said, and the thing struck out is somebody's statement and not the office's certainty — so by Tuesday there will be a line with her name on it and a line through her name, and both readable by anybody who asks.** And the third: **a provisional entry with no name cannot be closed; an entry with a name can be sent forward; an entry sent forward is the only thing in this flood anybody is paid money for, and it goes to a policyholder at an address on a date, and that somebody will be the person at fourteen Ashgill Row, and somebody is going to write to that person this week asking for a date.** And then the fourth, which is the one she rang about: **on the night of the eighteenth of June she did open her door, at about half past twelve in the morning, to a woman she had never seen who had a torch in her teeth and a rope over her shoulder and was going down toward the water, and she told her there was a woman and a girl in the row, and the woman said *go on then* and went out into it, and she stood in her hall with the door open for about four hours and then shut it and locked it, and she has not been able to say anything about that to anybody since the nineteenth of June because there has been nobody to say it to. She is aware that she is sixty-nine and she is aware of what that sounds like at this precise moment and she does not care.**

**At about twenty past one the woman of about forty-three comes up to the table and puts her hand flat on it, which is a thing about four people in this case do and she was not taught it from anybody, and asks *is it a good thing to be on it*.** The duty officer begins *I am sorry, I did not catch who I was talking to*; the woman says it does not matter and says the address. **The duty officer says *I would like your name for the—* and she says no, pleasantly, and about forty people have heard the question and four of them were children, and nobody in that hall had heard a name being asked for in nine weeks in a public room.** The duty officer says the name on the schedule that morning was not hers and was struck through at about twenty past twelve and is not erased and will be legible. The woman asks who it was. She is told: a woman of sixty-nine at number twenty-seven in the same row. **She says: *No. No, that is not right, and I am telling you it is not right, and I am not going to say anything else about it, and I have got a reason and the reason is that I have not been asked anything since June and I am not starting on a Saturday afternoon in a hall.*** The duty officer says that is completely hers and puts her hand back in her pocket. **The woman says *right*, and stays for another half an hour, and does not tell anybody her name, and nobody asks her again.**

**Three o'clock, a registrar of twenty-eight finds out that a document in her own office is wrong and a person is in a hall, and decides in about eleven seconds — and the thing she decides is to use the only instrument this case has ever had, and to point it at the man.** She asks him whether she can put his name on a record, having done it once in July to a man of fifty-eight whom she described as *alive* and *in post* and *no wrongdoing identified*, and having said out loud that if she does not use it on him she is only saving it for somebody worse. He says yes in about nine seconds and gives the reason in about forty words: **because you are the only person in this city who has ever said that the whole of what this case has is a name against a date, and if it is only ever pointed at people who have not asked to be caught then it is a weapon and not an instrument.** She uses it verbatim and says she is not going to improve on it. ⚠ **The card, `RG/26/6`, the third in a private series and not a collection, names one person and he is the man who made the error. It records that the person at the premises has not been asked her name and has not given it, that she was in the room when the name was entered, that she was asked for it once and declined and the request was withdrawn immediately and without argument, that the name was not taken from a record, and that there is no instrument in this city by which it could have been checked, and he said so in the room before anybody asked.**

**Saturday at ten past ten the name is struck through and not erased.** And the woman of sixty-nine telephones at about half past eleven and asks whether *no action is required from you* is true, and is told that a card in her hand does not change whose record it is and that if she gives it back the office will not be able to prove she was told, and would rather have it in her hand. **She says: I asked and I was told no, and that is the first time in about forty-nine years anybody in this city has asked me for something and said no to me, and I have found that I am not as upset about it as I would have thought.** Nobody asked her about the door. **And on the Monday she rang the office herself and asked to be asked about the night of the eighteenth, and was told that a schedule of persons provisionally recorded is not a place where a witness is taken, and that if she wished she could put it in writing to the district office, and she said she would think about it, and she did not, and that is the whole of what happened to the woman whose name is on a list of the drowned in this city. She is alive and she is at home and she opened a door.**

---

## Chapter 0130 — A Correction, And A Woman Who Is Not A Piece Of Paper

**Mon 6 – Tue 7 Sep 2025, and it is early in its pinned week on purpose, because Chapter 131 is the next batch's and needs the rest of it.**

**Monday at about eleven, the correction, on the record, and it is the first document in this flood that says a person was somewhere else.** Struck through, not erased, with a date, with a duty officer's name, and a reason that says the name was supplied in error in a public room by a person who stated at the time that it had not been checked and that there was no instrument by which it could have been checked. **And the duty officer says she would not have written that reason if the man had not stood up on Friday and said all of it in front of forty people, and that this is not a defence of what happened, it is a fact about why the document reads the way it reads.** And then: **the other document is not on the schedule and it is not hers to make. The letter that went to the woman on the Friday afternoon is entirely correct, and it is a record of what the office told her, and it does not say one word about the door, and it is going to sit on a table in a house in Ander's Row for the rest of that woman's life. A witness statement is what would say one word about the door, and there is no form and no place to send it, and she tried it in her head about nine times since Friday and it does not go in.**

**Monday afternoon, six people in a room, and a decision not to ask a man of fifty-five.** **He says he wants it and has wanted it since the fifteenth of June, and that he has never in thirty-eight weeks been able to say *do not ask him* about anybody, and the reason he could not refuse this one the way he refused the others is that the others were knocks and this is the only instrument anybody in this case has found that turns a rope into heights.** And then the whole of it, said out loud, because a decision that is not said out loud in a room in this case becomes a thing in a drawer and there are six drawers open now and one of them is his: **his condition is *tell me what happens to a street if the number is wrong*, and he has not answered it, and the answer is a man of thirty-two telling a man of fifty-five what happens to a street, and the man of thirty-two has no standing to say what happens to a street in this city, and the man of fifty-five knew that, which is why he set it in those words.** And the reason he is not going to ask: **in about four weeks a recovery page goes out for a district of about nineteen hundred tenancies, and a paragraph in it will name nobody, and a list of addresses in a district under flood emergency powers is not a list but an instruction with no verbs in it, and a man of thirty-two broke that minute on the twenty-seventh of August with a printed sheet and put his own name at the bottom of it, and a number in September is a computable district, and a man who has not been asked will go on being the only person in this county who knew it first, and that is his to keep and not mine to move him out of.** Hazel Cray writes four words on a pad and turns it round. They are not the four words she wrote on 4 August.

**Tuesday at about half past four, a correct nine-line letter goes out of an office on the north side to a ground floor in Bankfoot asking for a date of loss.** **A claims handler of thirty-four wrote none of it, read all of it, and says in a room that it is the best letter that office has produced since December, and that it is going to cost that woman something, and that the office is right, and that the entry is completable because of a man with no post, and that in about four years somebody is going to read about this week and assume everything was a mistake. It was not a mistake.** And she will not stop it, and gives the reason: **if a registrar of twenty-eight telephones that office and says hold that letter, then in about four years there is a memorandum, and a memorandum is a thing a person is protected by, and a person protected by a memorandum is a person who is on a record, and she did not ask to be on a record.**

**And then the sentence the batch ends on, said to him and not to the table: she is not going to forgive him on Monday and is not going to not forgive him either, and the reason she is not going to not forgive him is that he asked her to put his name on a card and meant it, and a man who means it has bought the only thing anybody in this case has ever been able to sell anybody, and she has watched a woman of sixty-eight say that a minute is not a thing you do for the person who asked you, and she has been putting people on cards in this case since the fifth of July because she did not know what else to do with a person. It is not the same as forgiveness and it is not going to be, and neither of them has said the other word and she has noticed for the second time this week that neither of them says it.**

**A woman of sixty-one comes down a hill for the third time in six weeks, unannounced, and says one thing about a woman she has not met and whose name she has not got and is not going to ask anybody for: there is a woman in this flood who opened her door in the middle of the night for a stranger and stood in her hall with it open for four hours, and nobody has asked her about it, and she did not know the woman on the eighteenth of June and does not know her now and is not going to. And the reason: she telephoned a registrar on the eighth of December 2024 and walked four miles and a man of ninety-one got onto a page. And: *you have been in this flood since the first of June, thirteen weeks, and not one of you has gone to a door, and I am not saying that to make you feel it.*** **And it has been exactly six weeks since Monday the twenty-sixth of July and he did not ask her once in six weeks and she has noticed every week, and she says in advance that there is a question she has not asked and that when it is asked somebody in that room should know that she said it was not now. The list of four names was not asked for. The book of the interments was not asked for. The registrar of twenty-eight was not asked about the parish registrar again, and Hesta's condition is that she is to be asked in September and not before, and September has not arrived when this chapter closes.** The last line is about nineteen things that nobody keeps, and a first of October that is twenty-four days away and was chosen by two people who have never said out loud in a room what happens on it, and that neither of them is in this case, and that he has now noticed seven times and asked for nothing seven times, and that the first person in this case who asks is the one who has to answer.

---

## THE ONE ERROR THIS REPAIR PASS MADE ITSELF

⚠ **Chapter 123's *a Friday night*, about a telephone minute taken on 6 August 2025, was changed to *a Wednesday night* by a check reading the real calendar. 6 August 2025 is a real Wednesday and a pinned Friday, and the original was correct. It has been put back. This is the ninth recurrence of the real-weekday-on-a-real-date failure and the first one committed by a repair pass on a line that was already right, and it is written down here because a class of error that appears in the corrector as well as in the writer is a class that belongs in a checklist rather than in a memory.**
